**The comment id is the SERVICE’s own.** A caller that named its own comment id could overwrite another account’s comment, and a comment is a thing that happened once. The instant is the service’s own too (the injected `Clock`, truncated to milliseconds like the publication’s) — a client that sent its own instant would be asking the service to believe a number it never checked.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/CommentEntry.java` (the entity, with the soft-delete flag), `.../social/CommentRepository.java` (the finders, every windowed one carrying a `Pageable`), `.../social/CommentService.java` (the four rules), `.../social/CommentController.java` (`POST /comments`, `DELETE /comments/{commentId}?claimer=`, `GET /comments?projectId=`), `.../social/CommentDtos.java` (the wire shapes), `.../social/InvalidComment.java`, `.../social/NotYourComment.java` (the 403 that is deliberately not a 400), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/CommentServiceTest.java` (17 cases).
Measured: `services/nexus-api` **the social package 31/31** (17 comment cases + 14 like cases), `mvn -o -B test -Dtest="dev.nexus.api.social.*Test"` **exit 0**, and the module COMPILES.
**Bounds (§60).** The service does NOT check that the publication exists, and it does NOT verify the account — the same posture the publish service takes (it records a claim and verifies nothing — ADR 0018). The body is stored as it arrived, so the ESCAPING is the caller’s responsibility and the page’s is to escape it again on the way out; the service does not pretend to be a sanitizer. There is no comment EDIT — a comment is appended or taken back, never rewritten, because a rewritten comment is a different comment and the reader cannot tell them apart. No moderation, no rate limit, and no profanity filter: a service that filtered words would be making editorial decisions with no policy behind them. The account is a CLAIM: the assurance travels with every comment so a reader is told what it is worth, and nothing here makes it worth more.
## v286 — the COMMENTS surface: the page lists them, the Studio writes them (M7/v286 — ADR 0016 amendment 286)

**The shape, and why it is split the way the like was.** The artifact page is SERVER-RENDERED and cannot act: it does not know who the device is (ADR 0018 is why there is no session). So the page LISTS the comments — read through the same server-side hop the publication, the history and the like count came through — and offers a link to `/?comment=<id>`, where the Studio opens a composer over it. The comment is written in the Studio, because the Studio is where the account lives.
**The body is ESCAPED AGAIN on the way out.** The service stored it verbatim (the client escaped it on the way in, per the community model), and a page that trusted the stored bytes would render whatever was in them. Escaping twice is not a bug: the first escape is the caller’s promise, and the second is the page’s own.
**A REMOVED comment is shown as removed — not hidden, and not as its text.** “This comment was removed” and “this comment never existed” are different facts, and a reader who can see the first is better served than a reader who cannot tell it from the second. The section states the VISIBLE count beside the rows, so a list of three of which one was removed cannot read as “there are three comments”.
**The query is NOT dropped, unlike the remix’s.** A remix is a one-time intent — a URL that kept its query would remix again on every reload, and a remix nobody asked for twice is a bug. A comment is not a one-time intent: the composer is a place the reader is, and a reader who reloads should still find it open over the publication it is for. The difference is not a detail; it is the difference between an intent and a destination.
**The composer never INVENTS an author.** A device with no account has no claim, and the composer says so by name (“this device has no account, and a comment needs one”) rather than posting a comment nobody made. The service refuses an authorless comment BY NAME (v285), and the client refuses to pretend otherwise.
**Four modules, in the shape every lane here has.** `commentServer.ts` is the server-side half (the read for the page and the write for the route); `commentLane.ts` is the browser half; `commentBar.ts` is the PURE renderer (the row, the section, the escaping); `CommentComposer.tsx` is the one piece of UI. The Studio’s `/api/comments` route forwards to the service’s `/comments`.
**What the real-browser proof grades.** §11: the artifact page carries a COMMENTS section and says the thread is empty (and why it is not a one-click thing); it offers the composer as a link to the Studio; the Studio opens a composer over the intent; posting says so in the Studio’s own words; the SERVICE recorded the comment (read independently of the browser, from the real database), naming the ACCOUNT with its assurance and the body as it was typed; the artifact page now lists the comment with its author; a device with NO account is refused and the refused comment changed NOTHING.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/commentServer.ts` (the server-side read and write), `apps/studio/src/lib/commentLane.ts` (the browser half), `apps/studio/src/lib/commentBar.ts` + `commentBar.test.ts` (the pure renderer, 15 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/commentIntent.ts` (the URL intent), `apps/studio/src/app/api/comments/route.ts` (the Studio’s door), `apps/studio/src/components/CommentComposer.tsx` (the one piece of UI), `apps/studio/src/components/Studio.tsx` (the intent), `apps/studio/src/app/p/[projectId]/route.ts` (the page reads the comments and injects the section), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §11).
**Two things the proof itself got wrong, found by the proof failing.** (1) The harness’s `type` helper cleared a field with the **input**’s `value` setter and then called it on a **textarea** — which throws “Illegal invocation” and killed the run. The setter now comes from the element’s OWN prototype. (2) The `until` helper returns **null** when it gives up, and several checks — including ones written in earlier increments — compared the result against `false`, so a TIMEOUT **PASSED** the check. Every such check now compares against `null`. A proof that cannot fail is not a proof, and both of these were a proof that could not fail.
Measured: `apps/studio` **998/998 (54 files)**, `tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**. The real-browser E2E **87/87** with the new §11 (the whole comment loop: the section, the composer, the row read independently from the service, the accountless refusal).
**Bounds (§60).** The page shows the thread and the visible count; it cannot say whether YOU commented, for the same reason it cannot say whether you liked. A comment is written in the Studio, so it costs a trip there — the price of not having a session. The body is escaped by the client on the way in and by the page on the way out; the SERVICE stores it verbatim and is not a sanitizer. There is no comment EDIT, no moderation, and no rate limit. The comment id and the instant are the service’s own. The account is a CLAIM (ADR 0018): the assurance travels with every comment so a reader is told what it is worth, and nothing here makes it worth more.
## v287 — FOLLOWS: the one social act that is about a PERSON (M7/v287 — ADR 0016 amendment 287)

**The milestone item.** The ROADMAP’s M7 row has carried **follow** beside likes and comments since the community surface was designed. This is the third of the three, and it is the first one whose subject is an ACCOUNT rather than a publication — which is exactly where its rules come from: the two ends of the edge are different things.
**The two ends are DIFFERENT COLUMNS, and the model says so.** `followerId` and `followeeId` are separate columns, and the repository’s finders for the two directions are separate too. A model that stored “a and b” would have to guess which end was which, and a social graph with ambiguous edges is a graph nobody can count. The UNIQUE constraint on (follower, followee) is what makes “the same account cannot follow the same account twice” a fact the DATABASE enforces.
**Rule 1: a follow needs an ACCOUNT.** The follower is the account the claim names; a follow with no follower has no edge to draw. The service refuses it BY NAME.
**Rule 2: an account cannot follow ITSELF.** A self-follow is not a relation — it is a no-op dressed as one — and it would put one account in its own follower count. The service refuses it by name, and the refusal says what the rule is for (“that is not a relation, it is a no-op”).
**Rule 3: following twice is following once.** The second follow finds the edge the first one wrote and answers with it; the counts do not move. And the counts are ASKED FOR, never incremented, so a lost update cannot inflate either — the same discipline the like uses.
**Rule 4: only the follower takes the edge back**, and somebody else’s un-follow is REFUSED rather than silently ignored: a caller that asked to remove something and was told “fine” would believe the edge is gone.
**What the service CANNOT know, stated rather than implied: whether either account EXISTS.** The service holds no account table (an account exists in it only as far as publications NAME it), so a follow of an account id nobody has ever published is a follow the service records faithfully — and the PROFILE page, which is the place where a reader would learn the truth, is the place that can say it. The service does not verify the account either (ADR 0018).
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/FollowEntry.java` (the edge and its unique constraint), `.../social/FollowRepository.java` (the finders, one per direction), `.../social/FollowService.java` (the four rules), `.../social/FollowController.java` (`POST /follows`, `DELETE /follows/{accountId}?claimer=`, `GET /follows?accountId=`), `.../social/FollowDtos.java` (the wire shapes), `.../social/InvalidFollow.java`, `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/FollowServiceTest.java` (12 cases).
Measured: `services/nexus-api` **the social package 43/43** (12 follow + 17 comment + 14 like), `mvn -o -B test -Dtest="dev.nexus.api.social.*Test"` **exit 0**, and the module COMPILES.
**Bounds (§60).** The service does NOT check that either account exists, and it does NOT verify the account — the same posture the publish service takes (it records a claim and verifies nothing — ADR 0018). A follow of an account id nobody has ever published is recorded faithfully, and the profile page is where that would be visible. There is no MUTUAL follow, no follow request to approve, and no unfollow-everything: a follow is a one-way edge and the service does not invent rules it has no policy for. The follower and followee are account ids — CLAIMS, not verified identities — and the assurance travels with the claim so a reader is told what it is worth.
## v288 — the FOLLOW surface: the profile shows the followers, the Studio does the follow (M7/v288 — ADR 0016 amendment 288)

**The third social action gets its surface, and M7’s three are now all reachable.** The profile page is where a reader goes to see an account, so the followers belong THERE: the page states the count and offers the follow as a link to `/?follow=<accountId>`, where the Studio follows on mount — the same arrangement the like and the comment use, for the same reason (the page is server-rendered and does not know who the device is; ADR 0018 is why there is no session).
**A failed read is NOT zero followers.** The bar is left out ENTIRELY when the count could not be read, because “0 followers” and “the followers could not be read” are different facts, and a page that showed the first when it knew the second would make a reader conclude nobody is watching. A count of ZERO, on the other hand, IS a fact.
**The page does not claim YOU follow them.** It cannot know — the account lives in the browser — so it offers the follow and lets the Studio, where the account lives, answer the question that only the Studio can.
**ONE claim builder for all three social verbs.** The like, the comment and the follow each need a claim — the account id, the handle, the assurance — and a claim that three modules each build slightly differently is a claim whose three shapes disagree about the same account. The builder moved to `socialClaim.ts` and the like intent now points at it. The device with NO account still has no claim, and the client still does not invent an actor.
**The proof needs TWO accounts, and that is the shape of the feature.** The service refuses a self-follow by name (a self-follow is a no-op dressed as a relation, v287), so the proof signs the device in as a SECOND account before following the one that published. The page’s bar, the Studio’s intent, the service’s row, the profile’s count and the accountless refusal are all graded.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/followServer.ts` (the server-side read and the two writes), `apps/studio/src/lib/followLane.ts` (the browser half, with the 10s timeout), `apps/studio/src/lib/followBar.ts` + `followBar.test.ts` (the pure bar, 16 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/followIntent.ts` (the URL intent), `apps/studio/src/lib/socialClaim.ts` (the ONE claim builder), `apps/studio/src/lib/likeIntent.ts` (points at the shared builder), `apps/studio/src/app/api/follows/route.ts` (the Studio’s door), `apps/studio/src/app/u/[accountId]/route.ts` (the profile reads the count and injects the bar), `apps/studio/src/components/Studio.tsx` (the mount intent), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §12).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1015/1015 (55 files)**, `tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**. The real-browser E2E **98/98** with the new §12 (the whole follow loop, with a SECOND account behind it: the bar, the intent, the row read independently from the service, the accountless refusal).
**Bounds (§60).** The page shows the count and nothing about the viewer. The follow happens in the Studio, so it costs a trip there — the price of not having a session. The service does NOT check that the account exists (it holds no account table), and it does NOT verify the account: the assurance travels with the claim so a reader is told what it is worth, and nothing here makes it worth more. There is no follower LIST on the page — only the count — and no mutual follow, no follow request to approve, and no way to list the accounts an account follows.
## v290 — the SEARCH door: the client’s `?q=` finally means something (M7/v290 — ADR 0016 amendment 290)

**The gap.** The explore and profile pages have carried a search box since v278 and v279, and the client has been sending `?q=` to a service that **ignored it** — the gallery showed the same unfiltered listing whatever you typed. A search box that does not search is worse than no search box: it tells a reader their words were understood.
**A NEW package, and why it is its own.** The publish package’s own comment said why it had no finder: "a search is a different feature with different rules (ranking, paging, what it does with a blank query)". So the search is its own door (`GET /search`), its own service, its own window and its own answer shapes — and the package is **self-contained on purpose**: it defines its own DTOs and its own claim record rather than reusing the publish package’s, because those DTOs are in flight in a concurrent lane and a search that would break when a record grew a field is a search that would break the build. The two doors answer the SAME shape, so the client parses them with the same code.
**The search reaches into the DOCUMENT, not only the title.** A word that names a node in the work is a word the reader typed, and a search that only matched titles would answer a different question than the one that was asked. The cost is stated rather than hidden: a `LIKE` over a text column cannot use an index, so this is a **SEQUENTIAL SCAN** — at this table’s size that is the price of searching what is actually IN the work, and a table big enough for that to matter wants an index (or a full-text one).
**A BLANK term is the plain listing, not an error.** “Show me everything” is a legitimate thing to mean, and a 400 would make the caller guess which word the service wanted. And a blank OWNER is the ABSENCE of one — the same rule the profile’s listing follows, and for the same reason: “the publications of nobody” is not a filter.
**The window is EXACT, and the cap is the listing’s.** The search is the same listing with a filter, and a filter that changed the rules would make the two disagree — so the cap is CLAMPED the same way and the window skips an exact number of rows. The window is a `SearchWindow` of the search package’s own, and the record says plainly that it duplicates a window the publish package carries in a concurrent lane: two copies of one idea is a wart, and the search could not wait for a file another lane has not finished, and a search that silently paged by whole pages would show a reader results that do not exist.
**What the real-browser proof grades.** §14: a term that appears in NO title — the source’s own id, which the forks carry in their lineage — finds exactly the forks (a title-only search would find nothing); the publication that does NOT mention the term is EXCLUDED (the filter really filters); and a term that matches nothing gives an EMPTY gallery rather than the whole one.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/search/SearchService.java`, `.../search/SearchController.java`, `.../search/SearchDtos.java`, `.../search/SearchWindow.java` (new), `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/publish/PublishedProjectRepository.java` (the two finders), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/search/SearchServiceTest.java` (8 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/publishServer.ts` (a query goes to `/search`, a plain listing to `/projects`), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §14).
Measured: `services/nexus-api` **the search package 8/8**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, `apps/studio` **1019/1019 (55 files)**, doc gate **exit 0**. The real-browser E2E **105/105** with the new §14 (a term that is only in the DOCUMENT finds the forks, the publication that does not mention it is EXCLUDED, and a term that matches nothing gives an EMPTY gallery).
**Bounds (§60).** The search is a SUBSTRING match, case-insensitive, over the title and the document text — not a word match, not a phrase match, not a ranking: the results are newest-first like the listing, because a relevance ranking would need rules this feature does not have yet. It is a SEQUENTIAL SCAN, stated above. The search does not verify the owner and does not check that the publication exists (the same posture the publish service takes — ADR 0018). There is no fuzzy matching, no stemming, no stop-word list and no spell correction: a search that guessed would be a search that answered a different question than the one that was asked.
## v292 — the follower LIST: a count with names beside it, each one a link (M7/v292 — ADR 0016 amendment 292)

**The gap.** The profile page showed a follower COUNT and nothing else — a number with no faces on it, and the service’s finder for the list was already there. A count says how many; a list says who.
**The edge now carries the follower’s HANDLE and assurance, because an id is an address and a reader wants the name.** The follow edge stored only the two account ids, so a follower list would have been a column of ids. The claim that made the follow carried a handle and an assurance, so the edge stores them beside the id — the same rule the like and the comment follow (the assurance travels with the actor, so a reader is TOLD what it is worth).
**The followers are LINKS to their own profiles.** An account id is an address, and a list of addresses with no links is a list nobody can act on: the reader would have to copy an id into the URL bar by hand. Each follower is now a link to `/u/<followerId>`, labelled with the name the claim carried.
**A follower with no id is DROPPED, not rendered as a nameless line.** A follower needs an address — that is what the link points at — and a name; a row with neither would be a line of nothing.
**What the real-browser proof grades.** §12: the profile page LISTS the follower, by the name the claim carried, with a link to their own profile — the same section that already proved the count moved.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/FollowEntry.java` (the follower’s handle and assurance), `.../social/FollowService.java` (the edge carries them), `.../social/FollowDtos.java` and `.../social/FollowController.java` (the wire shape), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/FollowServiceTest.java` (the new constructor), `apps/studio/src/lib/followServer.ts` (the reader returns the list), `apps/studio/src/lib/followBar.ts` + `followBar.test.ts` (the list, the links, the dropping, +6 cases), `apps/studio/src/app/u/[accountId]/route.ts` (the page renders the list), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (the §12 check).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1028/1028 (55 files)**, `tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, the service’s social package **48/48**, doc gate **exit 0**; the real-browser E2E **110/110**, with the follower list graded in §12 (the follower, by name, as a link to their own profile).
**Bounds (§60).** The follower list is CAPPED by the service’s window (100 at the most), so an account with more followers than that shows the newest ones — and the bar says how many there are in all, so the two can be compared. A follower is an ACCOUNT ID and the name the claim carried: the service holds no account table, so the name is whatever the claim said, and a follower who has never published appears by their id alone. The list does not say who a follower’s followers are, and there is no “followers of followers” and no suggestion — the service does not invent rules it has no policy for.
## v294 — the type checker found what no proof had: the un-verbs were routed to nowhere (M7/v294 — ADR 0016 amendment 294)

**The bug, and who found it.** The Studio’s un-like and un-follow call `/api/likes/<id>` and `/api/follows/<id>` — but both routes were FLAT (`/api/likes/route.ts`), with a DELETE handler that read `params.projectId` / `params.accountId` from a route that had no dynamic segment. The URLs the client called matched nothing, and the un-verbs would have answered **404**. The reads and the writes worked, which is why every proof passed: **nothing in six sections ever un-liked or un-followed.**
**What found it was the TYPE CHECKER, not a proof.** Next’s typed-routes check reads the generated route types and asserts that each route module satisfies the config for its path — and a handler that reads a path parameter from a route that is not one does not satisfy it. The check had been RED in the studio’s `tsc` since the routes were added, and the red was being cleared by DELETING the generated types — which is the worst possible response to a red check: it made the type checker stop looking. (The fix for the clearing: `next typegen` regenerates the types instead of removing them, and the studio’s `tsc` is now red-free with the types PRESENT — which is the only way the check is worth having.)
**The fix is the STRUCTURE, not a workaround.** The parent routes keep GET and POST; the DELETE handlers move into `api/likes/[projectId]/route.ts` and `api/follows/[accountId]/route.ts`. A route that reads a path parameter has to BE a path parameter.
**And now a proof covers it.** §15 of the real-browser harness un-does both, for real: the device that liked the publication takes the like back (the Studio’s `?unlike=` intent), the service’s count goes to ZERO, the SECOND account takes the follow back (the `?unfollow=` intent), and the service’s follower count goes to zero. The section exists because the bug was found by a type checker and NO PROOF had exercised the path — a proof that only ever does the happy path is a proof that cannot find this.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/app/api/likes/[projectId]/route.ts` and `apps/studio/src/app/api/follows/[accountId]/route.ts` (new — the DELETE doors), `apps/studio/src/app/api/likes/route.ts` and `.../api/follows/route.ts` (the DELETE handlers removed), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §15).
Measured: `npx next typegen` (the generated types are regenerated, not deleted) and `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0** with the types present; the real-browser E2E **110/110**, with §15 grading the un-verbs (the un-like takes the like back and the service’s count goes to zero; the un-follow takes the edge back and the follower count goes to zero).
**Bounds (§60).** The un-like and the un-follow are now REACHABLE, and that is a new surface rather than a fixed one: the service has always refused a stranger’s un-like and a stranger’s un-follow (v283, v287), and the Studio’s intents build the claim from the device’s own account — so the un-verbs can only ever remove the viewer’s OWN. The trails record the un-verbs (v289), so a taken-back like is a fact the trail keeps.
## v295 — REPORTS: the M8 social act that asks for help (M7/v295 — ADR 0016 amendment 295)

**The milestone item.** The ROADMAP’s M8 row has carried **reports** beside subscriptions and tips since the creator economy was designed. The parallel lane’s `ops.ts` already declares the client’s own `Report` shape (`id`, `reporterId`, `targetId`, `reason`, `detail`, `status`, `createdAt`), and the service’s wire shape is that shape field for field, plus the reporter’s handle and assurance.
**Rule 1: a report needs an ACCOUNT**, refused BY NAME with the reason that matters — “a report with no reporter cannot be followed up”. A report nobody can ask about is a report nobody can act on.
**Rule 2: the reason is one of FOUR, and the refusal NAMES them** — `spam`, `harassment`, `illegal`, `other`. A closed set means a reader can COUNT by reason, and a caller that is told only “no” has to guess. The four are the client’s own, so the service and the client agree on the set rather than each having one.
**Rule 3: the detail is bounded, and an EMPTY detail is allowed.** The reason alone is a report; the detail is the reporter’s words, and words have a length. The cap is named in the refusal.
**Rule 4: the same account cannot report the same publication for the same reason twice.** The UNIQUE constraint on (reporter, target, reason) is the DATABASE’s rule, and the service asks the repository first so the refusal can be a NAMED refusal rather than a constraint violation. A DIFFERENT reason is not a duplicate — a report that says “spam” and a report that says “illegal” are two different facts about the same publication.
**The withdrawal, and the statuses this build can and cannot reach.** The only move this build supports is the REPORTER withdrawing their own OPEN report, which answers `rejected` — and the service says so plainly. The other two statuses (`reviewed`, `resolved`) belong to a **moderator, and there is no moderator in this build**: the service does not invent a role it has no policy for. The row STAYS when it is withdrawn, because a report that was withdrawn is a fact that happened — the same rule the publication’s take-down and the trail’s entries keep.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/ReportEntry.java` (the entity and its unique constraint), `.../social/ReportRepository.java`, `.../social/ReportService.java` (the four rules), `.../social/ReportController.java` (`POST /reports`, `DELETE /reports/{reportId}?claimer=`, `GET /reports?target=`), `.../social/ReportDtos.java` (the wire shapes), `.../social/InvalidReport.java`, `.../social/NotYourReport.java` (the 403 that is deliberately not a 400), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/ReportServiceTest.java` (16 cases).
Measured: the service’s social package **64/64** (16 report + 19 comment + 17 like + 12 follow), `mvn -o -B test -Dtest="dev.nexus.api.social.*Test"` **exit 0**, and the module COMPILES.
**Bounds (§60).** The service does NOT check that the publication exists, and it does NOT verify the account (the same posture the publish service takes — ADR 0018). The detail is stored AS IT ARRIVED: the client escapes it and the page escapes it again, and the service is not a sanitizer. There is no moderation QUEUE, no priority, no assignment and no notification — a moderation workflow needs rules this build does not have. The account is a CLAIM: the assurance travels with every report so a reader is told what it is worth, and nothing here makes it worth more.
## v296 — the REPORTS surface: the page shows the count, the Studio files the report (M7/v296 — ADR 0016 amendment 296)

**The shape of the feature, and why the page cannot file one.** The artifact page is SERVER-RENDERED and does not know who the device is (ADR 0018 is why there is no session), so it shows the COUNT and links to `/?report=<id>`, where the Studio opens a composer — the same arrangement the like, the comment and the follow use. The composer asks for a REASON from a closed set of four (the client’s own) and takes an optional detail.
**The bar does NOT name the REPORTER, and that is the load-bearing decision.** The service records the reporter’s account id and the name the claim carried, and a page that printed them would turn a report into a PUBLIC ACCUSATION with no moderation behind it — the count is the fact, and the identity is a moderator’s business. The page shows the reasons and the count; the reporter’s name is nowhere on it.
**A failed read is NOT zero reports.** The bar is left out ENTIRELY when the count could not be read, because “0 reports” and “the reports could not be read” are different facts — and a reader misled into thinking nobody complained is the ONE reader a report page must not mislead.
**A withdrawn report is shown as withdrawn**, not hidden and not shown as its reason alone: “spam (withdrawn)” says what happened and that the reporter no longer stands by it.
**What the real-browser proof grades.** §16: the artifact page carries a REPORTS bar with a count of zero and offers the report as a link to the Studio; the Studio opens a composer over the intent; posting says so in the Studio’s own words WITH the reason; the SERVICE recorded the report (read independently of the browser, from the real database) naming the reason, the body and the ACCOUNT that filed it; the artifact page now says ONE report and does NOT name the reporter; a device with NO account is refused by name and the refused report changed NOTHING.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/reportServer.ts` (the server-side read and write), `apps/studio/src/lib/reportLane.ts` (the browser half, with the 45s write timeout v294 introduced), `apps/studio/src/lib/reportBar.ts` + `reportBar.test.ts` (the pure bar, 16 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/reportIntent.ts` (the URL intent), `apps/studio/src/app/api/reports/route.ts` (the Studio’s door), `apps/studio/src/components/ReportComposer.tsx` (the composer), `apps/studio/src/components/Studio.tsx` (the intent), `apps/studio/src/app/p/[projectId]/route.ts` (the page reads the count and injects the bar), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §16).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1050/1051 (57 files)** — this increment’s own **16** cases pass; the one failure is a concurrent lane’s in-flight `probe293`, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, the service’s social package **64/64**, doc gate **exit 0**; the real-browser E2E **121/121** with the new §16.
**Bounds (§60).** The composer is a Studio overlay, so filing a report costs a trip to the Studio — the price of not having a session. The page shows the COUNT and the reasons, never the reporter. There is no moderation queue, no assignment, no notification and no way for the reporter to change the reason after filing (a report is appended, and a second report for the same reason is refused by the service) — a moderation workflow needs rules this build does not have. The detail is stored as it arrived: the client escapes it and the page escapes it again.
## v297 — TIPS: the first social act that carries an AMOUNT (M7/v297 — ADR 0016 amendment 297)

**The milestone item.** The ROADMAP’s M8 row has carried **tips** beside subscriptions since the creator economy was designed, and the parallel lane’s `monetization.ts` already declares the client’s own `Tip` shape (`recipientId`, `amountCents`, `message`, `anonymous`). The service’s wire shape is that shape field for field, plus the payer’s account, handle, assurance, the currency, the id and the instant.
**Rule 1: a tip needs a RECIPIENT and a PAYER.** The recipient is who the tip is for; the payer is the account that gave it. Both are the claim’s account. A tip with neither is not a fact anyone can act on.
**Rule 2: the amount is POSITIVE and CAPPED, and each refusal says WHICH.** A tip of **zero** is not a tip. A **negative** amount is money moving the wrong way round, and the service refuses it by name rather than storing a number that reads like a payment in reverse. The **cap** (a million yuan) is named in the refusal: a tip that large is a typo or an attack, and this build has no payment rail that would make it meaningful.
**Rule 3: the MESSAGE is bounded** and may be empty — an amount with no note is still a tip.
**Rule 4: the amount is recorded AS IT WAS.** A price that changes later does not rewrite what was given, because “they gave 500 cents” is a fact about the past and a fact about the past does not move. The tip is APPENDED and never edited.
**The total is summed by the DATABASE**, not by the service: a service that counted in memory would drift the moment two tips arrived together, and a total that drifts is a total nobody can defend.
**What the service does NOT do: move money.** This service holds no payment token and moves no funds (ADR 0018). A tip here is a RECORD of a gesture — “this account gave that amount to that account” — and never a statement that a payment happened. The service verifies nothing: the accounts are claims, and the assurance travels with every tip so a reader is told what it is worth.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/TipEntry.java` (the entity), `.../social/TipRepository.java` (the finders and the DATABASE-side sum), `.../social/TipService.java` (the four rules), `.../social/TipController.java` (`POST /tips`, `GET /tips?recipient=`), `.../social/TipDtos.java` (the wire shapes), `.../social/InvalidTip.java`, `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/TipServiceTest.java` (12 cases).
Measured: the service’s social package **76/76** (12 tip + 16 report + 19 comment + 17 like + 12 follow), `mvn -o -B test -Dtest="dev.nexus.api.social.*Test"` **exit 0**, and the module COMPILES.
**Bounds (§60).** The currency is **CNY** in this build — and the client says the same — so the amount is never a bare number. There is no currency conversion, no refund, no payout, and no way to see what a recipient has been paid out: this build records gestures, it does not move money. The tip is not tied to a publication (the client’s `Tip` names a RECIPIENT, not a work) — and saying so is the point, because tying a tip to a work would be a claim the service cannot check. A spammer can tip many times: the rules here are about honesty, not about moderation, and moderation is the same missing piece the reports have.
## v298 — the TIPS surface: a tip is for an ACCOUNT, so the bar is on the profile (M7/v298 — ADR 0016 amendment 298)

**Where the bar belongs, and why.** A tip is for an ACCOUNT, not for a work — the client’s own `Tip` names a `recipientId`, not a publication — so the bar belongs on the **PROFILE**, the page a reader lands on when they want to know about a person. The explore page and the artifact page carry the things that happen to works; the profile carries the things that happen to people.
**The page shows the NUMBERS and the MESSAGES, and never the PAYER.** The service records who gave what, and a page that printed the payer’s name would turn a small gesture into a public list of who supports whom. The count and the total are the facts; the identities are the reader’s business.
**A failed read is NOT zero tips.** The bar is left out ENTIRELY when the count could not be read, because “no tips” and “the tips could not be read” are different facts — and a creator’s page must not say the first when it knows the second.
**The composer asks for YUAN and sends CENTS.** The currency is CNY in this build (the client says the same), and the composer converts before it sends: a decimal amount is a thing a client and a service can disagree about, and the service’s unit is cents.
**What the real-browser proof grades.** §17: the profile page carries a TIPS bar with a count of zero and offers the tip as a link to the Studio; the Studio opens a composer over the intent; posting says so in the Studio’s own words with the amount; the SERVICE recorded the tip (read independently of the browser, from the real database) naming the amount, the message and the account that gave it; the TOTAL is what the DATABASE summed; the profile page now says ONE tip and carries ¥5.00; and a device with NO account is refused by name, with the refused tip changing NOTHING.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/tipServer.ts` (the server-side read and write), `apps/studio/src/lib/tipLane.ts` (the browser half, with the 45s write timeout), `apps/studio/src/lib/tipBar.ts` + `tipBar.test.ts` (the pure bar, 13 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/tipIntent.ts` (the URL intent), `apps/studio/src/app/api/tips/route.ts` (the Studio’s door), `apps/studio/src/components/TipComposer.tsx` (the composer), `apps/studio/src/components/Studio.tsx` (the intent), `apps/studio/src/app/u/[accountId]/route.ts` (the profile reads the tips and injects the bar), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §17, and the harness’s own fixes).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1063/1064 (58 files)** — this increment’s own **13** cases pass; the one failure is a concurrent lane’s in-flight `probe293`; `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**; the real-browser E2E **133/133** with the new §17.
**The harness learned three more things, and each is a fix in the shipped file.** (1) The servers’ logs were declared INSIDE the harness’s `try`, so the failure path — which lives after it — could not see them: the feature that prints the servers’ own output printed nothing, and the run said “the tip did not land” without saying why. (2) The harness’s checks that waited for a page’s own text now print the PAGE’s text when the wait gives up — a lane that refused says why, and the reason is the fact worth printing. (3) The tipper signs in FIRST, because the previous section ends by clearing the account — a tip with no payer is refused by name, so a tipper with no account would have been testing the refusal instead of the tip.
**Bounds (§60).** The composer is a Studio overlay, so giving a tip costs a trip to the Studio. The amount is CNY and there is no conversion. The total is the DATABASE’s sum over the WHOLE history (not just the window the page read), so the number on the page and the number in the answer can differ when the window is shorter than the history — and the page shows the count it read beside the total the service knows. A tip is a RECORD of a gesture and never a payment (ADR 0018), and the page says the count and the total without ever saying that money moved.
## v300 — PLANS and SUBSCRIPTIONS: the price is a SNAPSHOT, because what was agreed is a fact about the past (M7/v300 — ADR 0016 amendment 300)

**The milestone item, and the product decision it needed.** M8 has carried **subscriptions** since the creator economy was designed, and the plan said the blocker was a product decision: who sets the price and the period. The answer that keeps the service honest is to let the CREATOR own the plan — a plan is a creator’s own claim about their own work (its id, its price, its currency, its period) — and to let the SUBSCRIBER name only the plan. That way the price is the creator’s, never the subscriber’s, and a subscriber cannot record a subscription at one cent.
**The price is a SNAPSHOT of the plan’s price at the moment of subscribing.** The subscription records the price, the currency and the period AS THEY WERE, and a later change to the plan does NOT rewrite them: what a supporter agreed to is a fact about the past, and a fact about the past does not move. A subscription that read the plan’s CURRENT price would change its own history every time a creator changed their prices.
**Six rules, and the refusals name themselves.** (1) A plan needs a CREATOR, a POSITIVE price (a price of zero is not a price, it is the absence of one) and a period from a closed set of three (`once`, `month`, `year`) — and the cap is named. (2) A subscription needs a SUBSCRIBER and a plan that exists and is still OFFERED. (3) An account cannot subscribe to its OWN plan — a self-subscription is a no-op dressed as a relation, the same reason a self-follow is refused. (4) One subscription per supporter per plan, with the UNIQUE constraint as the database’s rule and the service asking first so the refusal can be NAMED. (5) The price is the snapshot. (6) Only the subscriber can end their own subscription.
**The expiry is computed ONCE and then stands.** A month’s expiry is the subscribed instant plus thirty days; nothing renews it, because there is no billing in this build (ADR 0018). The service records COMMITMENTS, not payments — and the bound is stated rather than implied by a field that looks like it is ticking.
**A plan is an ADDRESS, and a second one with the same id is refused by name** — two plans with one id would make the address ambiguous, and an ambiguous address is one that cannot be subscribed to.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/PlanEntry.java` (the creator’s claim), `.../social/SubscriptionEntry.java` (the subscription and its snapshot), `.../social/PlanRepository.java`, `.../social/SubscriptionRepository.java`, `.../social/SubscriptionService.java` (the six rules), `.../social/SubscriptionController.java` (`POST /plans`, `GET /plans?creator=`, `POST /subscriptions`, `GET /subscriptions?subscriber=`, `DELETE /subscriptions/{id}?claimer=`), `.../social/SubscriptionDtos.java` (the wire shapes and the period set), `.../social/InvalidSubscription.java`, `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/SubscriptionServiceTest.java` (20 cases).
Measured: the service’s social package **96/96** (20 subscription + 12 tip + 16 report + 19 comment + 17 like + 12 follow), `mvn -o -B test -Dtest="dev.nexus.api.social.*Test"` **exit 0**, and the module COMPILES.
**Bounds (§60).** The service moves no money and renews nothing: a monthly subscription’s expiry is computed once and then stands, and nothing charges the supporter again. The price is the creator’s CLAIM and the assurance travels with the plan, so a reader is told what the price is worth (ADR 0018). The period is a closed set of three — a plan that needs a fortnight is a plan this build has no rule for. There is no plan catalogue page, no way to edit a plan’s price, and no way to retire one but create a new plan: a plan that was sold for 1500 is still sold for 1500, in the subscriptions that say so.
## v301 — the PLANS and SUBSCRIPTIONS surface: the creator offers from their own account face, the supporter backs from the profile (M7/v301 — ADR 0016 amendment 301)

**The two surfaces, and why they sit where they do.** The CREATOR offers a plan from their **account panel** — because a plan is a creator’s own claim about their own work, and the account is where a creator’s claims are made. The SUPPORTER backs a plan from the **profile page**, whose plans bar lists what the creator offers, at what price, for what period, and links to the Studio.
**The price is typed in YUAN and sent in CENTS.** A decimal amount is a thing a client and a service can disagree about, and the service’s unit is cents — so the composer converts once, at the edge, and the service never has to guess what ¥15.00 means.
**The plans bar does NOT show what a plan has EARNED.** The count of subscriptions is the creator’s business and the reader has no business seeing it unless the creator says so; the plans and their PRICES are what a supporter needs to decide. A RETIRED plan is shown as retired and offers no action.
**A list that could not be READ is not an empty list.** The bar is left out ENTIRELY when the plans could not be read, because “this creator offers nothing” and “the plans could not be read” are different facts — and a page that showed the first when it knew the second would say that a creator whose plans are one read away has nothing to offer.
**A subscription with no holder is not a commitment**, so the accountless device is refused by name — and the client does not invent a subscriber.
**The lane reads BOTH answer shapes.** The service answers a write with a LIST (a listing shape) and the Studio’s own door forwards a SINGLE row (a write shape); the lane understands both, because a lane that understood only one of them reported a price of ZERO for a real subscription — which is exactly what the first run of this section did, and the proof caught it.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/planServer.ts` (the server-side read and the two writes), `apps/studio/src/lib/planLane.ts` (the browser half, with the 45s write timeout and the two answer shapes), `apps/studio/src/lib/planBar.ts` + `planBar.test.ts` (the pure bar, 14 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/planIntent.ts` (the URL intent), `apps/studio/src/app/api/plans/route.ts` and `.../api/subscriptions/route.ts` (the Studio’s doors), `apps/studio/src/components/AccountPanel.tsx` (the creator’s offer form), `apps/studio/src/components/Studio.tsx` (the subscribe intent), `apps/studio/src/app/u/[accountId]/route.ts` (the profile reads the plans and injects the bar), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §18).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1099/1100 (60 files)** — this increment’s own **14** cases pass; the one failure is a concurrent lane’s in-flight `probe293`; `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0** for this lane’s files, doc gate **exit 0**; the real-browser E2E **142/142** with the new §18.
**Bounds (§60).** The price is the creator’s CLAIM and the assurance travels with the plan, so a reader is told what the price is worth (ADR 0018). There is NO BILLING: a monthly subscription’s expiry is computed once and then stands, nothing charges the supporter again, and the price is a COMMITMENT rather than a payment. The plans bar shows what a creator offers and never what they earned. There is no way to edit a plan’s price or to retire one — a second plan with the same id is refused, so a creator who wants a different price offers a different plan. There is no ‘cancel and refund’, because there is no payment to refund.
## v302 — the SEARCH’s RELEVANCE order, and a proof that builds in a copy (M7/v302 — ADR 0016 amendment 302)

**The gap.** v290 made the search real — and left it answering in whatever order the database happened to return, which is not an order anybody can rely on. A reader who searched for a word and got a document-only match above the publication NAMED after that word was reading an accident.
**The order, and why it is the one it is.** A publication whose TITLE mentions the term comes before one that only mentions it inside its DOCUMENT: a title is what a person names their work, and a word in a title is a stronger signal than the same word somewhere among the nodes. Within each class the order is newest-first — the search is a FILTER of the listing, and a filter that reordered its rows would be answering a different question than the listing does.
**The ORDER BY lives in the QUERY, and the window carries NO sort.** A `Pageable`’s own sort is APPENDED to the query’s `ORDER BY`, so the two would fight — the search’s window now says nothing about the order and lets the finder answer it (the plain listing’s order is in its finder’s NAME, the search’s is in its query). The change is one line in the finder and one in the window, and it would have been a silent mis-ordering if the second half had been missed.
**The proof, over the real service and a real database.** §12 of `publish-proof.mjs` publishes its OWN two publications so the two classes can be told apart — one whose TITLE mentions the word, and one whose DOCUMENT mentions it and whose title does not — and then asserts the title match comes FIRST. It also asserts that a term matching nothing answers with an EMPTY list (not the whole table) and that the LIMIT still applies to a search, because a search that ignored the cap would answer with more than a listing would.
**And the publish proof now builds in an ISOLATED COPY — the same fix the real-browser harness got in v282.** `mvn package` ends with spring-boot’s `repackage`, which RENAMES the jar it just built; on Windows a jar a RUNNING service has open cannot be renamed, and the symptom is a build that fails for a reason that has nothing to do with the code (“Unable to rename …jar to …jar.original”). The proof now copies `pom.xml` + `src` into a throwaway directory, builds there, and runs THAT jar — which is also more honest: the proof runs the jar it just built rather than whichever one happens to be lying in the working tree.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/publish/PublishedProjectRepository.java` (the relevance `ORDER BY` in both finders), `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/search/SearchService.java` (the window carries no sort), `docs/evidence/m7/publish-proof.mjs` (a new §12, and the isolated build).
Measured: `node docs/evidence/m7/publish-proof.mjs` **exit 0** (all sections, including the new §12: the term found two publications, the TITLE match came first, a term that matches nothing answered with an empty list), and the module COMPILES.
**Bounds (§60).** The order is a RELEVANCE order, not a scored one: a title match beats a document match, and nothing else is weighed. There is no field weighting, no phrase matching, no stemming and no spell correction — a search that guessed would be a search that answered a different question than the one that was asked. The search is still a SEQUENTIAL SCAN over a text column (v290’s bound, unchanged).
## v303 — the OTHER direction of the edge: who an account follows (M7/v303 — ADR 0016 amendment 303)

**The gap.** v292 showed WHO FOLLOWS an account. The other end of the edge — the accounts an account FOLLOWS — had no door, even though the service’s repository has had a finder for it since v287. A social graph with one direction is half a graph, and the profile page said “1 follower” while saying nothing about the other side.
**The two directions are DIFFERENT questions, and they get different answers.** “Who watches me” and “who do I watch” are different questions, one query cannot answer both, and the repository has one finder per direction for that reason. The service’s door now takes `follower=` for the other direction, and answers with its OWN shape — `FollowingAnswer(accountId, followingCount, following)` — whose rows are called `following` and not `followers`, because a list of the accounts somebody watches is not a list of their followers, and a wire field with the wrong name is a small lie that grows.
**And the client needed a SECOND mapping, not a second parameter.** A follow row carries BOTH ends of the edge, so the followers list shows the row’s FOLLOWER and the following list shows the row’s FOLLOWEE. One mapping for both would have rendered the following list with the wrong names on it — and the wrong names would have been the follower’s own account, which is a plausible-looking list of the wrong accounts. The real-browser proof caught it: the count was right and the id in the list was not.
**Both lists live in ONE bar, not two.** Two floating panels saying “followers” and “following” side by side would be two panels a person has to read together to learn one thing: the shape of this account’s social life.
**What the real-browser proof grades.** §19: the first account follows the second AND the second follows the first — so ONE account has both ends of the edge — and the profile page is checked for the follower list, the following list, and that the two are TOLD APART (`data-follower=` against `data-following=`).
**A proof that failed because it expected a state the run had deliberately undone.** The first run of §19 checked that one account carried both lists — and the page showed none of them. The page was RIGHT: §15 deliberately UN-follows (it proves the un-follow path), so at that point the account really had no follower, and the CHECK was wrong. The section now builds both ends before it looks — and the record says so, because a proof that fails for a reason the run created has to be fixed in the proof, not in the product.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/FollowService.java` (`followingOf` and its own result type), `.../social/FollowController.java` (the `follower=` direction), `.../social/FollowDtos.java` (`FollowingAnswer`), `apps/studio/src/lib/followServer.ts` (`readFollowing`), `apps/studio/src/lib/followBar.ts` (the following list, and `toFollowingRow` — the OTHER mapping), `apps/studio/src/lib/followBar.test.ts` (+3 cases), `apps/studio/src/app/u/[accountId]/route.ts` (the profile reads both directions), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §19).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1102/1103 (61 files)** — this increment’s own **3** cases pass; the one failure is a concurrent lane’s in-flight `probe293`; `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0** for this lane’s files, doc gate **exit 0**; the real-browser E2E **147/147** with the new §19.
**Bounds (§60).** The following list is CAPPED by the service’s window (100 at the most) and the count beside it is the service’s own. Neither list says who follows whom — they say who, and the other end is one click away on either profile. There is no mutual-follow, no follower request to approve, and no way to see a follower’s followers: a social graph that grew branches would be a graph whose rules nobody has written down.
## v304 — the OWNER’S DESK: the report finally gets read (M7/v304 — ADR 0016 amendment 304)

**The gap.** v295 gave the report its four rules and v296 gave it a face on the artifact page — the COUNT and the reasons, to everybody. What was missing is the other half: **the person whose work is reported never learns what was said about it.** The only status a report could reach was the reporter withdrawing it. A complaint with nobody to answer it is not a moderation system; it is a counter.
**Who moderates here — and the reason the status is not called `reviewed`.** The plan asked for `reviewed`/`resolved` and named the reason they do not exist: *there is no moderator in this build*, and the service does not invent a role it has no policy for. So this increment looks for the actor that DOES exist — **the owner of the publication the report is about**, which is the one relation the service already keeps and the one the take-down already uses — and gives that actor exactly ONE power: to say the report has been seen. The status is `acknowledged`, and it is **not** `reviewed` and **not** `resolved`, because those two are a moderator’s verdict and this build has no moderator to render one. (`ReportDtos` still declares the other two, unreachable on purpose.)
**The desk does NOT name the reporter — the load-bearing decision.** v296 said the identity is "a moderator’s business", and this increment creates a moderator-ish actor, so it is the place where that sentence could have been taken back. It is not, and the reason is written down rather than left implied: **a claim is not a key**. The desk is opened by naming an account id, and account ids are PUBLIC (every publication carries its owner in its byline), so whatever the desk shows can be read by a caller who knows whose work it is. A name cannot be un-said, and the reporter is the person most harmed by being named to a stranger; so the desk shows the **reason** and the reporter’s **words** — the substance of the complaint — and keeps the identity for the reporter’s own view. The wire shape has no field that could carry it, and a test asserts that the `DeskEntry` record has no reporter-ish component, so this cannot be widened later by accident.
**A NEW package, `dev.nexus.api.moderation`, because the desk needs BOTH halves.** The social package deliberately does not check that a publication exists (its reports are about targets it never looks up), and the publish package deliberately knows nothing about reports. The desk needs the owner of a publication AND the reports against it, so it lives in a package of its own that composes the two (`PublishedProjectRepository` + `ReportRepository`).
**The Studio’s panel.** The artifact page is server-rendered and does not know who the device is (ADR 0018 is why there is no session), so the desk lives in the Studio, next to Published / Trail / Economy: it shows the report, the reason, the reporter’s words, where it stands, and ONE button — *Acknowledge*. A failed read is **not** an empty desk: the panel says "could not read: …" rather than "no reports against your work", because a panel that showed the first when it knew the second would tell an owner that nobody had complained — the one thing an owner must not be misled about.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§20).** A report filed by the second account against the first account’s own work → the owner opens the desk → the desk shows the reason and the reporter’s WORDS and **does not name the reporter** → the owner acknowledges it and the desk says so → the SERVICE recorded the disposition (read back independently of the browser) → and another account’s desk does not carry it.
**The proof’s first run failed, and the PRODUCT was right.** The section filed its report against the first publication and found the desk empty. The desk was right: §9 takes that publication down, and a report about a work that no longer exists is a complaint nobody can act on — the desk covers the publications that EXIST. The section now files against the fork (which exists) and the bound is recorded rather than worked around. Same shape as §19’s lesson: a proof that fails for a reason the run itself created has to be fixed in the proof, not in the product.
**And a debt from v302 is paid here.** v302 moved the search’s ORDER into the finder’s query and left the search’s own test asserting that the window still carried a sort — so the service suite was RED from v302 until now. The test now asserts the contract v302 introduced (the window carries the exact offset and **no order of its own**, because a `Pageable`’s sort would be APPENDED to the query’s `ORDER BY`). v302 should have carried that edit; the ledger says so rather than leaving the hole to be found by somebody else.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/moderation/ModerationService.java`, `ModerationController.java`, `ModerationDtos.java`, `InvalidModeration.java`, `NotYourPublication.java`; `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/moderation/ModerationServiceTest.java` (12 cases); `.../social/ReportDtos.java` (the `acknowledged` status), `.../social/ReportEntry.java` (`markAcknowledged`), `.../social/ReportRepository.java` (the `IN` finder the desk needs); `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/search/SearchServiceTest.java` (the v302 debt); `apps/studio/src/lib/moderation.ts` (the pure mapping), `moderationServer.ts` (the server-side half), `moderationLane.ts` (the browser half), `moderation.test.ts` (12 cases); `apps/studio/src/app/api/moderation/route.ts` (the Studio’s own door); `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (the desk panel); `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §20).
Measured: the moderation package **12/12** and the service’s social package **96/96**; `apps/studio` **1112/1112 (60 files)**; `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**; doc gate **exit 0**; the real-browser E2E **154/154** with the new §20. (The service’s FULL suite is red in six cases in `PublishServiceTest` — a concurrent lane’s stale mocks against their own in-flight publish work, not this one’s.)
**Bounds (§60).** The desk is opened by a CLAIM of ownership, exactly as the take-down is, and it is no stronger than that claim: there is no session (ADR 0018), the assurance is `unsigned-local`, and an account id is public — so a caller who knows an owner’s id can read that owner’s desk, and the words on it. That is the price of having a desk at all in a build with no identity, and it is stated rather than hidden. The desk covers the owner’s most recent 200 publications, and the reports against publications that no longer EXIST are on no desk (the work they are about is gone). The owner can say ONE thing — that they have seen it — and cannot dismiss a report, cannot remove it, and cannot see who filed it. There is no queue, no priority, no assignment, no notification and no appeal: a moderation workflow needs rules this build has not written down.
## v305 — the REPORTER’s own view: where the complaint stands (M7/v305 — ADR 0016 amendment 305)

**The gap.** A reporter files a complaint and never learns what became of it. v295 put the finder in the repository (`findByReporterAccountIdOrderByCreatedAtDesc`) and v295’s service filed the report — but nothing ever READ that finder back: there was no door for it. So the report’s life was half-written: the owner could read it (v304), the reporter could not.
**The door, and the OTHER direction’s own shape.** `GET /reports?reporter=` answers with `FiledReportsAnswer(reporterAccountId, reports, count, limit)` and its rows are a record of their own — `FiledReport(id, targetId, reason, detail, status, createdAt)`. The field names are the report’s own, so a client reads one shape whichever door it came through; and the answer names the REPORTER in its own field, because a list of somebody’s own complaints is a list of their own.
**The wire carries NO identity, and that is the load-bearing decision of this increment.** The reader of this list IS the reporter, so a field naming them would say nothing they did not know — while saying something to a caller who merely knew the account id. The NAME is the one thing a report cannot afford to leak (v304’s desk refuses to carry it for the same reason), so the filed direction’s rows do not carry it, and the real-browser proof grades the wire itself: no `reporterId`, no `handle`, no `assurance`.
**The status vocabulary moved into the report’s OWN module.** `reportStatusText` used to live in the desk’s module, which is the wrong way round — the desk speaks the report’s language, not the other way about. It now lives in `reportBar.ts` beside `reportReasonText`, the desk re-exports it, and the reporter’s view uses it: ONE place says what `open`, `acknowledged` and `rejected` mean, so the two halves of the panel cannot drift apart.
**The Studio’s panel has TWO halves now.** `YOUR DESK` is what came to you (the reports against your work, with the one power you have over them); `YOU FILED` is what you sent (your own complaints, and where each one stands). The reporter’s half is the ONLY place a reporter sees a disposition, and it is also the only place the reporter’s identity appears in this feature — because the reader is the reporter.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§21).** The reporter opens the panel → sees the complaint they filed → the panel says where it stands (`acknowledged`, because §20 acknowledged it) → the SERVICE agrees, read independently of the browser → the wire carries no identity → and the owner’s desk still names nobody.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/ReportService.java` (`reportsFiledBy`, and the reader’s own account check), `.../social/ReportController.java` (the `reporter=` direction and the identity-free mapping), `.../social/ReportDtos.java` (`FiledReportsAnswer`, `FiledReport`); `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/ReportServiceTest.java` (+3 cases); `apps/studio/src/lib/reportServer.ts` (`readFiledReports`), `reportLane.ts` (`readMyReports`), `reportLane.test.ts` (new, 3 cases), `reportBar.ts` (the status and the target on the row, and `reportStatusText`), `reportBar.test.ts` (+4 cases), `moderation.ts` (the vocabulary now comes from the report’s module); `apps/studio/src/app/api/reports/route.ts` (a GET beside the POST); `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (the `YOU FILED` half); `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §21).
Measured: the service’s social package **99/99** (the report package 19/19), `apps/studio` **1119/1119 (61 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **159/159** with the new §21.
**Bounds (§60).** The door is opened by NAMING an account, the same posture as every other door in this build — so a caller who knows an account id can read that account’s complaints (their reasons, their words and the works they complained about). The identity is not among them ✓, but the complaint itself is, and that is the honest price of a build with no sessions. The reporter is NOT notified: they have to come and look, because there is no mail and no push in this build. The reporter cannot change the reason or add to the complaint — a report is appended (v295’s rule), and the withdrawal exists at the service but the panel does not offer it yet. And the OTHER direction still carries the reporter’s identity in its wire — that shape dates from v295, the artifact page has never shown it, and it is named here rather than left for somebody else to find.
## v306 — the REPORTER can take a complaint back (M7/v306 — ADR 0016 amendment 306)

**The gap.** The service has had the withdrawal since v295 (`DELETE /reports/{reportId}?claimer=`, and it refuses somebody else’s report by name). The Studio had no button for it, so a reporter could file a complaint and never take it back — and a complaint they no longer stand by stayed on the owner’s desk as if it were live. The door was there; the reach was not.
**The three layers, in the house order.** The server-side half (`reportServer.withdrawReport`) calls the service’s DELETE; the Studio’s own door (`DELETE /api/reports?reportId=&claimer=`) forwards it, so the browser never learns where `services/nexus-api` lives; the browser lane (`reportLane.withdrawReport`) answers with a REASON and never a thrown error, and carries the 45-second write timeout the other lanes carry.
**Withdrawing is NOT deleting, and the panel says so.** The row STAYS and its status becomes `rejected` — the same rule the taken-down publication and the acknowledged report keep: a report that was withdrawn is a FACT that happened. The button appears only on the rows that are still `open`; a report that was acknowledged or withdrawn has nothing left to withdraw, and offering the button there would be offering a write that cannot do anything.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§22).** The reporter opens the panel → the OPEN row offers its withdrawal → the withdrawal lands and the panel says so → the SERVICE recorded it (read back independently of the browser) → and the row STAYS, with its status, in the service’s answer about that publication.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/reportServer.ts` (`withdrawReport`), `apps/studio/src/lib/reportLane.ts` (`withdrawReport`), `apps/studio/src/lib/reportLane.test.ts` (+3 cases), `apps/studio/src/app/api/reports/route.ts` (a DELETE beside the GET and the POST), `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (the button on the open rows, and the note that says whether it moved), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §22).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1122/1122 (61 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **163/163** with the new §22.
**Bounds (§60).** The withdrawal’s gate is the SAME claim the take-down’s is: naming the account id, and the service comparing it with the row. The account id is PUBLIC (it is the byline of every publication), so a caller who knows a reporter’s id can withdraw a report that reporter filed — the build’s posture, stated here rather than hidden, and the same price the take-down already pays. The reporter cannot change the reason or add to the complaint (a report is appended, v295’s rule), cannot withdraw a report that was already acknowledged (the service says it did not move), and gets no notification of anything (there is no mail and no push in this build).
## v307 — the WIRE stops carrying the reporter’s name (M7/v307 — ADR 0016 amendment 307)

**The gap, and it is the same one v296 answered with a PAGE.** v295 made the report’s wire shape the client’s declared `Report` shape field for field, plus the reporter’s handle and assurance — and v296 then made the artifact page decline to SHOW them, because "a page that printed them would turn a report into a PUBLIC ACCUSATION with no moderation behind it". The PAGE was careful and the WIRE was not: the reporter’s identity sat in every answer about a publication, on a door **anybody can call**. A rule that only the page keeps is half a rule.
**The wire carries NO identity now.** `Report` is `(id, targetId, reason, detail, status, createdAt)` — what the report is about, why, what was said, and where it stands. The service still **stores** the reporter’s account id (it has to: the withdrawal compares it with the row, and v304’s desk needs to know which reports are against which owner’s work), but the wire does not carry it.
**And the reporter’s OWN view still names them — because the reader of that list IS the reporter** (v305). The two are not in tension: the name is the reporter’s to give, and they give it only to themselves.
**The attribution is now proved a DIFFERENT way, and it is a better proof.** The real-browser harness used to check `reporterId === the device’s account id` in the public answer — a check that could only exist because the wire leaked. Now it reads the **reporter’s own door** (`GET /reports?reporter=`) and asserts the service FOUND the report by that account id. That is the attribution stated the way it actually happens: the service looks the report up by the reporter’s own id. The harness also grades the wire itself — no `reporterId`, no `handle`, no `assurance` — so the rule cannot be quietly widened later.
**One shape for every door.** The filed direction’s own row record (v305’s `FiledReport`) is gone: the two directions now carry the SAME report shape, so a client reads one shape whichever door the answer came through. The wire is also **narrower than the client’s declared model** (`ops.ts`) on purpose, and the record says so rather than leaving the two to look like they agree when they do not.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/ReportDtos.java` (the `Report` record loses the identity; `FiledReport` is gone and the filed answer carries `List<Report>`), `.../social/ReportController.java` (one mapping for both directions), `apps/studio/src/lib/reportServer.ts` (the client’s wire shape), `apps/studio/src/lib/reportBar.ts` (the local shape), `apps/studio/src/lib/reportLane.test.ts` (the fixture), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (§16’s attribution check, plus the wire’s own check).
Measured: the service’s social package **99/99**, `apps/studio` **1122/1122 (61 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **165/165** with §16’s two new checks.
**Bounds (§60).** The reporter’s identity is still STORED, so the service can answer a withdrawal and the desk can find the right reports — this increment changes what is PUBLISHED, not what is remembered. The reporter’s own door names them to anyone who knows their account id (the same claim-posture as every other door in this build), but it names them in a list of their OWN complaints. A client that needs the reporter of a report for a legitimate purpose has no way to get it — and that is the honest answer in a build with no identity to give.
## v308 — the subscription’s STANDING is computed, not remembered (M7/v308 — ADR 0016 amendment 308)

**The gap.** The subscription row carries an `active` flag, and the flag cannot say what it was asked to say. The flag is set to false when the supporter cancels — and it is **never** touched again, because a period runs out on a day when nothing writes to the row. So a subscription whose period ended a month ago still reads `active: true` forever. The service’s **total** summed that flag, which means the answer said the subscriber was still backing a plan whose period had run out — a number about a promise nobody had made.
**The standing is now COMPUTED, from the row and the clock.** `SubscriptionService.standingOf(row)` answers one of three: `cancelled` (the supporter ended it), `expired` (the period ran out), `active` (it is still standing). The stored flag keeps its own honest meaning — *the supporter has not cancelled*, a fact about the past that never goes stale — and the standing is the fact about now.
**The wire carries both, and they are different facts.** `Subscription` gains a `status` field — the computed standing — and keeps `active` — what the row says. A row that says "active" and an expiry that has passed are BOTH true, and only one of them is the standing of the subscription. The service’s **total** now sums the subscriptions that are still standing: an expired subscription is not a commitment any more, and counting it would say the subscriber is backing a plan whose period has run out.
**The client prefers the SERVICE’s word, and computes its own only when the service is silent.** A clock is a fact about a machine, and the system of record’s clock is the one that decides when a period ends — so the service’s `status` wins when it is there, and the client’s own computation (the rule the monetization module has always stated) is the fallback for a service that does not say. The row’s `statusText` and `isActive` come from ONE standing, so the two can never disagree.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§18, +2 checks).** The subscription’s row carries the standing the service computed (`active`), and the total counts the standing subscriptions (1500 cents for the one standing subscription). The EXPIRED case is graded by the service’s own cases — the expiry is a date the harness cannot wait for, and a proof that waited a month to check a rule would be a proof that never runs.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/SubscriptionDtos.java` (the three statuses, the `status` field, and the total’s doc — which also said "summed by the DATABASE", which was never true: the sum is the service’s), `.../social/SubscriptionService.java` (`standingOf`), `.../social/SubscriptionController.java` (the wire carries the standing; the total counts the standing ones), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/SubscriptionServiceTest.java` (+5 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/economy.ts` (`subscriptionStandingOf` and `subscriptionStandingText`), `apps/studio/src/lib/economy.test.ts` (+3 cases), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (§18’s two new checks).
Measured: the service’s social package **104/104** (the subscription package 25/25), `apps/studio` **1125/1125 (61 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **167/167** with §18’s two new checks.
**Bounds (§60).** Nothing renews an expired subscription: there is no billing in this build (ADR 0018), so an expired subscription stays expired until a NEW one is made — **and a new one cannot be made**, because "one subscription per supporter per plan" is the DATABASE’s unique constraint and it does not know that the old row has expired. So a subscription whose period ran out is a dead end for that supporter and that plan, and the fix is a schema change (a partial unique index over the standing rows) rather than a line of service code — which is why it is named here and left for an increment that can carry a migration. The standing is computed at READ time, so two readers a moment apart can see two standings — that is what a clock is, and it is why the client asks the service rather than deciding for itself.
## v309 — the economy panel shows the SUBSCRIPTIONS and the PLANS, for real (M7/v309 — ADR 0016 amendment 309)

**The gap, and it is the gap a panel goes stale in.** v274 built the creator-economy panel and, because ADR 0018 blocked server endpoints, the panel said **"subscriptions are not built yet — a subscription needs a plan catalog, and that is a product decision this build has not made"**. That was TRUE when it was written. Then v301 built the service’s plans and subscriptions — and the panel kept saying it, so the product was telling a reader that a feature it had was not there. **A panel that says a true thing about a feature that has since arrived is still telling the reader something false**, and nobody notices a panel going stale: the assertion is in the panel, not in the code.
**The door stops being a placeholder.** `/api/economy` was a 503 that said "not built"; it now reads the service’s real doors — `GET /subscriptions?subscriber=` and `GET /plans?creator=` — through a new server-side half (`economyServer.ts`). The browser still never learns where `services/nexus-api` lives, and the route is `force-dynamic`.
**The account is NAMED, because this build has no session.** The route reads `?account=` and refuses an answer without one — the same claim the take-down and the withdrawal name. The panel’s own rule is unchanged and still load-bearing: a device with no account has no economy, and it says so rather than reading somebody else’s.
**The panel has THREE sections now, each with its own honest empty state.** Tips (what people gave), Subscriptions (what this account backs) and Your plans (what this account offers). The two new reads are a state of their own, because the tips and the subscriptions are two DIFFERENT reads of two different doors — and one of them failing must not blank the other.
**The subscription’s standing on the panel is the SERVICE’s word** (v308): the service computes it against its own clock, and the client prefers it over its own computation. The panel says `Active` because the subscription still stands — and it would say `Expired` when the period ran out, without anything having to write to the row.
**An EXISTING proof check changed its premise, and the record says so.** §17 graded "the panel names the gap instead of faking an empty feature" by looking for the words "subscriptions are not built". v309 closes that gap, so the check now grades the opposite — that the panel no longer claims the feature is unbuilt — and its comment says why: the assertion was about a gap that has since closed, and a proof that keeps grading a closed gap would keep the product honest about a thing that is no longer true.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§23).** The SUBSCRIBER opens the panel and sees the subscription it holds, with its standing (`Active`) → the CREATOR opens the panel and sees the plan it offers, with what it costs and for how long (¥15.00 · per month) → and the panel no longer says the subscriptions are unbuilt.
