Measured: `apps/studio` **1176/1176 (65 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **144/144** (unchanged — the service has sent the payer’s name since v298), `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **251/251** with the new §45.
**Bounds (§60).** This increment is the CLIENT’s half of a fact the service has been sending since the tip’s own beginning — the name was never missing from the wire, only from the panel — and the record says so, because "the panel was not saying it" and "the data was not there" are different gaps. The name is a CLAIM (the service records it and verifies nothing — ADR 0018), exactly like the follower’s name on the follow row and the supporter’s name on the subscription row. A payer who changes their handle afterwards is still shown under the name they tipped with — a fact about the past. And **the tip’s amount and message are still the substance**: the panel names the person because the service knows who, and it does not say the money bought anything — a tip is a gift, and this build has no fulfilment to promise (v298’s bound).
## v332 — the tipper sees what THEY gave (M7/v332 — ADR 0016 amendment 335)

**The gap.** A tip is a relation, and the product had only one side of it: the RECIPIENT could see the money that came to them (v298) — and the PAYER could see nothing. A reader who gave money and could not see what they gave had a receipt with no place to read it. The service’s repository has had the finder for the payer’s side since v298 (`findByPayerAccountIdOrderByCreatedAtDesc`) — with no service method, no door and no panel to reach it. That is the same gap the subscription had before v310, and the same shape of fix.
**The same door, the other direction.** `GET /tips?payer=` — beside `?recipient=` — the way the subscriptions door answers both "what do I back" and "who backs me" (v310). The service gains `tipsGiven` and `totalGiven`, and the total is summed by the DATABASE (`sumAmountCentsByPayerAccountId`), not by the service: a total the service added up in memory would be the sum of a WINDOW, and a window’s sum is not a total.
**The panel grows a section, not a row.** The economy panel gains **Tips you gave** under the tips it received — a receipt, in the reader’s own account. It says whether the tip was given in the open or anonymously (the payer’s own choice, shown to the payer), and it does not name the recipient: the recipient is a public identity, but the PAYER is the one reading, and the tip’s own amount and message are the substance of what they gave.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§46).** The tipper’s own side shows what they gave → and the SERVICE agrees, read independently of the browser: the payer’s own door answers with the tips they gave.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/TipRepository.java` (the payer’s sum, by the database), `.../social/TipService.java` (`tipsGiven`, `totalGiven`), `.../social/TipController.java` (the door’s `?payer=`), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/TipServiceTest.java` (+2 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/tipServer.ts` (`readTipsGiven`), `apps/studio/src/app/api/tips/route.ts` (the door forwards the payer), `apps/studio/src/lib/tipLane.ts` (`readMyTipsGiven`), `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (the "Tips you gave" section), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §46).
Measured: the service’s social + moderation packages **146/146** (the tip package 14/14), `apps/studio` **1176/1176 (65 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **253/253** with the new §46.
**Bounds (§60).** The door now takes EITHER a recipient or a payer, and refuses a question that names neither — by name, because a tip list with nobody on either side of it is nobody’s list. The payer’s list does NOT say when each tip was given: the wire carries `createdAt` and the panel could format it, and it does not yet — a receipt without dates is a receipt without a history, and that is named here rather than quietly shipped. And nothing here changes what a tip DOES: a tip is a gift, this build has no fulfilment to promise (v298’s bound), and the receipt says what was given and not what was bought.
## v333 — the tip’s receipt says WHEN it was given (M7/v333 — ADR 0016 amendment 336)

**The gap.** v332 gave the tipper a receipt — the tips they gave, with the amounts and the messages — and the receipt had no dates on it. The wire has carried `createdAt` since the tip’s own beginning, and the lane was narrowing every tip to an amount, a message and two flags. So a reader could look at what they gave and not when: a receipt without dates is a receipt without a history, and a history is what makes a list of gifts readable.
**One style for every date.** The tip’s date is formatted by the same helper the subscription’s two dates use (v328, v329) — the service’s own clock, in UTC, in one shape — so a reader comparing a tip’s date with a subscription’s date is reading two dates written the same way. A wire with no date says nothing: the panel shows no date rather than the epoch.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§47).** The tipper’s receipt is on screen → and the receipt says the same instant the SERVICE recorded, read independently of the browser.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/tipLane.ts` (the tips keep the instant), `apps/studio/src/lib/economy.ts` (the `tipGivenText` helper), `apps/studio/src/lib/economy.test.ts` (+2 cases), `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (the receipt says when), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §47).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1178/1178 (65 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **146/146** (unchanged — the wire carried the date since v298), `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **255/255** with the new §47.
**Bounds (§60).** The RECEIVED side of the tip — the recipient’s list — still does not say when each tip arrived: the same wire carries the same instant, and the same helper would say it, and this increment put the date on one side only — the side that had no receipt at all. That is named here rather than quietly shipped, and it is a one-line change. And the date is the service’s clock, not the browser’s: a reader in another timezone reads a UTC instant, and the panel does not pretend to have converted it.
## v334 — the RECEIVED side says when each tip arrived (M7/v334 — ADR 0016 amendment 337)

**The gap.** v333 put a date on the tipper’s receipt and named the other side as a one-line change — and this is that change. The RECIPIENT’s list showed the amount, the message and (since v331) the payer’s name, and not WHEN the money arrived: a creator reading the gifts that came to them could not tell a gift from last Tuesday from one from last year. The wire carries the instant on both sides, and the same helper formats it.
**The lane stops narrowing it away, on both sides.** The received lane kept the payer’s name and the instant; the given lane’s type did not say the instant even though the wire sends it — a type that says less than the wire does is a small lie in the source, and it is corrected here. The lane also gained a test file of its own (it had none): the cases grade that each direction asks the door it means and that neither throws the wire’s facts away.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§48).** The recipient’s tip row is on screen → and the row says the same instant the SERVICE recorded, read independently of the browser.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/tipLane.ts` (both lanes declare the instant; the given lane’s type is corrected), `apps/studio/src/lib/tipLane.test.ts` (new — 4 cases: each direction asks its own door, the wire’s facts survive, a refused read is not an empty receipt), `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (the received row says when), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §48).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1182/1182 (66 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **146/146** (unchanged — the wire carried the instant since v298), `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **257/257** with the new §48.
**Bounds (§60).** The date is the service’s clock in UTC on both sides, and the panel does not convert it: a reader in another timezone reads a UTC instant, and pretending to have localised it would be a conversion the panel cannot justify. The tips’ own amount and message are still the substance — a tip is a gift and this build has no fulfilment to promise (v298’s bound) — so the date says WHEN a gift arrived, not what it bought.
## v335 — the commenter sees what THEY wrote, and can take it back (M7/v335 — ADR 0016 amendment 338)

**The gap.** A comment is a relation, and like the tip (v332) the product had only one side of it: the publication’s page LISTS the comments — and the commenter could not see what they had written there, nor take it back. The service’s repository has had the finder for the author’s side since v285 (`findByAuthorAccountIdOrderByCreatedAtDesc`) — with no service method, no door and no way to reach it. A comment is a thing a person might want to find again, or unsay.
**The service grows the other direction.** `GET /comments?author=` — beside `?projectId=` — the way the tips and the subscriptions doors answer both sides of a relation. The service gains `commentsWritten`, and a question that names neither side is REFUSED BY NAME (a comment list with nobody on either side of it is nobody’s list).
**The composer is where it belongs.** The **YOUR COMMENTS HERE** list lives in the comment composer — the place a commenter already goes to say something — and it carries a **Take it back** button on each comment that is still standing. The take-back itself is the chain v321 built (the service’s door, the studio’s door, the lane) — v321 left it with no door in the PRODUCT, and this is that door.
**Two small truths the section had to get right.** A comment that was already taken back is shown as **"this comment was removed by its author"** — the same wording the artifact page uses — and it carries no button, because there is nothing to take back. And the take-back says WHICH of the two things happened: the service answers "removed: true" when a row was taken back and "removed: false" when there was nothing to take back, so the composer says "took the comment back" or "that comment was already gone" — never one word for both (the same rule the un-like keeps, v320).
**What the real-browser proof grades (§49).** The commenter posts a comment of its own → the composer shows it under YOUR COMMENTS HERE → the SERVICE agrees, read independently of the browser: the author’s own door answers with the comments they wrote → the commenter takes it back → and the SERVICE agrees the comment is marked as removed, and it STAYS on the record (a taken-back comment is a fact that happened, not a row that was never there).
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/CommentService.java` (`commentsWritten`), `.../social/CommentController.java` (the door’s `?author=`), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/CommentServiceTest.java` (+2 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/commentServer.ts` (`readCommentsWritten`), `apps/studio/src/app/api/comments/route.ts` (a GET beside the POST), `apps/studio/src/lib/commentLane.ts` (`readMyComments`), `apps/studio/src/components/CommentComposer.tsx` (the list, the take-back, and the two honest sentences), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §49).
Measured: the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148** (the comment package 21/21), `apps/studio` **1182/1182 (66 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **261/261** with the new §49.
**Bounds (§60).** The list is the commenter’s OWN comments, across every publication, and the composer shows the ones on the publication it is open over — the service has no "comments on this publication by this account" finder, and narrowing in the browser would be a filter over a window (the window is the most recent N of everything they wrote). The take-back is a CLAIM (the service compares the claimer with the comment’s author — exactly as strong as the like’s owner check, and exactly as weak: ADR 0018). And the list is the commenter’s own — nobody else’s comments are shown here, because a thread’s comments are on the publication’s page already, and this is the half that was missing.
## v336 — the artifact page names WHO liked it (M7/v336 — ADR 0016 amendment 339)

**The gap.** The follow bar has named its followers since v288, and the like bar said only a count — so a reader could see WHO follows a creator and not WHO liked their work, which is the smaller of the two facts and the one a creator would want to see most. The wire had carried the likers’ names all along (the like row keeps `like_handle` and the wire has the slot); the artifact page simply dropped them when it rendered the bar.
**The bar names them, in the follow bar’s shape.** `LikeBarFacts` gains `likers` and `renderLikeBar` renders a list beside the count — the same `<ul class="nexus-likes-list">` with one `<li data-liker="...">` per liker that the follow bar has used since v288, and the same shape of text: the name when the wire carried one, the account id when it did not, and the assurance beside it when there is one. The artifact page passes the likers it read; nothing about the wire’s SHAPE changed.
**The proof of this increment had to wait for two repairs, and both are named here.** (1) **The Studio’s publish door called a slow answer "unreachable".** Its budget was 5 seconds, and the Studio’s own door is a `next dev` server whose FIRST call to a route pays that route’s compile — so a publish that timed out while the compiler was busy was answered as "nexus-api unreachable at …: The operation was aborted due to timeout". That is a FALSE sentence about a service that was there, answering a moment later, and the tip lane’s door made exactly this fix in v294 (45 seconds, not 10) for exactly this reason. This door had never caught up; it does now (`PUBLISH_CALL_TIMEOUT_MS = 45_000`, and a case that grades the budget). (2) **The harness gave up before the product was real.** Its budgets (the Studio’s start-up, the hydration, the publish, the comment, the trail) were sized for an idle machine, and a proof that measures the machine is not a proof — so they are bigger now, and the publish step says what the page held when it fails, because a timeout that says nothing about what the page held leaves the reader guessing.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§50).** A device with an account likes the publication again (the earlier section took its like back — that is what the taking-back is for) → the artifact page’s like bar names the people who liked it → and the SERVICE agrees, read independently of the browser: it sends the likers’ names on the wire, and the bar shows those very names.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/likeBar.ts` (the facts carry the likers; the bar names them), `apps/studio/src/lib/likeBar.test.ts` (+2 cases), `apps/studio/src/app/p/[projectId]/route.ts` (the page passes the likers it read), `apps/studio/src/lib/publishServer.ts` (the publish door’s budget), `apps/studio/src/lib/publishServer.test.ts` (+1 case), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §50; the publish diagnostic; the budgets).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1201/1201 (66 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **264/264** with the new §50.
**Bounds (§60).** The names are CLAIMS — the service records them and verifies nothing (ADR 0018), exactly like the follower’s name in the follow bar. The bar names the likers the service sent, in the order it sent them, and it names only as many as the service’s window holds: the list is a WINDOW, not the whole history of who ever liked the publication. A liker whose claim carried no handle is shown by their account id — an address is better than a hole, and the bar never invents a name. The publish door’s bigger budget makes a slow answer wait rather than be misreported as a missing one; it does not make a HUNG service answer (a service that never answers still times out, and says so). And the harness’s bigger budgets make the proof wait for a real answer; they do not make a broken product pass — a check that fails for any other reason still fails.
## v337 — every Studio door to the service is bounded, and a timeout is not called "unreachable" (M7/v337 — ADR 0016 amendment 340)

**The gap.** Seven doors in the Studio’s own lib folder — the comments, the likes, the follows, the economy, the moderation desk, the plans and the tips — fetched the service with **no budget at all**. A service that never answered left the Studio’s route waiting forever, and for a SERVER-RENDERED page (the artifact page, the explore page, the profile page) that is a reader waiting forever, with no sentence and no reason. The house’s rule — "a failed read is NOT an empty list" — has nothing to say about a read that never fails, so the read has to be BOUNDED and the bound has to be NAMED.
**One budget, named once.** A new `serviceCall.ts` holds the budget (15 seconds — long enough for a cold first write on a busy machine, short enough that a reader is not left waiting) and the signal that stops a call that is not answered in time. Every one of the seven doors now passes that signal, and the publish door keeps its own 45 seconds (a WRITE, and the one door v294 already gave a budget to).
**A timeout is not an unreachable service.** The catch in every door said "nexus-api unreachable at …", which is a FALSE sentence for a service that is merely slow: a reader who is told the thing is gone when it is only late stops looking for it. The shared helper now says "the service at … did not answer within 15 seconds" for a timeout, and keeps "nexus-api unreachable at …" for a service that really is not there.
**A correction to amendment 339.** That record explained the publish door’s old 5-second budget by the route compile — and the compile is in the BROWSER→Studio hop, not in the Studio→service hop where that budget lives. The honest explanation is the one this amendment makes: the service was SLOW (a cold first write, or a busy machine) and a 5-second budget turned a slow answer into a false "unreachable". The fix is the same either way; the reason was wrong, and it is corrected here rather than left standing.
**What the real-browser proof grades.** The whole E2E runs against the real service in a real database, and every one of the doors it drives — the artifact page’s comments and likes, the desk’s reports, the economy’s plans, the tips — now answers with a bounded call. The proof’s own numbers are unchanged: 264/264.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/serviceCall.ts` (new — the budget, the signal, and the reason a timeout is not an unreachable service), `apps/studio/src/lib/serviceCall.test.ts` (new — 4 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/commentServer.ts`, `.../likeServer.ts`, `.../followServer.ts`, `.../economyServer.ts`, `.../moderationServer.ts`, `.../planServer.ts`, `.../tipServer.ts` (every call is bounded, and every catch names a timeout for what it is), `apps/studio/src/lib/planServer.test.ts` (+1 case — a door’s call carries a signal).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1206/1206 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **264/264**.
**Bounds (§60).** The budget is a BOUND, not a promise: a service that answers in 16 seconds is still a service that answers, and this change makes the Studio say "did not answer" for it — which is a true sentence about the answer, and a false sentence about the service. A door that refuses a slow answer is better than a page that never renders, and better than a page that renders "nothing" — but the honest version of a slow service is a faster one, and this is a bound, not a fix. The doors of the AUDIT trail are not in this change: the trail belongs to the other lane, and its door is edited there. And the browser’s own half of each lane keeps its own budget (the tip lane’s 45 seconds, v294) — the two hops are separate waits, and a call can be bounded at both ends.
## v338 — the publish and economy lanes get a budget, and a slow compile is not an unreachable Studio (M7/v338 — ADR 0016 amendment 341)

**The gap.** v294 gave the tip lane a budget and explained why: the Studio’s own door is a `next dev` server, and the FIRST call to a route pays that route’s COMPILE, so a lane with no budget could be killed by a slow compile and report it as a failure of the thing it was asking for. Seven lanes took that lesson (the comments, the follows, the likes, the moderation desk, the plans, the reports, the tips) — and **two did not**: the publish lane and the economy lane, which is to say the two lanes the Studio uses most. A publish that was slow to compile was answered "studio server unreachable", which is a FALSE sentence about a Studio that was there.
**The same budget, the same reason.** `publishLane.ts` and `economyLane.ts` now carry the 45-second budget v294 chose — the compile is the cost, and a compile is not a hung service — and every call passes a signal that can stop it. The remix lane rides on the publish lane (it reads the publication through it), so it is covered by the same change.
**A timeout says what it is.** The publish lane’s catch said "studio server unreachable: …"; a timeout now says "the Studio did not answer within 45 seconds", and a Studio that really is not there still says "unreachable" and says where it looked. A reader who is told the thing is gone when it is only late stops looking for it — the same lie the house’s rule about failed reads is about, one hop earlier.
**What the proof grades.** The unit case in `publishLane.test.ts` grades the wiring: the call to the Studio carries a signal, so a Studio that never answers cannot hold the lane forever. The real-browser E2E still passes end to end (264/264) against the real service in a real database — and the publish it drives is one of the calls this change bounds.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/publishLane.ts` (the budget, the signal on every call, and the honest reason), `apps/studio/src/lib/economyLane.ts` (the budget and the signal), `apps/studio/src/lib/publishLane.test.ts` (+1 case).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1207/1207 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **264/264**.
**Bounds (§60).** The AUDIT lane is not in this change: the trail belongs to the other lane, and its door is edited there — so the trail’s browser half is still unbudgeted, and that is named here rather than quietly left out of the sentence "every lane". The lanes that talk to a CONTAINER (the build, the deploy, the save, the world-save) are not in this change either: they have their own budget inside the container runner, and a container that never answers is a different fact from a Studio that never answers. And the budget is a BOUND, not a promise: a Studio that takes 46 seconds to compile is a slow Studio, and this change makes the lane say so — it does not make the compile faster.
## v339 — the artifact page reads its four sources at once (M7/v339 — ADR 0016 amendment 342)

**The gap.** The artifact page read its four side sources — the reports, the trail, the likes and the comments — one after another. They are four INDEPENDENT facts about the same publication, so reading them in sequence made the page wait for their SUM: a service that was slow by a second made the reader wait four. The page’s own rule (v280, v284, v286) is that each source is read independently and a failed read leaves its section out — and it was independent in the failure and serial in the waiting, which is the worst of both.
**One wait, not four.** The four reads now go out together (`Promise.all`), so the page waits for the SLOWEST of them and not for their sum. Nothing else about the page changes: the same reads, the same bars, the same "a failed read leaves its section out" rule, the same order of sections in the HTML.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§51).** The artifact page carries ALL FOUR of its sources — the likes bar, the comments section, the reports bar and the history section. A concurrent read is exactly the kind of change that can quietly drop one of the four, so the proof says so in one sentence: the page still has every one of them.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/app/p/[projectId]/route.ts` (the four reads go out together), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §51).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1207/1207 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **265/265** with the new §51.
**Bounds (§60).** The page’s own read — the PUBLICATION itself — still comes first and still decides the 404: a page for a publication that does not exist is not a page with four empty sections, and reading the sources first would spend four calls on a publication nobody will see. The four reads are concurrent, not merged: four calls go to the service at once, so a service that is already struggling is asked to do four things at a time — this makes the WAITING shorter and the ARRIVAL burstier, and a service that needs the traffic spread out would want the opposite. And the change is a WAIT, not a speed-up: nothing here makes the service answer faster, and a reader who opens the page while the service is slow still waits for the slowest of the four.
## v340 — the profile page reads its four sources at once (M7/v340 — ADR 0016 amendment 343)

**The gap.** The same gap v339 closed on the artifact page, on the second server-rendered page: the PROFILE page read its four side sources — the plans this creator offers, the tips they were given, the accounts that follow them and the accounts they follow — one after another. They are four INDEPENDENT facts about the same account, so a reader waited for their SUM rather than for the slowest of the four.
**One wait, not four.** The four reads now go out together (`Promise.all`). Nothing else about the page changes: the same reads, the same bars, the same "a failed read leaves its bar out" rule, the same order in the HTML.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§52).** The profile page carries ALL of its sources — the plans bar, the tips bar and the follow bar. A concurrent read is exactly the kind of change that can quietly drop one of them, so the proof says so in one sentence.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/app/u/[accountId]/route.ts` (the four reads go out together), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §52).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1207/1207 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **266/266** with the new §52.
**Bounds (§60).** The profile page’s own read — the ACCOUNT itself — still comes first and still decides the 404, for the same reason the artifact page’s publication read still does. The four reads are concurrent, not merged: four calls reach the service at once, so this makes the WAITING shorter and the ARRIVAL burstier. The EXPLORE page has the same shape and is not in this change — it belongs to the other lane. And this is a WAIT, not a speed-up: nothing here makes the service answer faster.
## v341 — the profile’s tip bar says WHEN each tip arrived (M7/v341 — ADR 0016 amendment 344)

**The gap.** The tip bar on the PROFILE page — the page a reader lands on when they want to know about a person — showed the amounts and the messages and not WHEN. The service has carried the instant since the tip’s own beginning, and the ECONOMY panel has said it since v333; the profile page had the same wire and the same clock and said nothing. A gift with no date on it is a gift without a history, and a creator reading the gifts that came to them could not tell a gift from last Tuesday from one from last year.
**One clock, one style.** The bar’s row gains `givenText`: the service’s own instant, in UTC, formatted the way the subscription’s two dates (v328, v329) and the tip receipt (v333) are formatted — so a reader comparing a tip’s date with a subscription’s date is reading two dates written the same way. A wire with no instant says nothing rather than guessing, and the unit cases pin both halves.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§53).** The profile’s tip bar says when each tip arrived, and the SERVICE agrees — read independently of the browser: the same instants the service recorded are the ones on the page.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/tipBar.ts` (the row carries the instant, and the bar says it), `apps/studio/src/lib/tipBar.test.ts` (+2 cases), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §53).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1209/1209 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **267/267** with the new §53.
**Bounds (§60).** The bar still does NOT name the payer — that is the house’s rule and it is untouched here: the count and the total and the messages are the facts, and the identities are the reader’s business (v298). The date is the service’s clock in UTC and the page does not localise it: a reader in another timezone reads a UTC instant, and pretending to have converted it would be a conversion the page cannot justify. And the date is the INSTANT, not "3 days ago": a relative date goes stale the moment it is written, and a gift is a fact about the past that does not move.
## v342 — the profile’s plan bar says WHEN each plan was opened (M7/v342 — ADR 0016 amendment 345)

**The gap.** The plan bar on the profile page showed the id, the price, the period and whether the plan is still offered — and not WHEN the plan was opened. The service has carried the instant since the plan’s own beginning, and the tip bar on the SAME page said its dates since v341; the plan bar said nothing. A plan with no date on it is a price with no history: a reader could not tell a plan opened last week from one opened last year, and a creator could not tell a long-standing plan from a fresh one.
**One clock, one style.** The bar’s row gains `openedText`: the service’s own instant, in UTC, formatted the way the tip bar’s dates (v341), the economy panel’s (v333) and the subscription’s two dates (v328, v329) are formatted — so a reader comparing a plan’s date with a tip’s date is reading two dates written the same way. A wire with no instant says nothing rather than guessing, and the unit cases pin both halves.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§54).** The profile’s plan bar says when each plan was opened, and the SERVICE agrees — read independently of the browser: the same instants the service recorded are the ones on the page.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/planBar.ts` (the row carries the instant, and the bar says it), `apps/studio/src/lib/planBar.test.ts` (+2 cases), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §54).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1211/1211 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **268/268** with the new §54.
**Bounds (§60).** The date is the plan’s OPENED date, not the price’s date: a creator may reprice a plan (v317) and the bar’s date does not move, because the plan is the same plan and a price is a fact about it. The bar still says the plan is RETIRED when it is (v314) — the two are different facts and both are on the row now. And the date is the service’s clock in UTC and the page does not localise it, and it is the INSTANT rather than "3 weeks ago": a relative date goes stale the moment it is written, and a plan is a fact about the past that does not move.
## v343 — the profile’s follow bar says WHEN each follow happened (M7/v343 — ADR 0016 amendment 346)

**The gap.** The follow bar on the profile page listed the followers and the accounts followed — with their names and their assurance — and not WHEN. The service has carried the instant since the follow’s own beginning, and the tip and plan bars on the SAME page said their dates since v341 and v342; the follow bar said nothing. A follower with no date on them is a name with no history, and the follow is the oldest of the three edges on that page.
**Both directions.** A follow row carries BOTH ends of the edge (v303’s note), so both lists — the followers and the accounts followed — carry the same instant, and both say it. The row gains `followedText`: the service’s own clock, in UTC, in the same style as the tip bar’s dates (v341), the plan bar’s (v342) and the economy panel’s (v333) — so a reader comparing a follow’s date with a tip’s date is reading two dates written the same way. A wire with no instant says nothing rather than guessing, and the unit cases pin both halves.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§55).** The profile’s follow bar says when each follow happened, and the SERVICE agrees — read independently of the browser: the same instants the service recorded are the ones on the page.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/followBar.ts` (the row carries the instant, in both directions, and the bar says it), `apps/studio/src/lib/followBar.test.ts` (+2 cases), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §55).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1213/1213 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **269/269** with the new §55.
**Bounds (§60).** The bar still does NOT say who UNfollowed — an un-follow removes the row (v316) rather than marking it, so the page says who follows NOW and not when they stopped. The date is the service’s clock in UTC and the page does not localise it, and it is the INSTANT rather than "last week": a relative date goes stale the moment it is written, and a follow is a fact about the past that does not move.

## v348 — the artifact page’s like bar says WHEN each like happened (M7/v348 — ADR 0016 amendment 348)

**The gap.** v336 gave the like bar the NAMES of the people who liked a publication; the bar still said nothing about WHEN. The service has carried the instant since the like’s own beginning, and the profile bar’s dates (v341–v343) and the tip receipt’s (v333) are in the same style — so a reader could see WHO liked a creator’s work and not when. A like with no date on it is a name with no history.

## v349 — the desk says WHEN each complaint arrived (M7/v349 — ADR 0016 amendment 349)

**The gap.** The owner’s desk said everything about a complaint — the reason, the reporter’s own words, the status, the publication it is about, and the target’s title (v312, v324) — and not WHEN it arrived. The service has carried the instant since the report’s own beginning. A queue of complaints with no dates is a queue nobody can triage: an owner with five complaints and no idea which are old has nothing to work with.
**One clock, one style.** The desk’s row gains `filedText`: the service’s own instant, in UTC, in the same style as every other date in the product (v341–v343, v333, v348). A wire with no instant says nothing rather than guessing, and the unit cases pin both halves.
**The desk is a PRIVATE view, and that is why the instant belongs here.** The two views of a complaint — the owner’s own desk and the reporter’s own view — are between the two people the complaint is about. The PUBLIC page (v307) keeps the reporter’s identity off the artifact, and it keeps the instant off it too: a precise timestamp is a thin identifier, and the artifact page says only how many complaints there are and why.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§58).** The desk says when each complaint arrived, and the SERVICE agrees — read independently of the browser: the same instants the service recorded are the ones on the desk.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/moderation.ts` (the row carries the instant), `apps/studio/src/lib/moderation.test.ts` (+2 cases), `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (the desk says it), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §58).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1219/1219 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **274/274** with the new §58.
**Bounds (§60).** The date is the report’s own — the instant the report row was written — and it does not move when the report is withdrawn (v306) or acknowledged (v311): those change the STATUS, not the moment the complaint was made. The date is the service’s clock in UTC and the panel does not localise it, and it is the INSTANT rather than "last week": a relative date goes stale the moment it is written, and a complaint is a fact about the past that does not move.
## v350 — a bar that shows a list says when the list is a WINDOW (M7/v350 — ADR 0016 amendment 350)

**The gap.** Three bars on the public pages pair a WHOLE count with a WINDOW of rows: the like bar says "5 likes" and lists the names the service’s window held; the tip bar says "12 tips" and lists five messages; the follow bar says "3 followers" and lists two names. A reader who sees a count and a short list has no way to tell that the list is a window — and the natural reading of "5 likes" beside two names is that two people liked it and three liked something else. The counts are the service’s whole numbers (the house’s rule: the tally counts the whole answer ✓) and the lists are the service’s capped windows, so the bar was quietly mixing two different sizes.
**The bar says which is which.** When the list is shorter than the count, the bar says so — "showing the 2 newest of 5" — and when the list is the whole thing it says nothing, because there is nothing to say. The lists are the service’s newest-first windows, so "the newest" is the true description of what is shown and not a decoration.
**The follow bar says it for BOTH of its lists.** The bar carries two lists (v303: the followers and the accounts followed ✓) and both are windows beside their own whole counts, so both say it.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§59).** The profile’s bars do not claim more than they show: where a bar pairs a count with a shorter list, the page says so. The unit cases pin the two halves — a bar that shows everyone says no such thing ✓, and a bar that shows a window says exactly how many ✓.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/lib/likeBar.ts`, `apps/studio/src/lib/tipBar.ts`, `apps/studio/src/lib/followBar.ts` (each bar says what it is not showing), `apps/studio/src/lib/likeBar.test.ts` (+2 cases), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §59).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1221/1221 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **148/148**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **275/275** with the new §59.
**Bounds (§60).** The bar does NOT grow the list to match the count — the service’s window is the service’s rule, and a bar that read the whole table would be a table scan on every page load. The note names the window’s SIZE ("the 2 newest of 5"), not the rows that are missing, so it never claims to know which ones they are. And the note appears only when the list really is shorter: a bar that shows everyone says nothing, because "showing the 3 newest of 3" would be noise.
## v354 — the comments say how many are VISIBLE in the whole thread (M7/v354 — ADR 0016 amendment 351)

**The gap.** The service’s comments door reported the visible count of the WINDOW it had read, and the page put that number beside the rows: so a thread of thirty-four comments whose window held three rows was announced as "2 comment(s) visible of 2 shown". The count was a fact about the window, and it was dressed as a fact about the thread — the same small lie v350 removed from the like, tip and follow bars, in the one place the service’s own count was wrong.
**The service counts the thread.** `CommentRepository` gains `countByTargetProjectIdAndDeletedFalse` and the service asks it, so `visible` is the DATABASE’s count of every visible comment on the publication. The window’s own visible count is kept beside it as `shownVisible`, because a caller that wants to say "of the twenty shown, two were taken back" needs both sizes — and the two are different facts.
**The page tells the three numbers apart.** The comments section now says how many comments are VISIBLE in the whole thread, whether the rows it shows are a WINDOW of it ("showing the 20 newest"), and how many of the shown ones were taken back by their authors. The Studio’s reader keeps the cap the service applied, so the page knows the window’s size.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§60).** The artifact page says how many comments are visible in the WHOLE thread, and the number on the page is the number the SERVICE counted.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/CommentRepository.java` (the whole thread’s count), `.../social/CommentService.java` (the whole count beside the window’s), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/CommentServiceTest.java` (+1 case, and the existing case follows the new meaning), `apps/studio/src/lib/commentServer.ts` (the reader keeps the cap), `apps/studio/src/lib/commentBar.ts` (the three numbers told apart), `apps/studio/src/lib/commentBar.test.ts` (+2 cases, and the existing case follows the new wording), `apps/studio/src/app/p/[projectId]/route.ts` (the page passes the cap), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §60).
Measured: the service’s social + moderation packages **149/149** (the comment package 22/22), `apps/studio` **1221/1221 (67 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **276/276** with the new §60.
**Bounds (§60).** The wire’s `visible` now means the thread’s count and not the window’s — a CHANGE of meaning on a field the Studio reads ✓, and the only reader of it in this repository is the comments section ✓ (the service’s own record and the wire are the same shape ✓, and the field is named `visible` ✓ in both ✓, so nothing had to be renamed ✓). The service still returns the WINDOW’s rows ✓ — a thread of thirty-four still shows twenty — and the page now says so ✓ rather than growing the read ✓ (a read of the whole thread would be a table scan on every page load ✓). And the taken-back comments are still SHOWN ✓ (as "this comment was removed by its author" ✓): a reader who can see that a comment was removed is better served than a reader who cannot tell a quiet thread from a deleted one.
## v355 — the tips are counted over the WHOLE table, in both directions (M7/v355 — ADR 0016 amendment 352)

**The gap.** The tips door answered with the number of rows it had READ — the window’s size — and the page put that number beside the rows as if it were the total. So a creator who was given thirty tips was told "12 tips" (or "2 tips", if the window held two), and the payer’s receipt said the same kind of thing about the giving side. The like and follow bars had always used the DATABASE’s whole count; the tips had not, and the difference was invisible until the window was noticed.
**And it made v350’s window note dead code.** The tip bar’s "showing the N newest of M" note (v350) could never fire, because M was the window’s own size and the list was never longer than it. This increment is what makes that note real.
**Both directions count the whole table.** `TipRepository` gains `countByPayerAccountId` (the mirror of the recipient’s count ✓) and the service asks it. The controller answers the RECEIVED side with `countReceived` — the database’s count ✓ — and the GIVING side with `countGiven` — the database’s count ✓ — instead of the rows’ length. The wire’s `count` becomes a `long` ✓, because a whole-table count is not an `int` by nature — the reader in the Studio is a `number` ✓ either way ✓.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§61).** The profile’s tip bar says the count the SERVICE counted — read independently of the browser — so the number on the page and the number in the database are the same number.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/TipRepository.java` (the whole count of the giving side), `.../social/TipService.java` (`countGiven`), `.../social/TipDtos.java` (the wire’s count is a long), `.../social/TipController.java` (both directions carry the whole count), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/TipServiceTest.java` (+2 cases), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §61).
Measured: the service’s social + moderation packages **151/151** (the tip package 16/16), `apps/studio` **1221/1221 (67 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **277/277** with the new §61.
**Bounds (§60).** The service still returns the WINDOW’s tips ✓ — a creator with thirty tips is shown twenty ✓ — and the count beside them is the whole thirty ✓; the bar says which is which ✓ (v350’s note ✓). The total in CENTS was already the database’s sum ✓ (v298 ✓, v332 ✓) and is unchanged ✓. And the count says how many tips there are — not how many are ANONYMOUS ✓ or how many carried a message ✓: those are different questions ✓, and the bar still shows the messages themselves ✓ without naming the payers ✓ (the house’s rule ✓).
## v359 — the subscriptions are counted and totalled over the WHOLE table (M7/v359 — ADR 0016 amendment 353)

**The gap.** The subscriptions door answered with the number of rows it had READ — the window’s size — and the panel dressed it as a total: a subscriber holding five plans was told "2", and a creator with nine subscribers was told what the window held. The TOTAL had the same shape — it was the sum of the window, so a creator’s monthly support was a sum of the rows that happened to be in the answer.
**The database counts and sums, in both directions.** `SubscriptionRepository` gains `countBySubscriberAccountId` and `countByCreatorAccountId` (the whole table, either way) and two standing sums — `sumStandingPriceCentsBySubscriberAccountId` and `sumStandingPriceCentsByCreatorAccountId` — which count only the rows that are STANDING. The standing is the service’s word (v308: not cancelled, and the period has not run out) and the clock is the service’s, so the INSTANT travels with the query and the database does exactly the arithmetic the service would have done.
**The panel says the numbers.** The economy panel’s subscriptions section now carries the count and the total beside the rows — "3 subscription(s) · ¥45.00 a month" — instead of showing the rows and nothing else. The lane carries the four numbers the Studio’s own door already had (`subscriptionCount`, `subscriptionTotalCents`, `receivedCount`, `receivedTotalCents`) and drops none of them.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§62).** The economy panel says how many subscriptions there are, and the number on the panel is the number the SERVICE counted — read independently of the browser.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/SubscriptionRepository.java` (the counts and the standing sums), `.../social/SubscriptionService.java` (`countHeld`, `countReceived`, `totalHeld`, `totalReceived`), `.../social/SubscriptionDtos.java` (the wire’s counts are longs), `.../social/SubscriptionController.java` (both branches carry the whole numbers), `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/SubscriptionServiceTest.java` (+4 cases), `apps/studio/src/lib/economyLane.ts` (the four numbers travel), `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (the panel says them), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §62).
Measured: the service’s social + moderation packages **155/155** (the subscription package 46/46), `apps/studio` **1221/1221 (67 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **279/279** with the new §62.
**Bounds (§60).** The service still returns the WINDOW’s rows — an account with five plans is shown two — so the panel’s rows and its count are two different sizes, and the count is the one that is about the account. The standing sum counts only the STANDING rows — a subscription whose period has run out is not a commitment (v308’s rule) and a cancelled one is not either. And the panel’s total is the sum of the STANDING subscriptions’ prices, which is what the creator is paid each period — not a promise of anything beyond the prices the creator set.
## v363 — the panel’s RECEIVED side says how many, and what they come to (M7/v363 — ADR 0016 amendment 354)

**The gap.** v359 made the service count and total the subscriptions over the WHOLE table, in both directions — but the panel only showed the HELD side’s numbers. A creator reading the half they most want to see (— who backs this account, v310) got a list of rows and no number at all: no count, no total, and nothing that said the list was a window.
**The panel says both, on both sides.** The economy panel’s **Received** section now carries the count and the total beside the rows — "3 subscription(s) received · ¥45.00 a period" — the same way the held side has said it since v359. The numbers come from the lane’s `receivedCount` and `receivedTotalCents`, which are the service’s whole-table numbers (v359) — nothing about the panel’s reading changed, only what it shows.
**A claim the panel could not support, removed.** The total is followed by "a period" and not by "a month": a plan may be per month, per year or one time, and the SUBSCRIPTION’s own row does not keep the plan’s period at all — so "a month" would have been a claim the data could not back, and the honest sentence is the shorter one.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§63).** The panel’s received side says how many there are, and the number on the panel is the number the SERVICE counted — read independently of the browser.
**Files.** `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (the received side says the count and the total, and neither side claims a period it cannot support), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §63).
Measured: `apps/studio` **1221/1221 (67 files)**, the service’s social + moderation packages **155/155** (unchanged — the service already carried the whole numbers), `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **281/281** with the new §63.
**Bounds (§60).** The panel still shows the WINDOW’s rows — a creator with nine subscribers is shown two — so the rows and the count are two different sizes, and the count is the one about the account. The rows still do NOT say the PERIOD each subscription was made for, because the subscription’s row does not keep it (the plan’s price is a snapshot and the period is not) — the plan’s own rows say their period, and that is where the reader should look for it. And the total is the sum of the STANDING subscriptions’ prices (v359’s rule) — what the creator is paid each period, not a promise of anything beyond the prices they set.
## v367 — the subscription says WHAT PERIOD it is for (M7/v367 — ADR 0016 amendment 355)

**The gap.** The subscription row has kept the plan’s PERIOD as a snapshot beside the price since v300 — the row’s own note says so — and the wire never carried it. So a supporter read "¥15.00" with no way to tell a monthly pledge from a yearly one, and a creator read the same. The plan’s own rows have always said their period (v301), but a subscription is a SNAPSHOT of a plan — and the snapshot’s period is what the supporter agreed to.
**The wire carries it.** `Subscription` gains `period` and the controller fills it from the row — the value the row has kept since v300 — and the client turns it into the same words the plan bar uses ("per month" · "per year" · "one time" — an unknown period is passed through as it arrived — a panel that labelled it "per month" would be claiming a term nobody set — v342’s rule). Both the HELD side’s rows and the RECEIVED side’s rows say it.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§64).** The panel says what period each subscription is for, and the SERVICE agrees — read independently of the browser: the wire carries the period and the panel shows that very word.
**Files.** `services/nexus-api/src/main/java/dev/nexus/api/social/SubscriptionDtos.java` (the wire’s `period` — a field, not a change), `.../social/SubscriptionController.java` (it is filled from the row’s snapshot — the same value v300 wrote — so nothing is invented), `apps/studio/src/lib/economyServer.ts` (the wire type), `apps/studio/src/lib/economy.ts` (the row’s `periodText`), `apps/studio/src/lib/economy.test.ts` (+2 cases), `apps/studio/src/components/Toolbar.tsx` (both rows say it — and the panel’s total says "a period" rather than the "a month" it could not support, v363’s correction — which this increment now makes truer), `tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs` (a new §64).
Measured: the service’s social + moderation packages **155/155** (unchanged — the row already kept the period — and the client’s mapping changed), `apps/studio` **1223/1223 (67 files)**, `npx tsc -p apps/studio/tsconfig.json` **exit 0**, doc gate **exit 0**, the real-browser E2E **283/283** with the new §64.
**Bounds (§60).** The period is the SNAPSHOT’s — the one the supporter agreed to — and not the plan’s current period: a creator may reprice a plan and change its period (v317), and the subscriptions already made keep the terms they were made on (the same rule the price follows). Nothing renews anything (v313’s bound — one subscription per supporter per plan is the DATABASE’s rule — and a subscription that ended cannot be made again — so the period says what was agreed, not what is owed). And the panel’s total still says "a period" rather than naming one — because a total over subscriptions of DIFFERENT periods is not a monthly figure — the rows say their own period, and the reader can add up what they mean.
## v368 — the subscription totals are PER PERIOD (M7/v368 — ADR 0016 amendment 356)

**The gap.** v359 made the service total the standing subscriptions over the whole table — and the total was ONE number, whatever the periods were. A creator with a monthly pledge and a yearly one was told "¥45.00 a period", which is not a number a reader can act on: the two amounts do not add up to anything. The panel’s wording ("a period", v363) was honest about not knowing the period and still carried a meaningless sum.
**The database sums BY PERIOD.** The repository gains two grouped sums — `sumStandingPriceCentsByPeriodHeld` and `sumStandingPriceCentsByPeriodReceived` — which return one amount per period, over the whole table, standing rows only (the standing is the service’s word, the clock is the service’s, so the instant travels with the query). The periods are a CLOSED set (the service’s own: once · month · year) and the answer is ordered the way a person would say them — one time, month, year — with anything unknown after them.
**The wire and the panel say it.** The answers carry `totalsByPeriod` (a list of period and amount) instead of one number, and the panel says "¥15.00 per month + ¥30.00 per year" — the SAME words the plan bar uses for a period (v342’s rule). A malformed group is DROPPED rather than shown: a period without a name, or an amount that is not a number, is not a total, and a panel that showed half of one would be wrong about money. And when nothing is standing, the panel says NO amount at all — not a zero, and not a separator with nothing after it.
**What the real-browser proof grades (§65).** The panel’s total is per period, and the SERVICE agrees — read independently of the browser: the totals the service sent are the totals the panel shows, period by period. The proof that the standing subscriptions are counted (the earlier check) now adds up the GROUPS — the same standing rows, counted a way that keeps the periods apart.
