**The gap.** The zustand store has shipped since v132 and has never had a test file of its own: component/instance authoring (ADR 0015), the remix fork (v233) and the lineage stamp (v267) were graded only through the browser harnesses, which need a dev server and a real browser. The actions themselves are plain functions over a plain document — `get()`, a batch of ops, `set()` — so every rule in them was reachable headlessly and none of them was asked. A rule with no case is a rule the next refactor is free to break quietly — the same gap v401 and v402 found in the world-engine, in the one file the Studio’s editing actually runs on.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/store.test.ts` (17 cases) drives the store directly. `createComponent` moves the selected layers under a new master at the selection’s bounds, re-based onto the master’s origin, and leaves an instance where they stood; an empty selection, a locked layer and a layer that is not on the active page are each refused with the same sentence and no change. `insertInstance` places a new instance at the staggered spot, sized from the master, and refuses an unknown component by name. `detachInstance` replaces the instance with concrete copies — no `componentId`, no overrides, the text keeps its letters — at the instance’s own layout position, and refuses a selection that holds no instance. `setInstanceOverride` writes the patch onto the named master child and only there, in one op; width clamps at 1 and radius at 0 (a square corner is a value, not an absence); an empty patch on an already-empty override removes the key, while an empty patch on a real override leaves it; a node that is not an instance is refused in silence. `remixDocument` gives the fork a FRESH id so publishing it can never overwrite the source, writes the `_lineage` stamp the public pages read back, and starts an empty history — the source’s undo log is not inherited. And `instanceView` keeps its memoization contract: the same document object answers with the identical projection, a different document recomputes, and the projection names the instance itself as well as its clones.
Measured: apps/studio **1389/1389 (72 files)** (+17); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the store’s authoring rules graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the STORE’s own rules — the canvas, the panels and the real-browser harnesses stay where they were graded (golden-path, world, assets). `generate`/`editSelection` are deliberately not graded here: they go through the DEMO-MODE router and are the composer’s subject, not the store’s. The suite is a singleton store, so each case reloads a fresh document through `loadDocument` — itself one of the graded rules. The browser-only surfaces (drag, on-canvas text edit, the 3D and World panes) remain harness work, unchanged by this file.

## the TIPS door’s own answers, graded directly (M7/v404 — ADR 0016 amendment 397)

**The gap.** v399 and v400 gave the comments and likes doors their own direct cases; the tips door — the social act that carries an AMOUNT, written since v298 — still had none. Its two readers page by rows (v391) and its writer carries the viewer’s claim (v298), and none of those contracts was asked directly: a window that stopped being echoed, or a refusal that started reading as an empty day, would have been caught by no unit and graded only by a browser harness that cannot run here.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/tipServer.test.ts` (18 cases) drives the door with an injected fetch. `readTips`: the ask names the recipient with limit and offset rows, a negative offset is clamped to the first window rather than refused, and the answer echoes the window it is FOR — or keeps the one that was asked when the body carries none; an HTTP refusal names its status, a body with no tips array is a refusal rather than an empty list, a non-JSON body is a refusal with a reason, and a call that never answers is an unreachable reason rather than a hang. `readTipsGiven`: the same door from the PAYER’s side, and the two readers’ real differences are pinned rather than smoothed over — this one reads `ok:false` bodies for their message (v313: the service’s error body carries no `ok` field, so the old shape threw away every message the service ever wrote), and it counts ZERO when the body carries no count where the received reader falls back to the rows it carried. `tipOnService`: the POST body carries the claim exactly as the browser built it, with an absent handle, assurance or instant travelling as null rather than undefined, and a refusal answers with a reason and NO tip — a refused tip is not a tip that found nobody.
Measured: apps/studio **1407/1407 (73 files)** (+18); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the tips door graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CLIENT door’s contracts — the service’s own tip rules (the amount floor, the per-recipient identity) stay where they are graded (TipServiceTest). The injected fetch is a stub: the real service’s statuses and bodies are graded by the harness and the service suite, not here. And the two readers’ count asymmetry is recorded as it stands; if the service ever gives the GIVEN reader an explicit count, this file is where the change would be made honestly.

## the REPORTS door’s own answers, graded directly (M7/v405 — ADR 0016 amendment 398)

**The gap.** v399, v400 and v404 gave the comments, likes and tips doors their own direct cases; the reports door — the social act that asks for help, written since v296 — still had none. Its two readers (the reports ABOUT a publication, the reports ONE ACCOUNT filed), its withdrawer and its writer each carry a contract the studio pages depend on, and none of them was asked directly.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/reportServer.test.ts` (21 cases) drives the door with an injected fetch. `readReports`: the ask names the target and the cap, the count falls back to the rows carried rather than to zero, and a refusal is never an empty list — an HTTP refusal names its status, a body with no reports array is a refusal, a non-JSON body is a refusal with a reason, and a call that never answers is an unreachable reason. `readFiledReports`: the ask names the reporter with the window as rows, the answer echoes the window it is FOR, the count is the WHOLE filed list while the rows are the window (v380 — both true at once), and a refusal still names the window it was asked for. `withdrawReport`: the DELETE names the claimer, and the service’s 200-either-way answer is read honestly — an already-withdrawn report is a FACT (`withdrawn: false` with no reason), not an error. `reportOnService`: the POST body carries the claim exactly as the browser built it, an absent handle, assurance or instant travelling as null rather than undefined, and a refusal answers with a reason and NO report.
Measured: apps/studio **1428/1428 (74 files)** (+21); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the reports door graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CLIENT door’s contracts — the service’s own report rules (the reason vocabulary, the one-standing-report-per-target rule, who may withdraw) stay where they are graded (ReportServiceTest, ModerationServiceTest). The injected fetch is a stub; the real service’s statuses and bodies are graded by the harness and the service suite. And the wire shape carries NO reporter identity on a target’s reports (v307) — that is the service’s answer shape, pinned here as the reason a client never expects a name.

## the ECONOMY readers’ own answers, graded directly (M7/v406 — ADR 0016 amendment 399)

**The gap.** v399, v400, v404 and v405 gave the comments, likes, tips and reports doors their own direct cases; the economy readers — what one account BACKS, who backs one creator, what one creator OFFERS — still had none. Three reads go out together from the economy panel and answer in whatever order the network chooses, so each answer’s window echo is load-bearing (v382/v392), and the plans reader carries v373’s hardest rule: a count is either the whole table or UNKNOWN.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/economyServer.test.ts` (14 cases) drives all three readers with an injected fetch. The window: the ask names subscriber / creator / creator with limit and offset rows, a negative offset is clamped to the first window rather than refused, and the answer echoes the window it is FOR. The plans count: a wire with NO count answers `null` — never the window’s length, because counting what happened to be on the screen and calling it the creator’s whole catalogue is the exact lie v373 removed; the subscriptions readers fall back to the rows they carried, and that asymmetry is graded on both sides. The per-period totals are FILTERED, not trusted: a group with an empty period or currency, a non-finite amount, a missing amount or a non-object entry is dropped, and a body with no totals answers an empty list rather than a crash. And every refusal is named — the service’s own message when there is one, the HTTP status when there is not, “not JSON” for a body that is not one, and an unreachable reason for a call that never answers — because “nobody backs this” and “the economy could not be read” are different facts.
Measured: apps/studio **1442/1442 (75 files)** (+14); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the economy readers graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CLIENT readers’ contracts — the service’s own economy rules (the amount floor, the period vocabulary, the standing-only totals) stay where they are graded (SubscriptionServiceTest, PlanServiceTest). The injected fetch is a stub; the real service’s statuses and bodies are graded by the harness and the service suite. And the WRITES on this lane (subscribe, plan create) have no client door in this build — ADR 0018 — so only the readers exist to grade.

## the DESK door’s own answers, graded directly (M7/v407 — ADR 0016 amendment 400)

**The gap.** v399, v400, v404, v405 and v406 gave the comments, likes, tips, reports and economy doors their own direct cases; the moderation desk’s three doors — the owner’s report list, the tally, the acknowledge — still had none. The desk is where the whole moderation story lands for a creator, and its contracts were graded only by the audit proof and the browser harness.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/moderationServer.test.ts` (18 cases) drives all three doors with an injected fetch. `readDesk`: the ask names the owner and the cap, the reason travels TRIMMED and only when it is not blank (a blank reason is not a filter — it is the absence of one), a negative offset is clamped to the first window the way the service clamps it, and every refusal is named. `readTally`: the counts and both breakdowns are read, missing breakdowns answer empty maps rather than undefined, and a body missing its totals is a REFUSAL rather than a row of zeros — “nobody reported this” and “the counts could not be read” are different facts. `acknowledgeOnService`: the POST goes to the report’s own door with the owner named, the answer’s reportId falls back to the one that was asked about, `moved` is read STRICTLY (a truthy non-boolean is not a move), and an answer that moved nothing says so without being an error.
Measured: apps/studio **1460/1460 (76 files)** (+18); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the desk graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CLIENT doors’ contracts — the service’s own moderation rules (the reason vocabulary, who may acknowledge, the tally’s whole-table scope) stay where they are graded (ModerationServiceTest, the audit contract proof). The injected fetch is a stub; the real service’s statuses and bodies are graded by the harness and the service suite. And the desk’s window echo is deliberately thinner than the tips door’s: the answer carries the cap the service applied and no offset of its own, because the panel’s page lives in the URL the route built (v389) — a fact about the desk’s shape, pinned here so a future offset echo is a decision.

## the INTENT readers’ own rule, graded directly (M7/v408 — ADR 0016 amendment 401)

**The gap.** Nine pure readers — the comment, like, follow, subscribe, report and tip verbs and their four un-verbs — decide what the Studio DOES when a public page links a reader into it, and not one of them had a case. Each is a one-liner, which is exactly why they drifted ungraded: the rule is small enough to state exactly and small enough to break quietly. A reader that answered an empty id, or one verb’s parameter answering another verb’s question, would have opened a composer over nothing.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/intents.test.ts` (54 cases) grades all nine readers against the same six rules. The id is read from ONE named key, with or without the leading question mark, and from among other parameters. An absent, empty or whitespace-only value is NO intent rather than an empty id — the blank check trims. And the edge the readers actually carry, pinned rather than smoothed: the check trims but the ANSWER does not, so a padded id is returned as it stands — the reader’s job is to say what the URL named, not to repair it, and a future trim is now a decision. An encoded id is decoded, the FIRST value wins when a key repeats, and no reader answers for another verb’s parameter — the un-verbs read their own keys, not the base verb’s.
Measured: apps/studio **1514/1514 (77 files)** (+54); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — every intent reader graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the READERS’ contracts — what the Studio DOES with an intent (open a composer, act on mount, drop the query once used) stays where it is graded (the route and the harnesses). The readers are pure by construction (their own doc-comments say so), so there is no service, no fetch and no browser in this file. And the padded-id behaviour is pinned as it stands; if a future verb needs the id trimmed, that is a change to all nine readers at once, made on purpose.

## the GALLERY builder’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v409 — ADR 0016 amendment 402)

**The gap.** The gallery is shared by the explore page and the profile page, and it exists so the per-row fan-out (a second read for the document, a thumbnail drawn from it) is written ONCE rather than twice and drifting — and neither of its two functions had a case. `firstPageId` is the defensive read that decides whether there is anything to draw at all, and `buildGalleryItems` keeps the rule the module’s own doc-comment states: a row whose document cannot be read is shown WITH ITS REASON, never as a blank card.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/gallery.test.ts` (12 cases) drives both. `firstPageId` answers null for anything that is not a document at all, for a missing or non-string active page id, for a document with no nodes map, and for a STALE active page id that names no node — the last one matters because a stale id is not a page, and drawing against it would be a lie about the document. `buildGalleryItems`: each row is read once, in order, by its project id; a document with a live page is drawn and the card carries no note; a read that failed shows the read’s OWN reason, and a read with no reason still says “preview unavailable” rather than nothing; a document with no drawable page (or none at all) says “nothing to draw” — the card’s own fact, not a failure — and the drawer is never called for it; an empty listing reads nothing at all. And the card keeps the row’s facts and the lineage the document carries, so the shared builder hands the renderer exactly what the two pages need.
Measured: apps/studio **1526/1526 (78 files)** (+12); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the gallery graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the BUILDER’s contracts — the card’s own mapping (`toExploreItem`) and the HTML (`renderExploreHtml`) stay where they are graded (explore.test.ts), and the real service’s document answers stay with the harness. `read` and `draw` are injected stubs here: the module’s freedom from the service’s address and the renderer is the design, and the test keeps it that way. The thumbnail is whatever `draw` answers — the bytes are the painter’s subject (`preview.ts`), not the gallery’s.

## the PREVIEW renderer’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v410 — ADR 0016 amendment 403)

**The gap.** The artifact page and the gallery card both render a published document through `renderPreviewHtml`, and every value it writes comes from JSON a client posted — a publication is stored verbatim (v294 read a malformed style back out of the store to prove it). The renderer’s own comment names the nine document-derived fields that could end an attribute or close the style block, and the artifact tests exercise the renderer THROUGH the page — so the escaping itself, the module’s whole reason for the composed-attribute rule, was nobody’s direct subject.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/preview.test.ts` (13 cases) drives the renderer directly. Text characters are HTML-escaped — a publication cannot write markup through its words. A token value carrying a quote cannot end the style attribute: the composed attribute is escaped WHOLE, so a field added later cannot be forgotten. The `:root` block is CSS-escaped rather than HTML-escaped — CSS does not decode entities, so `<` travels as `\3c ` and the value survives as one character while the block stays closed. And a token NAME is escaped like its value: a key of `x}</style><img src=y>` cannot reach the block as a rule. The shape rules too: the stage is the page’s size from the layout, a rect is an absolutely positioned box at its layout rect, an ellipse is a 50% radius, a turned node carries its rotation, a text node carries the full shorthand, an image is the honest placeholder, an invisible node is not drawn, an unknown page id is an empty stage rather than a crash — and an instance’s bodies are PROJECTED, the clone drawn and not just the instance box, which is the line whose absence once made the artifact draw one div where the canvas drew two.
Measured: apps/studio **1539/1539 (79 files)** (+13); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the renderer graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the RENDERER’s rules — the artifact page’s own chrome (byline, remix link, the two failure pages) stays where it is graded (artifact.test.ts), and the clip-path divergence for a turned child inside a turned frame is pinned there and in the module’s comment, not re-pinned here. The token VALUES are escaped as written; what a well-formed token looks like is the editor’s subject, not the renderer’s. And the escape is inert for every drawing the canvas itself could make — a well-formed CSS value holds none of `& < > " '\` — which is why the tests use deliberately hostile values.

## the PAINT resolver’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v411 — ADR 0016 amendment 404)

**The gap.** `paint.ts` is the seam every renderer shares: the canvas puts its answers on a context, the artifact writes them into a `style` attribute, the thumbnail into SVG attributes. Its own comment names the reason the coercion lives here — a value that is really `'bold" onload="x'` would take three shapes of damage from one malformed style — and not one of those defenses had a case. A published document is JSON a client posted, so the malformed inputs are not hypothetical; they are the population.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/paint.test.ts` (13 cases) asks the resolver directly. The four paint kinds each answer in their own shape: a solid and a stroke are their rgba rounded to 8-bit channels, a token ref resolves through the document’s own tokens (a hex string passes through, an RGBA object is normalized), a linear gradient lists its stops in order with positions rounded to whole percents, and an image fill is the labeled placeholder rather than a broken url. A token the document does not have is a NAMED grey — not transparent, not a crash — and `tokenColorCss` answers its fallback (the default and a caller’s own) for the same absence. And the malformed-style defenses, which are the point of the module: a reference that is not a path resolves to nothing (family falls back to `Inter, sans-serif`, size to 16), a number that is not finite falls back rather than reaching a renderer as NaN, and an align that is not one of the three the model owns reads as `left` — one malformed style cannot take the gallery page down, and now that is graded rather than asserted in a comment.
Measured: apps/studio **1552/1552 (80 files)** (+13); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the resolver graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the RESOLVER’s contracts — what a well-formed token LOOKS like is the editor’s subject, and the three renderers that consume these answers keep their own tests (canvas, artifact, thumbnail). The gradient’s angle is passed through as authored; a hostile angle is a style question, not a paint one. And the fallbacks are the module’s own numbers (16px, 400, 1.2, 0, `Inter, sans-serif`, the grey) — pinned so a tuning of any of them is a decision rather than a drift.
## the shared ESCAPER’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v412 — ADR 0016 amendment 405)

**The gap.** `escapeHtml` in `pageShell.ts` is the ONE escaper every server-rendered Studio page shares — drawn out of artifact.ts at v241 precisely because two copies of the rule would be two answers to “is this safe”. Every value it writes comes from JSON a client posted, and the module’s own comment names the ORDER as the load-bearing property: the ampersand runs first, so the entities the later rules introduce are never re-escaped. The pages were graded through their own tests, but the shared rule underneath them had no direct subject.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/pageShell.test.ts` (5 cases) asks the escaper directly. The five characters that can end an attribute or a text node each answer their own entity. The ORDER is pinned: `&` runs first, so `<b>` answers `&lt;b&gt;` and not `&amp;lt;` — an escaper that double-escapes is not safer, it is a different answer to the same question. An already-escaped string is escaped AGAIN: the contract is one escape of raw text, so a double-write does not silently decode back into markup. An attribute-breaking payload — `" onmouseover="alert(1)` — cannot break out; no quote survives in the answer. And ordinary text — letters, digits, punctuation that is not one of the five, the empty string — travels untouched.
Measured: apps/studio **1557/1557 (81 files)** (+5); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the escaper graded directly, and the whole studio suite still green.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the ESCAPER’s contract — what each page writes through it (the artifact’s byline and remix link, the explore and gallery pages) stays where it is graded (their own page tests), and the preview renderer’s CSS escaping is a different spelling for a different consumer, pinned in preview.test.ts rather than re-pinned here. The five characters are the whole rule; a future consumer that needs a sixth escaped is a change to every page at once, made on purpose.


## the CLAIM builder’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v413 — ADR 0016 amendment 406)

**The gap.** `claimOf` is the ONE builder of the claim every social verb travels with — the like, comment, follow, tip, report and subscribe composers all call it, because a claim six modules build slightly differently is six shapes disagreeing about the same account (v288). The module’s comment states the rules plainly — no account means no claim, the client does not invent one — and the service refuses an accountless social act BY NAME (v283/v285/v287), yet the builder itself had no direct case: its behaviour was only ever exercised through the composers and their tests.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/socialClaim.test.ts` (11 cases) asks the builder directly. A device with no account has NO claim — null, empty and whitespace-only ids all answer null, because an invented account id is an action nobody took. Only the ID gates the claim: an account with neither handle nor assurance still carries one. The id, handle and assurance each travel TRIMMED, and a blank handle or assurance answers null rather than an empty string — an absent field says so by name. And `claimedAt` is ALWAYS null on this side: the interface allows a number so the SERVICE can carry one, but the browser’s builder never stamps a time, so a claim says when the service saw the act rather than when the browser decided to make it.
Measured: apps/studio `socialClaim.test.ts` **11/11** (+11); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the builder graded directly; full-suite baseline 1557/1557 (81 files, v412) held per the scoped-gate policy (AGENTS.md), suite arithmetically 1568/1568 (82 files).
**Bounds (§60).** These are the BUILDER’s contracts — the service’s own rules about a claim (that it records but never verifies, that `unsigned-local` is the only assurance it accepts) stay where they are graded (the service suite, ADR 0018). The six composers’ behaviour when the claim is null — refusing by name rather than acting anonymously — is theirs, graded in their own tests. And `claimedAt: null` is the CLIENT side of a two-sided fact: the routes read a posted number off the wire when there is one, so the field is nullable by design, not by oversight.


## the SLUG namer’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v414 — ADR 0016 amendment 407)

**The gap.** Three exports share one namer — `exportJson`, `exportHtml` and `exportCanvasPng` all end in `${slug(name)}-…` — and the slug itself had no case. The rule is three rewrites deep (lowercase, every run of non-alphanumerics to one hyphen, no leading or trailing hyphen), and its interesting edges are what it does NOT do: it does not rescue a name that reduces to nothing, and it does not transliterate. A download’s filename is the first part of an export a person sees, and it was graded by nobody.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/export.test.ts` (11 cases) asks the namer directly. An empty name answers the fallback `nexus` — a document nobody named still downloads — and the check is falsiness, not emptiness, so any falsy answers the same. A spaced name lowercases; a RUN of non-alphanumerics collapses to ONE hyphen rather than many; leading and trailing separators are stripped; digits and the fallback itself survive the rule unchanged. And the honest edge, pinned rather than smoothed: a name with no ASCII alphanumerics — `你好`, `---`, a lone space — is truthy, so the fallback never fires and the rewrites leave the EMPTY string. The download is then `-snapshot.json`, and that is now a stated fact rather than a surprise. The rule is also idempotent — slugging a slug changes nothing.
Measured: apps/studio `export.test.ts` **11/11** (+11); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the namer graded directly; full-suite baseline 1557/1557 (81 files, v412) held per the scoped-gate policy (AGENTS.md), suite arithmetically 1579/1579 (83 files).
**Bounds (§60).** These are the NAMER’s rules — the three exports themselves (the JSON snapshot’s shape, the HTML’s provenance, the PNG’s capture) stay where they are graded. And the DOM surface (Blob, object URLs, the anchor click) needs a browser the node test environment does not provide, so the download MECHANISM is the harness’s subject, not this file’s. The empty-slug edge is a filename fact, not a correctness claim: `-snapshot.json` is an ugly name, not a broken download.


## the browser-side DESK lane’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v415 — ADR 0016 amendment 408)

**The gap.** `moderationLane.ts` is the ONLY place the browser’s side of the owner’s desk is written: the service’s address is server-side, so the panel talks to the Studio’s own `/api/moderation` and the route forwards. Its server-side twin was graded directly at v407 — and the browser half, the seam the panel actually stands on, had no case of its own. Three of its rules are exactly the kind that drift ungraded: a negative offset is the first window rather than a refusal, a tally that could not be read must not read as zero, and a disposition says whether the status MOVED.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/moderationLane.test.ts` (16 cases) drives both doors with an injected fetch. `readDesk`: the ask names the owner and the window (a negative offset clamped to the first, a deeper one travelling whole), the reason travels TRIMMED and only when it is not blank, the rows are mapped through the shared desk-row rule (a malformed entry is dropped, not crashed on), `open` counts only the rows still waiting, the cap is echoed with 20 as the lane’s own default, and every refusal is named — the service’s reason, the route’s message, the HTTP status, a non-JSON body, a thrown fetch. The tally travels whole when the service gave one and answers null when it did not: “nobody complained” and “the counts could not be read” are different facts. `acknowledgeReport`: the POST names the owner and carries the report in the body, `moved` is read STRICTLY (a truthy non-boolean is not a move), and the status travels only when it is a string.
Measured: apps/studio `moderationLane.test.ts` **16/16** (+16); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the lane graded directly; full-suite baseline 1557/1557 (81 files, v412) held per the scoped-gate policy (AGENTS.md), suite arithmetically 1595/1595 (84 files).
**Bounds (§60).** These are the LANE’s contracts — the service’s own moderation rules (the reason vocabulary, who may acknowledge, the tally’s whole-table scope) stay where they are graded (ModerationServiceTest, the audit contract proof), and the server-side half of this same door keeps its own file (moderationServer.test.ts, v407). The injected fetch is a stub; the real route’s statuses and bodies are graded by the harness and the route’s own tests. And the lane deliberately does NOT send a signal on the read the way it does on the write: a hung READ is the route’s to time out, and a write’s 45-second budget (the first-call compile) is pinned in the module, not re-pinned here.


## the storage I/O seam’s own contracts, graded directly (M7/v416 — ADR 0016 amendment 409)

**The gap.** `persist.ts` is pure and window-free by design, which makes `storage.ts` the ONLY module in the Studio that touches `window` (ADR 0002 testability) — and the seam itself had no case. Its contracts are small and load-bearing: the two fakes must answer the KvStore shape exactly (a missing key is null, an empty string is a VALUE), the browser resolution must DEGRADE rather than dereference, and the one key every saved document lives under is a fact about every device that ever saved one.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/storage.test.ts` (10 cases) asks the seam directly. `nullStore` forgets everything it is given — every write is a no-op, every read answers null, and nothing throws. `memoryStore` round-trips a written value, a second write replaces the first, a removed key reads null, removing a missing key is a no-op, an EMPTY STRING is a stored value rather than an absence (the `?? null` must not eat it), and the seed is a SNAPSHOT — a later change to the caller’s record does not reach into the store. `STUDIO_DOC_KEY` is pinned as the stable namespaced key: a rename would orphan every document on every device, so it is a decision, not a string. And `browserStorage` is graded under three windows: none at all (SSR, headless) degrades to nullStore; a localStorage that THROWS on the probe (private mode, blocked) degrades the same way; a working one is used and CACHED, so the write-probe runs once rather than on every save.
Measured: apps/studio `storage.test.ts` **10/10** (+10); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the seam graded directly; full-suite baseline 1557/1557 (81 files, v412) held per the scoped-gate policy (AGENTS.md), suite arithmetically 1605/1605 (85 files).
**Bounds (§60).** These are the SEAM’s contracts — what `persist.ts` does with the stored string (the envelope, the version checks, the graph validation) stays where it is graded (persist.test.ts), and the app’s choice to persist the whole document under this one key is the Studio’s subject. The cache is module-level BY DESIGN: a browser has one localStorage for the page’s lifetime, and re-probing per call would re-run the write probe on every save — the price, pinned by the ordering note in the test file, is that a test cannot un-cache a store it installed. And the probe writes and removes one transient key (`__nexus_probe__`); a real localStorage sees it for an instant, which is the whole cost of knowing the store is writable.


## the deploy lane adapter’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v417 — ADR 0016 amendment 410)

**The gap.** `deployLane.ts` is the CLIENT half of the dock’s deploy face — a fetch adapter and nothing more (M6 v177) — and it had no case of its own. Two rules are its whole character: unavailability is a NORMAL answer rather than an error (a probe that cannot reach the studio server answers a named unavailability with every field at its empty default and never throws into the panel), and the browser names a GOAL — never argv, never a fact. The module calls the global `fetch` directly (it predates the injected-fetch lanes), which is precisely why it drifted ungraded: there was no seam to inject through, so no test reached it.
**The proof.** `apps/studio/src/lib/deployLane.test.ts` (10 cases) stubs the global and restores it. The probe passes a 200 answer through untouched — the route owns the shape and the lane re-maps nothing — and asks with `cache: 'no-store'`, because a stale availability answer is a lie about the server. A non-ok probe is a NAMED unavailability (`probe route answered <status>`) with every field at its empty default, and a thrown fetch is caught the same way (`studio server unreachable: …`). The run POSTs the goal as ONE JSON field; NO goal means no body and no content-type — the ask is the route itself; and an EMPTY-string goal still travels, so the server refuses by name rather than the client inventing “no goal”. A JSON object answer passes through as the lane answer — including an HTTP error whose body still speaks, because the route’s own verdict is the answer — while a non-JSON body is a named refusal and a thrown fetch is caught.
Measured: apps/studio `deployLane.test.ts` **10/10** (+10); `tsc --noEmit` exit 0 — the adapter graded directly; full-suite baseline 1557/1557 (81 files, v412) held per the scoped-gate policy (AGENTS.md), suite arithmetically 1615/1615 (86 files).
**Bounds (§60).** These are the ADAPTER’s rules — the route’s own answers (the probe’s shape, the arc walk, the deploy receipts) stay where they are graded (deployServer.test.ts, the agent-runtime and exec-host suites), and the server-side half of the deploy door is the route’s subject. The global-`fetch` stub is a test seam, not a design change: the module predates the injected-fetch lanes and rewriting it is a separate decision. And “unavailability is a normal answer” stops at the adapter — what the PANEL shows for an unavailable lane is the component’s subject, not this file’s.


## the comments HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v418 — ADR 0016 amendment 411)

**The gap.** Every service in `nexus-api` had its own direct cases; every CONTROLLER had none. The controller owns only HTTP, but “only HTTP” is where the status codes that carry the difference are decided — 400 for a request that broke a rule, 403 for one that was understood and refused — and where the window arithmetic and the answer shapes are pinned before the ask goes out. `CommentController` was the first to be graded: the comments door is the one with the most surface (three directions, two exception translations, a window on one direction only).
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/CommentControllerTest.java` (13 cases) drives it with Mockito, no Spring context, the same rule the service tests follow. A written comment answers 201 with the row, the count and the DEFAULT cap; a null request body is asked as nulls rather than thrown into an unmapped 500 — the SERVICE decides what a null body means. The DELETE door builds its claim out of the query parameter with the account id and NOTHING else (no handle, no assurance, no instant — a URL names one thing), answers 200 whether the comment moved or not, and asks the service with no claim at all when no claimer was named. The thread direction clamps before it asks: a cap over the max is the max, a non-positive cap is the default, a negative offset is the first window — and the answer carries the window it answered (cap, offset, the ROW count). The author direction asks with the RAW cap (the service’s own clamp decides) and answers with the visible count beside the list and no window at all. A question that names neither project nor author is refused by name; a broken rule is a 400 that names it; somebody else’s comment is a 403, because the request was well-formed and a 400 would tell the caller to fix a field that is fine.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=CommentControllerTest` → **13/13, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — the comment rules themselves (who may comment, what a body must be, who may take back) stay where they are graded (CommentServiceTest), and the wiring (that `@RequestBody` binds JSON, that `@ExceptionHandler` maps a thrown refusal to its status) is Spring’s, graded by the contract proofs that drive the real jar. The claim the DELETE door builds is the query parameter’s account id and nothing else because a URL names one thing; a future door that needs more must take it from somewhere it can name. And the author direction’s unclamped cap is deliberate: one clamp, in the service, for every door — the controller echoing what it asked is how the answer stays honest about the window.


## the likes HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v419 — ADR 0016 amendment 412)

**The gap.** The likes door is the second controller to be graded directly, and it owns a rule the comments door does not: the STATUS a like answers with. 201 when the like was NEW, 200 when it was already there — the same distinction the publish receipt makes, visible to a caller without reading the body. The controller computes it from the service’s answer as `count() > 0 && liked()`, which is two facts and not one, and nothing graded that reading. The list’s window arithmetic and the DELETE door’s one-field claim were equally ungraded.
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/LikeControllerTest.java` (11 cases) drives it with Mockito, no Spring context. A new like answers 201 with the count AFTER the like (so a caller never adds one itself and gets it wrong); an already-there like answers 200 with the same count — liked twice is not an error. And the guard is pinned as written: a `liked` with a count of zero is NOT a 201, because a like that did not land is not a like that was written. A null request body is asked as nulls rather than thrown into an unmapped 500. The DELETE door builds its claim out of the query parameter with the account id and NOTHING else, and asks with no claim at all when no claimer was named. The list clamps before it asks (over-max is the max, non-positive is the default, negative offset is the first window), travels the viewer with the ask, and answers with the whole count beside the window it came from — the count does not depend on who is asking, and the `liked` flag is the only thing the viewer decides. A broken rule is a 400 that names it.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=LikeControllerTest` → **11/11, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — the like rules themselves (an anonymous like cannot be counted honestly, the second like’s same count) stay where they are graded (LikeServiceTest), and the Spring wiring is graded by the contract proofs that drive the real jar. The 201/200 reading is pinned EXACTLY as the controller computes it — `count() > 0 && liked()` — so a future change to either half of that condition is a decision rather than a drift. And the viewer travels as a bare account id: the door does not authenticate it, the same honesty the whole claim story keeps (ADR 0018) — a `liked` flag is what the service reads, not who the reader is.


## the follows HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v420 — ADR 0016 amendment 413)

**The gap.** The follows door is the first controller with TWO read directions on one door: `follower=` asks “who do I watch” and `accountId=` asks “who watches me”, and one query cannot answer both. The routing between them, each direction’s window arithmetic, and the shape each answers in were ungraded — and the door carries two honest edges nobody had pinned: the POST answers 201 for a REPEAT follow too, although the door’s own comment promises “200 when it was already there”; and the missing-parameter refusal is answered by the controller ITSELF rather than thrown, because an ask with no parameter is the caller’s to fix.
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/FollowControllerTest.java` (11 cases) drives it with Mockito, no Spring context. The POST answers 201 pinned AS THE CODE READS — a repeat follow is a 201 too — with the divergence from the comment stated in the test rather than smoothed, so making the comment true is a decision somebody takes on purpose. A null body is asked as nulls; the DELETE door builds its claim out of the query parameter with the account id and nothing else. The follower direction asks `followingOf` with the clamped window (over-max is the max, non-positive is the default, negative offset is the first window) and its answer echoes the CAP but not the start row — that window is a size, not a page. The account direction asks `followersOf` with the viewer travelling beside the ask, and its answer carries both halves of the window beside the whole count. A BLANK follower falls through to the account direction rather than being an empty following question. A question that names neither is a 400 the controller answers itself, and its message names BOTH doors so the fix is findable.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=FollowControllerTest` → **11/11, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — the follow rules themselves (who may follow, the counts’ meaning) stay where they are graded (FollowServiceTest), and the Spring wiring is graded by the contract proofs that drive the real jar. The 201-for-a-repeat pin is a statement about TODAY’s code, not an endorsement: the likes door distinguishes 201/200 on the same question (v419), and the two doors disagreeing is now visible in two graded files rather than hidden in two comments. And the following answer’s missing offset is the shape it ships; a pager that needs the start row there is a change to the record, made on purpose.


## the tips HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v421 — ADR 0016 amendment 414)

**The gap.** The tips door is the fourth controller graded directly, and its own decisions are unusual in two ways. It CLAMPS THE OFFSET BUT NOT THE CAP: a cap of 500 travels to the service as 500, because the service’s own clamp is the one clamp every door goes through — the opposite choice from the likes and follows doors, which clamp in the controller and echo what they applied. And the write door’s null-guards are part of the contract: a missing amount is ZERO rather than a null reaching the service as a `Long`, and a missing `anonymous` is false by `Boolean.TRUE.equals` rather than by a null check that would NPE. None of it had a case.
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/TipControllerTest.java` (11 cases) drives it with Mockito, no Spring context. A tip answers 201 with the row, the amount, a count of one and the DEFAULT cap. A missing amount is asked as `0L` and a missing anonymous as `false` — the service refuses “more than nothing” BY NAME, which is a rule, where a null reaching it would be a crash; a TRUE anonymous travels as true. A null body is asked as nulls. The recipient direction asks with the RAW cap (500 stays 500, zero stays zero — the controller does not second-guess the one clamp), clamps only the offset, and echoes what was ASKED on the answer, with the DATABASE’s total and the WHOLE count beside the window. The payer direction asks the given side with the given totals, and a blank recipient falls through to it rather than being an empty received question. A question that names neither is refused by name.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=TipControllerTest` → **11/11, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — the tip rules themselves (what an amount must be, who may tip) stay where they are graded (TipServiceTest), and the Spring wiring is graded by the contract proofs. The raw-cap choice is pinned as the tips door’s OWN: likes and follows clamp in the controller (v419, v420), and three doors now state their clamp policy in graded files instead of leaving it to whoever reads the code next. And the totals are the service’s numbers by name — this file grades that they TRAVEL, not that the database summed them right.


## the reports HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v422 — ADR 0016 amendment 415)

**The gap.** The reports door is the fifth controller graded directly, and it carries the sharpest asymmetry of them all: the FILED direction pages and the TARGET direction does not — an offset asked on the target direction is ignored entirely, because the target finder takes no offset at all. Two more edges were ungraded: the filed answer TRIMS the reporter it echoes while the ask travels the raw string, and the wire shape carries no reporter identity whatsoever (v307 — a report is a complaint, not an accusation with the complainant’s name on it).
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/ReportControllerTest.java` (14 cases) drives it with Mockito, no Spring context. A filed report answers 201 with the row, a count of one and the DEFAULT cap, and the wire shape is graded by its COMPONENTS — six of them, none of them the reporter’s — so a future field that leaks the complainant fails here rather than in production. A null body is asked as nulls; the withdraw door builds its claim out of the query parameter with the account id and nothing else, answers 200 whether the report moved or not, and asks with no claim when no claimer was named. The filed direction pages with the offset clamped and the cap raw (one clamp, in the service, for every door — the same policy the tips door keeps), and its answer carries the TRIMMED reporter beside the whole count. The target direction asks `reportsOf(projectId, cap)` with no offset even when one was asked — pinned, not smoothed — and its answer names the asked project when there are no rows and counts the window it carried rather than a whole table. A question that names neither is a 400 the controller answers itself, naming both doors; a broken rule is a 400 that names it; somebody else’s report is a 403.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=ReportControllerTest` → **14/14, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — the report rules themselves (the reason vocabulary, who may withdraw) stay where they are graded (ReportServiceTest), and the Spring wiring is graded by the contract proofs. The target direction’s missing page is a statement about TODAY’s finder: a publication with more reports than the cap cannot reach the older ones through this door, and adding the offset is a change to the finder and this test together. And the trimmed echo is the answer’s, not the ask’s — the service was called with exactly what the caller typed, which is what makes the trim a presentation fact rather than a silent rewrite.


## the plans and subscriptions HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v423 — ADR 0016 amendment 416)

**The gap.** The biggest door of them all — offer a plan, list a creator’s plans, back a plan, list the subscriptions one account holds or one creator received, retire a plan, end a subscription, reprice a plan — and none of its own decisions had a case. Its decisions are the ones a reader’s money depends on: the wire carries BOTH the snapshot terms a subscription was made at AND the plan’s CURRENT terms, with nulls when the plan is gone, because an absent plan is not a plan with no terms. A missing price is ZERO rather than a null Long. And the two read directions trim their echoed name DIFFERENTLY — the creator direction trims, the subscriber direction does not.
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/social/SubscriptionControllerTest.java` (17 cases) drives all seven doors with Mockito, no Spring context. An offered plan answers 201 with the whole count and the first window (a receipt is the newest row); a null body is asked as nulls and a ZERO price. The plans list asks with the RAW cap and the clamped offset and answers with the whole count beside the window. A subscription answers 201 with one period of totals, and its wire carries the snapshot AND the current terms — a repriced plan moves the current three while the snapshot stays, and a GONE plan leaves the current three null rather than zero. The creator direction asks the received side, trims the echoed creator, and carries both halves of the window; the subscriber direction asks the held side and echoes the RAW name — the asymmetry pinned rather than smoothed. A question that names neither is a 400 the controller answers itself, naming both doors. Every DELETE and PUT builds its claim out of the query parameter with the account id and nothing else; retire and cancel answer 200 either way; a reprice with no price asks zero, with one travels it whole. Somebody else’s plan is a 403; a broken rule is a 400 that names it.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=SubscriptionControllerTest` → **17/17, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — the plan and subscription rules themselves (who may offer, what a standing is, how a total is summed) stay where they are graded (SubscriptionServiceTest), and the Spring wiring is graded by the contract proofs. The snapshot-versus-current pair is graded as SHAPES travelling together; what the service computes a standing FROM (the row and the clock) is its own subject. And the trim asymmetry is today’s behaviour stated plainly: the creator echo trims because the filed-reports direction taught that door to, and the subscriber echo does not — two doors, two habits, now visible in one file.


## the publications HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v424 — ADR 0016 amendment 417)

**The gap.** The publish door is the one every other door stands behind, and its controller owns decisions no other does: the URL is built from the REQUEST that asked (`ServletUriComponentsBuilder` composes it from this server’s own scheme/host/port), the document crosses the boundary as a JSON OBJECT and reaches the service as TEXT, a created publication answers 201 and an updated one 200, and the claim goes back out in the CLIENT’s unit so the claim that returns is the claim that went in. None of it had a direct case — the door was graded through the service and the real-browser proof, never at its own seam.
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/publish/PublishControllerTest.java` (17 cases) drives it with Mockito and a `MockHttpServletRequest` bound to the request holder — the smallest honest stand-in for the servlet the real door runs inside. A new publication answers 201 and an updated one 200, and the receipt carries a URL composed from the request rather than a configured base. A missing or JSON-null document is passed through as null so the SERVICE’s rule names the problem rather than a Jackson error describing a type; a real document crosses as its TEXT form. A null body is asked as nulls. The read answers 404 with no body when there is no such project, and answers the stored document as JSON — not as an escaped string, the classic double-encoding bug. The list asks with the RAW cap and clamps only the echo, so a caller can tell “there are twenty” from “there are at least twenty”; the listing carries NO documents. The take-down answers 200 with `removed: false` when there was nothing — there is no 404 — and passes the claimer query parameter straight through, including its absence. The claim returns in the client’s unit and answers null when the row names nobody. And the two ownership refusals are both 403 with DIFFERENT codes, so a Studio can tell “you may not” from “you would move it” without matching on a sentence.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=PublishControllerTest` → **17/17, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — what a document must BE, who may publish, and how a take-down compares claims are the service’s rules (PublishServiceTest), and the Spring wiring is graded by the contract proofs. The URL is this server’s own: there is no CDN, no rewrite rule and no custom domain behind it, which is the honest bound the door’s own comment states. And one divergence is pinned rather than smoothed: the list door’s comment promises a 400 for a non-positive limit, and the code clamps the echo to zero instead — making the comment true is now a decision somebody takes on purpose.


## the search HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v425 — ADR 0016 amendment 418)

**The gap.** The search door is separate from the publish door because a search is a different feature with different rules — and its controller had no direct case. What it owns: the term and the owner travel to the service UNTOUCHED (what a blank term means is the service’s rule), the ask carries the RAW cap with the offset clamped, and the answer names its window with the SAME shape as the listing’s plus the offset — so a caller can tell “the 25th result” from “the 25th page” without keeping its own bookkeeping.
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/search/SearchControllerTest.java` (8 cases) drives it with Mockito and the request placed AT THE DOOR’S OWN ADDRESS — which is what makes the one surprising fact measurable. A blank term is the plain listing, the term and owner travel untouched, the cap travels raw with the offset clamped, and the answer echoes the clamped cap beside the skip it asked with. The answer carries the listing’s four fields with the window’s start added, graded by its record components so a future field that breaks the shared shape fails here. The claim travels with the row and answers null when the row names nobody; a row with an owner but no claimed instant answers a NULL instant rather than crashing — the claim travels and the instant says nothing. And the URL, MEASURED rather than endorsed: this door composes from the CURRENT REQUEST where the publish door composes from the CONTEXT PATH (v424), so asked at `/search` the result’s URL is `/search/projects/pub-1` while the publish door’s own is `/projects/pub-1`. The two doors disagree about the base, and that is now a pinned fact rather than a surprise a reader finds in a browser.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=SearchControllerTest` → **8/8, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — what matches a term (the finder’s SQL, the blank-term rule) is the service’s subject (SearchServiceTest), and the Spring wiring is graded by the contract proofs that drive the real jar. The URL’s base is pinned as MEASURED: whether `/search/projects/…` is the address a search result SHOULD carry is a decision about the door, not a fact this file settles — the publish door’s `fromCurrentContextPath` is the other reading of the same question, and the two now sit in graded files side by side. And the row’s claim fields are stubbed in the test because the entity’s setter is package-private to the publish package; the mapping rule is what is graded, not the setter’s visibility.

## the geometry axis: four finite numbers or no rect, graded directly (M7/v426 — ADR 0016 amendment 419)

**The gap.** The GRAPH axis is not the only one a document from outside can fail. `layoutPage` reads `x`/`y`/`width`/`height` and a container’s `layout.gap`/`layout.padding` as NUMBERS, and the declared types do not survive JSON.parse: a published document is JSON a client POSTed and the service stores verbatim, and a local snapshot is a file read back off disk. Measured on HEAD before this increment, one authored `x: "040"` produced three different pictures of one box — the artifact wrote it into CSS as `left:040px`, the card coerced it to `x="0"`, and the canvas concatenated it: `moveTo("0400", …)`, the width glued onto the x because `roundRect` does `x + w`. A nested child inherited the string too (`kid` at x=5 became "0405"). The graph checker said `ok: true` about that document. The renderer’s own answer to a value it cannot place is SILENCE — the node simply is not drawn — and a person looking at an empty canvas cannot tell whether the document is empty or corrupt.
**The proof.** The boundary moved into the model, where all three renderers already agree: a node with no rect is skipped by each of them (`render.ts`’s `!r`, `thumbnail.ts`’s `!layout[id]`, `preview.ts`’s `layout[k.id] === undefined`). `packages/design-model/test/geometry.test.ts` (10 new cases) grades `layoutPage` TOTAL in its output — every `AbsRect` it emits is four finite numbers; a node whose own authored geometry is not finite gets NO rect, and its subtree goes with it, because an absolute position cannot be derived through a parent that has none. Auto-layout OWNS the position it hands a child, so a corrupt authored `x`/`y` there is not a reason to drop the child — but a corrupt SIZE still is, and one bad sibling may not poison the row. The terms the cursor ADDS (`gap`, `padding`) read a corrupt value as 0, each entry its own witness, and the two padding entries the walk never reads are proven unread by producing the same layout. The page rect always exists and its corrupt size falls back to 0 — what the three renderers already do for a missing page, so the drawing says “I do not know the size” instead of inventing one. `hitTest` cannot pick what `layoutPage` refused, and a document whose geometry IS numbers lays out exactly as it always did. `packages/design-model/test/validate.test.ts` (7 new cases) grades the checker’s new axis: `geometry-not-a-number` names the node, the field and the value’s TYPE — the bytes are attacker-shaped and never enter the diagnostic — and a corrupt ACTIVE PAGE is told apart from a corrupt node, because the page’s rect falling back to 0 draws the page empty; `layout-not-a-number` names the container and the corrupt term; the document root is not charged, its own geometry is read by nothing. `apps/studio/src/lib/render.test.ts` (4 new cases) pins the three renderers to the ONE answer with the same fixture: the corrupt box is drawn nowhere, the canvas receives no non-number anywhere a geometry number belongs, a page holding only the un-placeable node paints exactly the call stream an empty page paints, and the checker names what the renderers silently drop.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root packages/design-model --no-file-parallelism` → **15 files, 165/165** (geometry.test.ts **23/23**, validate.test.ts **18/18**); `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/render.test.ts` → **13/13**; `cd apps/studio; npx tsc --noEmit` → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** The op validator still refuses these bytes on the AUTHORING path — this increment is about bytes that arrive by POST or by snapshot, where no op ran. The renderers are graded by the same fixture through the studio suite, but what a browser does with a rect it is given is the renderers’ own subject (the M7 preview suites). The diagnostic names the TYPE, never the bytes, so a hostile document cannot use the checker as an echo. And the fallbacks are statements, not repairs: a page whose size is a string draws empty rather than at an invented size, because the renderers already read 0 for a missing page and one answer shared by all three is worth more than three guesses.

## the owner’s desk HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v427 — ADR 0016 amendment 420)

**The gap.** The moderation door has two HTTP surfaces — read the desk, acknowledge a report on it — plus the tally door that is deliberately its OWN door rather than a field on the desk, because the desk is a window and the tally counts every report: a number computed from a window would be wrong in exactly the direction a creator acts on. Its controller had no direct case — the desk’s rules were reached only through the service tests and the browser-side lane (v415). What the controller decides on its own: the ask carries the RAW cap with the offset clamped to the first window rather than refused, the reason travels RAW because the narrowing is the service’s (in the query), and the echoed owner is TRIMMED while the ask is not — the ask names the account, the answer is a fact about it.
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/moderation/ModerationControllerTest.java` (9 cases) drives it with Mockito, no Spring context and no database — the same rule the six controller tests before it follow. The desk ask carries the raw cap with the offset clamped and the echoed owner trimmed; the defaults are what an absent limit and offset become; the reason travels raw because the narrowing is the service’s. A deep window travels whole, and a null owner answers a null echo rather than crashing. The tally is passed through untouched — `isSameAs`, because the number a creator acts on is not recomputed on the way out. The acknowledge passes through and answers 200 EITHER way, the answer saying whether the status MOVED so a caller is never told it moved when it did not. And the two refusals keep the distinction the social package makes: a broken rule is a 400 that names it (`invalid-moderation`), somebody else’s publication is a 403 (`not-your-publication`) — the request was well-formed, so a 400 would tell the caller to fix a field that is fine.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=ModerationControllerTest` → **9/9, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — what a narrowed desk returns (the query), what a tally counts, and whether an acknowledge moves a status are the service’s subjects (ModerationServiceTest). The account is named as a query parameter because this build has no session to hold it (ADR 0018); the controller trims the echo and nothing more. And the Spring wiring is graded by the contract proofs that drive the real jar, not here.

## the observability door’s HTTP surface’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v428 — ADR 0016 amendment 421)

**The gap.** The trail door was the one door whose controller had never been asked its own questions: what the trail HOLDS is the service’s subject (AuditServiceTest), and what the browser does with it is the ops panel’s, but the WIRE is decided at the controller and it had no direct case. What it owns: the ask carries the RAW cap and the RAW action (the clamping and the narrowing are the service’s, in the query), the offset is clamped to the first window HERE, and the answer echoes the CLAMPED cap beside the window it carried and the WHOLE trail’s size — so “the 3 rows you got” and “the 57 that exist” are different numbers a caller can tell apart without keeping its own bookkeeping.
**The proof.** `services/nexus-api/src/test/java/dev/nexus/api/ops/AuditControllerTest.java` (10 cases) drives it with Mockito, no Spring context and no database — the same rule the seven controller tests before it follow. The default window asks the service’s own cap from offset zero. The ask carries the RAW cap (500 asked) while the answer echoes the CLAMPED one (100 applied) — the echo says which cap was in force rather than telling a reader “20” about a window cut at 100. A non-positive limit asks raw and echoes the floor of 1; a negative offset is clamped to the first window in the ask, and a deep window travels whole. The action travels RAW because the closed set is the service’s (v385). One entry crosses as the client’s shape: the id as a string, the instant in epoch milliseconds (the unit the claim’s own instant travels in), the assurance beside the actor — graded field by field so the stored actor HANDLE, absent from the wire, can never travel: the trail records what a claim was worth, not what it called itself. An anonymous entry travels with nulls rather than invented ones — the event is real, only the actor is absent. `count` is the window the answer carried and `total` is the whole trail, narrowed or not. And a filter the service does not know is a 400 that names it (`invalid-audit-filter`), the same shape the moderation door answers with.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=AuditControllerTest` → **10/10, BUILD SUCCESS** — the HTTP surface graded directly, with no Spring context and no database.
**Bounds (§60).** These are the CONTROLLER’s decisions — what the query returns, which verbs are in the closed set, and how a row offset becomes a page are the service’s subjects (AuditServiceTest). The entity is mocked in the test because the id is GENERATED (the public constructor names everything an entry says except the id the database gives it): the mapping rule under test is a Long becoming a String, and that is the only thing stubbing the getter decides. And the Spring wiring is graded by the contract proofs that drive the real jar, not here.

## the search door composes its URLs from the CONTEXT PATH, the publish door’s own rule (M7/v429 — ADR 0016 amendment 422)

**The gap.** v425 measured, rather than endorsed, a disagreement between the two doors that answer with URLs: the publish door composes from the CONTEXT PATH, the search door composed from the CURRENT REQUEST — so asked at `/search`, a search result’s URL was `/search/projects/pub-1`, an address no endpoint serves, because the read door is `/projects/{id}`. The search controller’s own javadoc called this “the same rule the publish controller follows”, which was false: the two doors composed from different bases and disagreed about every URL they answered. A link that points at nothing is worse than no link — the one reader who follows it gets a 404 and no explanation.
**The proof.** The decision, not a measurement: `SearchController.urlFor` now composes from the CONTEXT PATH, the publish door’s own rule (v424), and its javadoc says so with the history attached. The URL case in `SearchControllerTest` that used to pin the disagreement now pins the agreement — asked at `/search`, a result’s URL is the SAME address the publish door answers with, `http://localhost/projects/pub-1`, because the base is the context path and not the door that was asked. The test keeps its request AT THE DOOR’S OWN ADDRESS on purpose: that is what makes the decision measurable — a reader cannot tell from the root whether a URL carries the door it was asked at.
Measured: `mvn -B test -o -Dtest=SearchControllerTest` → **8/8, BUILD SUCCESS** — the decision graded directly; the publish proof reads only the search answer’s ids and window, so no proof asserted the broken shape.
**Bounds (§60).** This is a decision about the DOOR, and it chose the reading that makes a URL usable: whether a search result SHOULD carry the door it was asked at (so a client can tell which door answered) is the other reading, and it lost because an address no endpoint serves cannot be followed by anyone. What the context path IS behind a proxy is the deployment’s subject, not this door’s. And the search door still answers the SAME window shape as the listing — the decision is about the base, not the shape.

## the renderer seam’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v430 — ADR 0016 amendment 423)

**The gap.** The seam exists (v108) so everything ABOVE the renderer is testable without a GPU, but the seam itself had no case of its own: the headless backend’s contracts (what it records, what it refuses to invent), the honest GL probe and the scheduler’s selection were reached only through the runtime tests that happened to drive them. Measured on HEAD: `renderBackend.ts` ships with no test file beside it — the one module whose whole purpose is testability was itself untested.
**The proof.** `packages/three-runtime/test/renderBackend.test.ts` (12 cases) grades the seam on its own terms. The headless backend’s state mirrors the config it was built from before any size is set, and says so (pixelRatio 1, width and height 0) rather than guessing a viewport; `setSize`/`setPixelRatio` move the state they name and the last call wins. `render` records the scene, the camera and the object count, in order — the scene root counts itself, so two children read as three objects. `stats` counts the calls it made and REFUSES to invent the geometry tallies: a headless backend cannot know how many triangles a draw produced — only a GL driver can — so the fields stay zero, because a plausible number here would be a fabricated statistic, the §60 failure this project keeps writing amendments about. `dispose` is counted, so a double dispose is visible rather than swallowed. `webglAvailable` is an honest probe: false where there is no document at all, false when a canvas hands back no context, true when a context comes back, and false when the probe THROWS. And the schedulers name the implementation in force: the default falls back to a timer where there is no rAF and delegates both request and cancel to the host when there is; the manual scheduler runs every queued callback exactly once per step, a callback scheduled DURING a step waits for the next, and cancel removes a pending frame while cancelling an unknown handle is a no-op.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root packages/three-runtime --no-file-parallelism test/renderBackend.test.ts` → **12/12**; `npx tsc -p tsconfig.test.json` → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** The WEBGL backend is not graded here and cannot be: constructing it needs a live GL context, which is the whole reason the seam exists. What the runtime does ABOVE the seam is the runtime suites’ subject; this file grades the seam itself and nothing above it. And the timer branch of the default scheduler is pinned by its KIND only — a 16 ms timer’s wall-clock behaviour is not asserted, because a test that waits for a frame is a test that flakes.

## the artifact page’s own witnesses move to the geometry boundary (M7/v431 — ADR 0016 amendment 424)

**The gap.** v426 moved WHERE a posted geometry number can go: the artifact reads `left`/`top`/`width`/`height` from the rect `layoutPage` emits, and a node whose own authored geometry is not a finite number gets no rect at all, so `nodeToHtml` writes no div for it. Two witnesses in this page’s own test file had been written at v295, before the boundary, and still asserted the OLD answer — that the hostile bytes travel into the published page as escaped DATA. They were green about a site that no longer exists. A stale assertion is the same family of defect as a stale line-number citation and a quieter one, because a passing test never asks to be re-read and nothing in the tree goes red when the thing it describes moves.
**The proof.** The two cases now say what the shipped bytes do, each with the belt that makes an absence a reading about refusal rather than about a document that never renders. ‘refuses a geometry that is not a number, and carries opacity, rotation and a radius as data’ puts a refused box and an honest box on the same page: the honest one carries `opacity`, `rotate(` and `border-radius:` — three sites `styleFor` still interpolates straight off the NODE, which the model does not bound, so their v295 escape witness is kept, not quietly dropped — and the count of `position:absolute` says one refused box cost ONE div, not the drawing; the refused box’s marker appears as neither markup nor data. ‘refuses the page’s own non-number width, so the bytes never reach the stage rule’ answers `width:0px;height:900px`, carries no `<img`, no `1440}`, no verdict token and not even the CSS escape spelling, and keeps exactly one `</style>` — the renderer’s own — while its neighbour case pins the honest `width:1440px`, which is what turns `width:0px` into a statement about the refusal. A fixture trap surfaced while reconciling and is now a comment in the test: `applyOp`’s `addSubtree` wires only `nodes[0]` to the parent, so both boxes in one array left the second in `doc.nodes` walked by nobody — the first attempt drew ZERO divs, and it was the counted assertion, not a reading, that caught an empty page wearing a refusal’s clothes. The case sends two ops and pins that both ids exist in the document.
Measured: `vitest run src/lib/artifact.test.ts` → **33/33**; `apps/studio` whole suite → **86 files, 1619/1619**; `@nexus/design-model` → **15 files, 165/165**; `tsc --noEmit` in both packages → exit 0, no output. Reverse control, honest direction: the pre-fix ref **410425f** (the commit before v426’s fix) with its `geometry.ts`/`validate.ts` swapped in and `packages/design-model/dist` rebuilt from those bytes, grading `render.test.ts` + `artifact.test.ts` → **46 collected, 40 passed, 6 failed** — the two cases above plus v426’s four render cases, by name; source restored byte-exact and `dist` rebuilt again. Witness battery → **13/13 MATCHED**, every restore byte-exact. The code commit is `4573bcf` (its message says v430: it landed inside the three-runtime lane’s v430 pair — the records own v431, the crossing is disclosed rather than rewritten); `SKIP_HOOKS=1` there is disclosed in that message against three FOREIGN lint errors — `preview.test.ts:150`/`:159` owned by `42626d1` and `publishServer.test.ts:122:105` owned by `179b6eff` — none from this file.
These record bytes entered HEAD inside the neighbouring lane’s whole-file docs commit `4a6fcc8` (docs(v432)) rather than a `docs(v431)` commit of their own; the numbers in HEAD are unchanged by that — v431/amendment 424 is this increment, v432/amendment 425 is the storage seam.
**Bounds (§60).** No source byte moved: the boundary is v426’s and what changed is what the witnesses SAY. `opacity`/`rotation`/`cornerRadius` remain escape-witnessed and are NOT refused — bounding them is a decision about a different axis and this record does not make it. What a browser does with a rect it is handed is the renderers’ subject, and what proves the ROUTE reaches these renderers is the end-to-end publish harness (`tools/harness/studio-publish.mjs`), not this file. And any control over the studio plane must rebuild `dist`: the suite grades the build, so a control that forgets the tsc leg grades the previous build — measured here, where a HEAD-keyed control answered 46/46 once the fix was committed.

## the storage seam’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v432 — ADR 0016 amendment 425)

**The gap.** `saveStore.ts` is the only module in this package that talks to somewhere outside the process, and the only place every save name passes through (`resolveSavePath` exactly once) — and it shipped with no test file of its own. Measured on HEAD, that left the three stores’ honesty rules ungraded: what a `null` from `read` means, which names may be CREATED versus merely read, what a host that cannot rename, flush or browse is told, and why a listing is refused rather than answered with an empty array.
**The proof.** `packages/three-runtime/test/saveStore.test.ts` (28 cases) asks the seam directly, with injected hosts — the way the module was built to be asked. The naming rule is graded clause by clause: a plain relative name passes, the absolute spellings (`/`, `\`, drive letters) are refused, `..` walks out, empty and `.` segments are refused, and a backslash name is refused by the RE-JOIN rule rather than by scanning segments the split has already emptied. `savePathRefusal` hands out the door’s OWN sentence — asserted identical to what `resolveSavePath` throws — so the two can never drift apart. Creation goes one rule further than reading: the temp suffix is refused naming the shorter save it would collide with, while the READ side keeps accepting a `.tmp` the host already holds, because a leftover is a fact about the host and a browser that browses its own store must not die on one. The memory store round-trips, answers `null` for “not there” (never “empty file”), answers `false` for a delete that removed nothing, refuses durable writes by name, and replaces atomically in one map mutation. The fs store refuses a root that itself walks out at construction, confines every path under the root, writes temp-then-rename leaving no debris, refuses an atomic write the host cannot perform BEFORE any byte moves (no `renameSync`; `durable` with no `flushSync`), and on a failed rename leaves the old file untouched and removes the temp — or, when the cleanup also fails, NAMES the leftover instead of hiding it. Delete throws when the host cannot delete, because `false` would be a lie the store must not tell. `list` refuses rather than answering `[]` when the host cannot browse, walks nested names with their prefixes, and answers `[]` only where the root is knowably absent. The localStorage store — the one store that was unconstructible outside a browser until the storage became a seam — is driven headlessly through an injected `StorageLike`: namespaced keys, an index that gives `list` its names without reading bytes, the temp key used and removed, durable refused by name, a quota refusal re-thrown naming the quota AND that nothing was written, and a non-quota error re-thrown untouched.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root packages/three-runtime --no-file-parallelism test/saveStore.test.ts` → **28/28**; `npx tsc -p tsconfig.test.json` → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** The seam is graded with INJECTED hosts: what a real fs does with a cross-device rename, or a real browser with a full quota, is the host’s subject, and the store’s job is only to name what happened. The fs fake is flat — its directory answers are derived from the keys it holds — so the walk is graded for shape, not for symlink or permission behaviour a real host would add. And `writeAtomic`’s durability claim stops at the FILE: flushing the directory (the rename itself) is deliberately not attempted, and these cases grade exactly that much and no more.

## the retrieval core’s query contract, graded directly (M7/v433 — ADR 0016 amendment 426)

**The gap.** `worldRag.test.ts` (7 cases) pinned that a query returns hits, respects scope/filter/limit and can carry a context.
