What it did not pin is the contract those answers are made of: what ORDER equal scores come back in, what a limit does to the count the answer reports, what the threshold admits when a document shares nothing with the question, what a rebuilt index does to the documents the last world state left behind, and what a document actually SAYS. Measured on HEAD, each of these was reachable only by reading the code — the query answered correctly while its own rules went unasked.
**The proof.** Nine new cases in the same file (7 → 16) ask them directly. A score tie is broken by ID, so the order is a rule and not a map-iteration accident — and a document that shares nothing sorts LAST at score 0 rather than being dropped, because the default threshold admits it. A limit truncates the results while `totalHits` keeps counting the whole answer — the window is not the answer’s idea of how many matched. The default threshold admits a zero-score document and SAYS it is worth 0; a named threshold above the score excludes it and `totalHits` follows. A filter is exact equality on every key it names, including a null one. The context is the RANKED hits, each as `[TYPE] title: content`, blank-line separated, and the answer says how long it took. A second world state REPLACES the index: no document from the last world lingers. And the documents themselves are graded: a character reads as labelled lines with only ACTIVE goals listed, an absent faction as “none”, a relationship as `id: type (strength)`, and its own `updatedAt`; an event titles itself type + the first 50 characters of its description and keeps its own timestamp; a location — the one builder whose type carries no clock — stamps itself with NOW, and an unconnected place says so with an empty list.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root packages/world-engine --no-file-parallelism test/worldRag.test.ts` → **16/16**; `npx tsc -p tsconfig.test.json` → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** The scoring itself (`hybridScore`, the hash embeddings) is `@nexus/agent-runtime`’s subject, pinned there; this file grades what the retrieval core DOES with a score — order, threshold, window, count. The fixtures are minimal worlds built by hand, so what a full WorldEngine timeline looks like is the engine suite’s subject. And the location builder’s `Date.now()` is pinned as a window (before ≤ stamp ≤ after), because a clock read is the one thing a test cannot make exact.

## the demo batch producer’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v434 — ADR 0016 amendment 427)

**The gap.** `compose.test.ts` pins `compose`: the template detection, the tree wiring, the determinism. The function the DemoDesignProvider actually drives is `composeDesignBatch`, which wraps `compose` and turns the tree into the OpBatch the editor applies — and its own decisions had no direct case: where the section enters the page, in what order the nodes travel, what the provenance and the summary say, what clock the batch is stamped with, and what the seed defaults to. Measured on HEAD, every one of those was reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Seven new cases in the same file (8 → 15) ask them directly. The section is inserted as ONE `addSubtree` at index 0 of the ACTIVE page — on top, not appended under whatever is already there. The nodes travel ROOT-FIRST, every non-root naming a parent that appears earlier in the list, so `applyOp` can wire the whole subtree in one pass; the count matches what `compose` produced, no node lost or duplicated. The provenance names the demo mode and the template that was chosen (`demo:auth`, `demo:dashboard`), so a reader can tell which generator spoke. The summary quotes the prompt — with the typographic quotes the composer actually writes, pinned as measured — and only its first 60 characters. The batch is stamped at 0 (the demo authors at clock-zero; the runtime stamps when it applies) and its id derives from the seed. With no seed named, the prompt IS the seed: two calls with one prompt and one document produce byte-identical batches, and the id is the documented `demoId('batch', hashString(prompt))`. And the batch applies cleanly to a real document: the section lands on the active page as its first child, the threaded parent survives the apply, and every node the batch carried exists afterwards.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root packages/ai-providers --no-file-parallelism test/compose.test.ts` → **15/15**; `npx tsc -p tsconfig.test.json` → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** The templates themselves — what each builder puts in a section — are `compose`’s subject and stay pinned by the cases above; this file grades the batch the tree becomes. The document the batch lands on is a fresh `createEmptyDocument`, so what the section does beside EXISTING page content is the editor suite’s subject. And the summary’s curly quotes are pinned as MEASURED: whether a summary should carry typographic quotes is a decision about the composer’s voice, not a fact this file settles.

## the embedding seam’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v435 — ADR 0016 amendment 428)

**The gap.** `hashEmbed` and `cosine` are pure, and both are load-bearing: the router embed/rank surface, the agents-runtime token-bag seam and the vector stores all lean on them, and the runtime hybrid score literally reads `0.6 * cosine(qv, e.vector) + 0.4 * coverage` — a formula that stays bounded only because `hashEmbed` hands back unit vectors while `cosine` stays a raw dot product. Neither half of that contract had a case: the only existing checks ask “same text, same vector” through the router and read one self-similarity at 5 digits. Measured on HEAD, the normalization, the dim parameter, the value range and the behaviour on mismatched lengths were reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** A new `test/embedding.test.ts` asks them directly, 22 cases. Shape: `EMBED_DIM` is 64 and is the default length, custom dims are honoured (1, 8, 257), dim 0 yields an empty vector rather than a crash or NaN, the empty string is a legal input seeded by the FNV offset basis, and long and unicode text stays finite. Determinism: byte-identical per text, at every dim, and distinct texts diverge. Normalization: unit length for every probe (empty, one char, long, unicode), every component inside [-1, 1], and never the all-zero vector that would rank nothing. `cosine` is pinned as the raw dot product it is: 2,0 × 3,0 = 6 and not 1, orthogonal 0, antipodal -1, signs multiply, symmetric, and two empty vectors score 0 rather than NaN. Mismatched lengths are zero-padded rather than rejected, so the same text at two dims scores below identity (measured 0.778 — each dim count is its own normalization space). And the pair is graded the way the retrieval stack actually uses it: every pair of probe embeddings scores inside [-1, 1], the bound the runtime weighting leans on, while distinct texts sit at effectively independent directions (measured 0.0008), because `hashEmbed` is a per-string projection and NOT token-aware — the token-bag averaging that makes a shared word matter lives in the runtime seam, not here.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root packages/ai-providers --no-file-parallelism` → **9 files, 95/95** (was 73 across 8 files); `npx tsc -p tsconfig.test.json` → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** The token-bag composition (`embedTokens`) and the hybrid score that mixes cosine with token coverage are the agents-runtime seam’s subject; here they appear only as the reason the [-1, 1] bound matters. Whether a hash embedding is the right retrieval primitive at all is ADR 0010’s decision — this file grades the seam that decision installed. And the two numbers quoted above (0.778, 0.0008) are pinned as MEASURED snapshots of a deterministic generator, not as claims about its distribution.

## the likes door’s write half, graded directly (M7/v436 — ADR 0016 amendment 429)

**The gap.** `readLikes` had six direct cases (v400). The two writes and the viewer read — `likeOnService`, `unlikeOnService`, `readLikesOnService` — were reached only through the lanes that call them, so nothing ever asked what a write carries or what a refusal answers: whether an unfilled claim field is dropped or named empty, whether a 200 saying `ok:false` counts as a like, whether a refused like comes back with a count, which verb goes where, and whether every ask is bounded. Measured on HEAD, all of it was reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Fourteen new cases in the same file (6 → 20) ask them directly. A like is POSTed to the likes door with the claim exactly as the browser built it — the publication in the body, the account, handle, assurance and instant beside it — and an absent handle, assurance or claimedAt is written as `null` rather than dropped, so the service can tell “empty” from “never asked”. A refusal carries a reason and NO count: a 200 saying `ok:false` is still a refusal, and a count of zero there would show a reader a like the service never took. The service error body carries no `ok` field (v313), so the reason is read from `message`, and with neither a reason nor a message the HTTP status is named. A body that is not JSON is a refusal, not a crash. Unreachable and merely-late are different sentences: a `TimeoutError` names the 15-second budget rather than calling a busy service gone. Taking a like back is a DELETE naming the claimer and only the claimer — no handle, no assurance, no instant, and no body at all. The POST names the publication in its body while the DELETE names it in its path, encoded. The viewer read asks with the viewer when there is one and omits the parameter entirely when there is not, and it answers with no window, because it is the who-am-I read and not a page. And every ask carries the service-call budget signal, so a silent service cannot hang a server-rendered page.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/likeServer.test.ts` → **20/20**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the SERVICE does with the claim — recording it, verifying nothing (ADR 0018), what it answers — is the nexus-api suite’s subject; this file grades what the Studio sends and what it makes of the answer. The lanes that call these functions keep their own cases; here the door is asked with an injected fetch and no lane in the way. And the 15-second budget is `serviceCall.ts`’s constant, asserted here only as PRESENT, not as the right number.

## the comments door’s write half, graded directly (M7/v437 — ADR 0016 amendment 430)

**The gap.** `readComments` had six direct cases (v399). The two writes and the other-side read — `writeComment`, `removeCommentOnService`, `readCommentsWritten` — were reached only through the lanes that call them, so what a comment carries on its way out and what an answer means on its way back were never asked. Two rules here have no counterpart in the likes door and are the reason this is its own increment: a take-back that found nothing is still a SUCCESS (the service answers 200 either way and says which), and the comment a write answers with is the first entry of the answer’s list rather than a field of its own. Measured on HEAD, both were reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Nineteen new cases in the same file (6 → 25) ask them directly. A comment is POSTed with the text a person wrote beside the claim — an absent handle, assurance or claimedAt written as `null` rather than dropped — and the text survives JSON with its quotes, newlines and markup intact. The comment a write answers with is the FIRST entry of the answer’s list, and an ok answer carrying none leaves the field absent rather than inventing one. A refusal carries a reason and no comment; the error body has no `ok` field, so the reason is read from `message` and then from the HTTP status. A take-back is a DELETE naming the comment and the claimer and only those, with no body. And the marquee rule: a take-back that found NOTHING is still a success — `removed: false` means “already gone”, not “your take-back failed”, the same rule the un-like keeps (v320) — while `removed` is read as the boolean the service said and not as truthiness. The author’s own read asks by author and limit with NO window — the other side of the relation, not a page — and answers `visible: 0` when the service does not say, a divergence from `readComments` that leaves the field absent, pinned as measured. Every refusal and every unreachable call names itself.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/commentServer.test.ts` → **25/25**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the SERVICE does — recording the comment, taking it back, answering 200 either way — is the nexus-api suite’s subject; this file grades what the Studio sends and what it makes of the answer. The lanes that call these functions keep their own cases; here the door is asked with an injected fetch and no lane in the way. And the `visible: 0` default is pinned as MEASURED: whether the two readers SHOULD answer an unstated count the same way is a decision about the door’s shape, not a fact this file settles.

## the follows lane’s write half, graded directly (M7/v438 — ADR 0016 amendment 431)

**The gap.** `readMyFollowing` had seven direct cases. The two writes — `followAccount`, `unfollowAccount` — were reached only through the profile bar that calls them, so the three things this lane does differently from every server-side door were never asked: it talks to the STUDIO’S OWN door at a relative path, because the service’s address is server-side and the browser never learns it; it carries a 45-second budget on a WRITE rather than the read budget (v294 — the first call to a route pays that route’s compile); and it answers a thrown fetch with the RAW error rather than an “unreachable” sentence, because the thing that failed is this Studio and not a remote service. Measured on HEAD, all three were reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Ten new cases in the same file (7 → 17) ask them directly. A follow is POSTed to `/api/follows` — a relative path, so no browser ever learns where the service lives. A write carries the 45-second budget, pinned by spying on `AbortSignal.timeout` rather than by restating the number the module keeps private. The claim travels as the browser built it: this lane does NOT null-fill an absent handle, a divergence from the server-side doors (v436, v437) pinned as MEASURED. A refusal carries a reason and no count, the door’s error body having no `ok` field, so the reason is read from `message` and then from the HTTP status. A follow that never arrived answers with the raw error, and not an “unreachable at <address>” sentence that would send a reader looking for a remote service that was never called. And a take-back is a DELETE naming the account and the claimer and only those, with no body, answering with the count that is left and that the follow is no longer standing.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/followLane.test.ts` → **17/17**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the Studio’s own `/api/follows` route does with the forwarded claim is the route suite’s subject; this file grades what the browser sends and what it makes of the answer. Whether the lane SHOULD null-fill its claim the way the server-side doors do is a decision about one wire shape across two doors, not a fact this file settles. And the read half of this lane carries no budget of its own — measured, and named here rather than pinned as intended, because a read that hangs a panel is the failure mode v337 was written for.

## the tips lane’s write half, graded directly (M7/v439 — ADR 0016 amendment 432)

**The gap.** Both reads (`readMyTips`, `readMyTipsGiven`) had seven direct cases between them. `giveTip` was reached only through the tip bar, so what a tip carries on its way out and what the answer means were never asked: the five fields of the ask, whether `anonymous` survives as the boolean the payer chose, which tip of the answer’s list is reported, and whether the write is bounded. Measured on HEAD, all of it was reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Nine new cases in the same file (7 → 16) ask them directly. A tip is POSTed to `/api/tips` with all five fields — recipient, amount, message, the anonymous boolean and the claim — and `anonymous` travels as the boolean while the amount travels as a number, so neither is coerced on the way out. A message with quotes, newlines and markup survives the round trip. The answer reports the FIRST tip the door lists, narrowed to the receipt’s two facts, and an ok answer carrying none leaves the field absent rather than inventing one. A refusal carries a reason and no receipt; the door’s error body has no `ok` field, so the reason is read from `message` and then from the HTTP status. A tip that never arrived answers with the raw error and not a remote address, because the door is this Studio’s own route. And a write carries the 45-second budget (v294), pinned on `AbortSignal.timeout`.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/tipLane.test.ts` → **16/16**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the Studio’s own `/api/tips` route does with the forwarded tip, and what the service records, are the route and nexus-api suites’ subjects; this file grades what the browser sends and what it makes of the answer. Whether an anonymous tip should hide the payer at the SERVICE rather than only in the panel is ADR 0018’s subject, not this file’s. And the write answer’s narrowing to two facts is pinned as MEASURED: whether a receipt should carry the payer and the instant is a decision about what a tip bar shows, not a fact this file settles.

## the plans lane’s two writes, graded directly (M7/v440 — ADR 0016 amendment 433)

**The gap.** `retirePlan`, `cancelSubscription` and `repricePlan` had direct cases. `subscribeToPlan` and `offerPlan` were reached only through the plan bar, and the rule that makes them interesting is the one `readAnswer` spells out: the SERVICE answers a write with a LISTING shape (`subscriptions`, `plans`) while the Studio’s own door forwards a SINGLE row (`subscription`, `plan`), and this lane reads BOTH. Measured on HEAD, a lane that understood only one of them would have reported a price of zero for a real subscription, and nothing in the suite would have noticed.
**The proof.** Fourteen new cases in the same file (8 → 22) ask them directly. `subscribeToPlan` POSTs to `/api/subscriptions` with the plan and the claim; `offerPlan` POSTs to `/api/plans` with the price and the period, the price as a number and the currency as the code it was given. Both answer shapes are pinned side by side: the single row the Studio’s own door forwards AND the listing the service itself answers, so the lane’s indifference to which door spoke is graded rather than assumed. An ok answer carrying no row leaves the field absent rather than a zero, and a refusal carries a reason and no row. This lane’s own non-JSON wording — “the answer was HTTP N with something that is not JSON”, which differs from every other lane’s — is pinned alongside the raw-error answer for a fetch that never arrived. And a write carries the 45-second budget (v294), pinned on `AbortSignal.timeout`.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/planLane.test.ts` → **22/22**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the Studio’s own `/api/plans` and `/api/subscriptions` routes forward, and what the service records, are the route and nexus-api suites’ subjects; this file grades what the browser sends and what it makes of either answer. Whether the two doors SHOULD answer a write in two different shapes is a decision about the door’s contract, not a fact this file settles — the lane is graded for reading both, which is the only choice available to it. And which of the two rows wins when a listing carries several is the service’s ordering, pinned here only as “the first”.

## the reporter’s write, graded directly (M7/v441 — ADR 0016 amendment 434)

**The gap.** `readMyReports` and `withdrawReport` had direct cases. `fileReport` was reached only through the report bar, so what a report carries on its way out and what the answer means were never asked: the four fields of the ask, which report of the answer’s list is reported, and whether the write is bounded while its take-back is not. Measured on HEAD, all of it was reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Eleven new cases in the same file (9 → 20) ask them directly. A report is POSTed to `/api/reports` with the project, the reason, the detail and the claim, and the detail travels intact through JSON with its quotes and newlines. The answer reports the FIRST report the door lists, and an ok answer carrying none leaves the field absent rather than inventing one. A refusal carries a reason and no report; the door’s error body has no `ok` field, so the reason is read from `message` and then from the HTTP status. A report that never arrived answers with the raw error and not a remote address, because the door is this Studio’s own route. A write carries the 45-second budget (v294), pinned on `AbortSignal.timeout`. And the take-back beside it carries NO budget of its own — pinned as MEASURED and named as the failure mode v337 was written for, rather than left silent in a green suite.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/reportLane.test.ts` → **20/20**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the Studio’s own `/api/reports` route forwards, and what the service records and verifies (ADR 0018), are the route and nexus-api suites’ subjects; this file grades what the browser sends and what it makes of the answer. The withdrawal’s missing budget is pinned as MEASURED and NOT as intended: whether to give the take-back the same 45 seconds the filing carries is a decision for the next cycle, and a test that asserted a budget nobody wrote would be the lie this house refuses. And the report answer’s narrowing to the first row is pinned as measured, the service’s ordering being its own.

## the comments lane’s write and the author’s read, graded directly (M7/v442 — ADR 0016 amendment 435)

**The gap.** `removeComment` had three direct cases. `postComment` and `readMyComments` were reached only through the comment bar, and the rule that makes the write interesting is the one that separates it from the server-side door (v437): there the comment a write answers with is the FIRST entry of a list, while HERE it is a field of its own. Measured on HEAD, a lane that read one shape against the other door would have answered a posted comment with nothing, and nothing in the suite would have noticed.
**The proof.** Thirteen new cases in the same file (3 → 16) ask them directly. `postComment` POSTs to `/api/comments` with the text and the claim, and the text travels intact through JSON with its quotes and newlines. The comment comes back as its OWN field — and a listing shape, the one the server door answers with, reaches this lane as NO comment at all, the divergence pinned as measured rather than averaged away. An ok answer carrying no comment leaves the field absent rather than inventing one; a refusal carries a reason and no comment, with the message-then-status fallback. A comment that never arrived answers with the raw error and not a remote address, and a write carries the 45-second budget (v294), pinned on `AbortSignal.timeout`. And `readMyComments` asks by author with NO window — the other side of the relation, not a page — with a refusal that is not an author who wrote nothing.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/commentLane.test.ts` → **16/16**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the Studio’s own `/api/comments` route forwards, and what the service records, are the route and nexus-api suites’ subjects; this file grades what the browser sends and what it makes of the answer. Whether the two doors SHOULD answer a write in two different shapes is a decision about the door’s contract — the lane is graded for reading the one it is handed, which is the only choice available to it. And the author read’s missing budget is the same measured gap v441 named for the report take-back: pinned here as observed, not as intended.

## the subscription standing derivation, graded directly (M7/v443 — ADR 0016 amendment 436)

**The gap.** `subscriptionStandingOf` has been exercised since v274 only through `toSubscriptionRow`, which asks it once and shows the answer. Asked directly it states a rule no row can show: **the SERVICE’s word wins, and the client’s own computation is the fallback.** A wire that says `cancelled` while carrying `active: true` and a future `expiresAt` is a subscription the service has ended, and a client that recomputed the standing from the flags would show it as standing — answering a question about time with a clock that is not the service’s. Measured on HEAD, the precedence itself was reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Seven new cases in the same file (69 → 76) ask it directly. The service’s word wins in both directions: a wire saying `cancelled` beside `active: true` and a future expiry reads `cancelled`, and a wire saying `active` beside `active: false` and a past expiry reads `active`. A status this runtime does not know — including a wrong-case one — falls through to the client’s own computation. That computation is pinned rule by rule: not active is cancelled whatever the expiry says; an expiry at or before now is expired, with the boundary INCLUSIVE, so the instant of expiry is already expired; and an expiry that is not a number means no expiry rather than an expired subscription. Anything that is not a subscription object is cancelled, never active. And the same wire read at two different nows answers about time and not about the wire. `subscriptionStandingText` is pinned beside it: the three standings in the words the economy surface already uses, a cancelled one reading `Never`.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/economy.test.ts` → **76/76**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the SERVICE decides a standing is — and whether it may say one thing while its flags say another — is the nexus-api suite’s subject; this file grades what the client makes of the wire it is handed. Whether the client’s fallback computation should exist at all, or a wire with no status should be refused rather than computed, is a decision about the door’s contract. And the inclusive expiry boundary is pinned as MEASURED: “expired at the instant of expiry” is the reading this code gives, and a reader who wants the last second covered is asking for a different rule.

## the export seam’s own rules, graded directly (M7/v444 — ADR 0016 amendment 437)

**The gap.** `export.test.ts` had graded `slug` since v414 — the namer all three downloads share. The three downloads themselves (`exportJson`, `exportHtml`, `exportCanvasPng`) were reached only through the toolbar that calls them, so what a download actually IS was never asked: which bytes travel, under which filename, and in what order the anchor is handled. They touch `document`, `Blob` and `URL.createObjectURL`, which is also why nothing had asked: the suite runs in Node with no DOM. Measured on HEAD, every one of those decisions was reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Seven new cases beside the existing eleven (11 → 18) install the smallest honest fakes for the three globals and ask directly. `exportJson` downloads the document as pretty-printed JSON under a slugged snapshot name — the bytes are the canonical snapshot itself, two-space indented, and nothing else. The anchor is appended, clicked, and the SAME node removed: the seam detaches through the element’s own `remove()`, not the body’s `removeChild`, which a fake body alone would have hidden. A document with no usable name still downloads, under the fallback name. `exportHtml` downloads the html it was handed, unchanged. `exportCanvasPng` hands the canvas its own data URL and builds NO blob, asking `toDataURL` for `image/png`. And the three exports name their file the same way from the same name, one namer for all three. The object URL is never revoked — pinned as MEASURED, a small bounded leak named rather than left silent in a green suite.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/export.test.ts` → **18/18**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether a download SHOULD revoke its object URL, and whether a browser’s own cleanup is enough, is a decision about the seam’s lifetime; this file records what it does now. The document the snapshot carries is `createEmptyDocument` with no nodes, so what a snapshot of a REAL design looks like is the design-model suite’s subject. And the fakes grade the seam’s own calls and nothing else: no assertion here claims a browser would honour a detached anchor, only that this code does not detach it before the click.

## the service-side follows door’s write half, graded directly (M7/v445 — ADR 0016 amendment 438)

**The gap.** Both reads (`readFollowers`, `readFollowing`) had eleven direct cases. The two writes — `followOnService`, `unfollowOnService` — were reached only through the Studio’s own route, so what a follow carries on its way to the service and what a refusal answers were never asked. This door is the third of the three server-side writes (likes in v436, comments in v437), and the family shape is what makes it worth pinning: the claim is NULL-FILLED here, where the browser lane (v438) sends it as built — and the budget is the SERVICE’s 15 seconds, not the lane’s 45. Measured on HEAD, both halves of that contrast were reachable only by reading two files side by side.
**The proof.** Nine new cases in the same file (11 → 20) ask them directly. A follow is POSTed with the claim null-filled — every absent field written as `null` rather than dropped, the divergence from the lane pinned rather than smoothed. A refusal carries a reason and no count; the service’s error body has no `ok` field, so the reason is read from `message` and then from the status (v313). A follow that never reached the service says UNREACHABLE with the address named, and a slow one says it was late — the contrast with the lane, which has no remote service to name. The ask carries the SERVICE budget of 15 seconds and not the lane’s 45, pinned on `AbortSignal.timeout`. And a take-back is a DELETE naming the account and the claimer and only those, with no body, its id encoded.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/followServer.test.ts` → **20/20**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the SERVICE records, and whether it should accept a claim whose fields are null rather than absent, are the nexus-api suite’s subject; this file grades what the Studio sends and what it makes of the answer. Whether the lane and the door SHOULD carry the claim in two different shapes is a decision about one wire across two callers, and it is pinned here as MEASURED rather than argued. And the read half of this door carries no budget of its own — the same measured gap v441 and v442 named, now seen a third time.

## the agent dock’s batch and QA-input readers, graded directly (M7/v446 — ADR 0016 amendment 439)

**The gap.** `agentBatchFor` and `qaInputForDoc` were reached only through the panel and through `qaForDoc`, so their own decisions were never asked: where the banner lands on the page, what its words are, and which children a QA read admits. Two of those decisions are the reason this is its own increment: the banner is APPENDED to the page where the demo batch (v434) lands on top, and a child whose geometry is not finite is SKIPPED rather than laid out as NaN (v426). Measured on HEAD, every one of those decisions was reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Twelve new cases in the same file (25 → 37) ask them directly. `agentBatchFor` lands its banner as a single `addSubtree` at the END of the page’s children — where the demo batch (v434) lands on top — and applying it leaves the banner as the LAST child, a text node named ‘Agent Note’. The banner’s characters read `AGENT · {goal} · proposed for {refs joined by ‘, ’}`, so a reader knows whose words these are; a handoff with no refs still builds a batch, the empty join pinned as measured rather than smoothed over. The provenance names the agent runtime and the studio dock, the summary names the goal, and the batch is stamped with a wall-clock instant rather than clock-zero — the demo batch’s zero (v434). The banner’s fixed geometry (24, 24, 560×28) rides flat on the node, not under a frame. `qaInputForDoc` answers null for a page the layout does not know, and otherwise reads the page rect and one rect per child from the real layout. A filled child carries its css fill with the page’s background riding along as `bg`; a child with no fill carries neither. And a child whose geometry is not finite is SKIPPED — absent from the QA input rather than arriving as a rect full of NaN — the v426 rule honoured by the reader as well as by the renderers.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/agentDock.test.ts` → **37/37**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the service records of a handoff (ADR 0018) and what the QA rules themselves say about a rect are the nexus-api and visual-QA suites’ subjects; this file grades what the dock builds and what the QA reader admits. Whether the banner SHOULD append rather than land on top is a decision about the page a reader sees; the contrast with v434 is pinned as measured, not argued. And the corrupt-geometry case is pinned by corrupting one node AFTER a clean batch, so what is graded is `layoutPage`’s skip as the reader honours it, and not what `ops` validation would have refused at the door.

## the service-side plans door’s OFFER, graded directly (M7/v447 — ADR 0016 amendment 440)

**The gap.** `offerOnService` was reached only through the Studio’s own `/api/plans` route, so what an offer carries on its way to the service and what the answer means were never asked. This door is the fourth of the server-side writes (likes in v436, comments in v437, follows in v445), and the family shape is what makes it worth pinning: the claim is NULL-FILLED here too — every absent field written as `null` rather than dropped — and the answer is read through the SAME reader the subscription write uses, so a refusal keeps the service’s own words (v313) and a success carries the FIRST plan of the answer’s list. Measured on HEAD, both halves of that were reachable only by reading two files side by side.
**The proof.** Eleven new cases in the same file (9 → 20) ask them directly. An offer is POSTed to `/plans` with all five fields in the JSON body — the plan id, the price, the currency, the period, and the claim. A claim carrying only an accountId sends `handle`, `assurance` and `claimedAt` as `null`, the family shape pinned rather than smoothed. The ask carries the SERVICE budget of 15 seconds and not the lane’s 45, pinned on `AbortSignal.timeout` (v337). A refusal keeps the service’s own words even though the body carries no `ok` field, whether or not it says `ok: false` (v313), and it carries no plan — a refused offer is not a plan that was made. A success carries the FIRST plan of the answer’s list, and an ok answer with no plans leaves the field absent rather than inventing one. An answer that is not JSON is a refusal naming the HTTP status, an offer that never arrived answers UNREACHABLE with the address named, and a slow one says it was LATE — the v337 distinction, pinned again on this door.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/planServer.test.ts` → **20/20**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the SERVICE records of an offered plan, and whether it should accept a claim whose fields are null rather than absent, are the nexus-api suite’s subject; this file grades what the Studio sends and what it makes of the answer. Whether the offer and the subscription writes SHOULD share one answer reader is a decision about one wire across two writers, and it is pinned here as MEASURED rather than argued. And the repricing write (`repricePlan`) beside it carries the same shape — the same null-filled claim and the same status-named refusal — and is left for the next cycle to ask directly, rather than assumed here.

## the service-side plans door’s REPRICING write, graded directly (M7/v448 — ADR 0016 amendment 441)

**The gap.** `repricePlan` sat beside `offerOnService` in the same file with no direct cases of its own, reached only through the Studio’s own route. The contrast between the two doors is the reason this is its own increment: the offer POSTs a JSON body with the claim NULL-FILLED (v447), while the repricing PUTs with the price and the period in the QUERY and carries ONLY the claimer — the handle, the assurance and the claimedAt never leave the browser. Measured on HEAD, both halves of that contrast were reachable only by reading two functions side by side.
**The proof.** Twelve new cases in the same file (20 → 32) ask them directly. The repricing is a PUT to the plan’s own door with NO body and an accept header only — the one server-side write in this family that sends no JSON at all. The plan id and the claimer are URL-ENCODED into the query, so a name with a space or a slash cannot forge a second door. Only the claimer travels: the handle, the assurance and the claimedAt stay behind, the divergence from the offer’s null-filled claim pinned rather than averaged away. The ask carries the SERVICE budget of 15 seconds (v337), pinned on `AbortSignal.timeout`. A refusal keeps the service’s own words (v313), whether or not the body says `ok: false`, and the status fallback names the door it asked. A success carries the NEW price and period the service answered with, and a price that is not a number leaves the field absent rather than becoming zero. A non-JSON answer is a refusal naming the HTTP status in the door’s own words, a repricing that never arrived answers UNREACHABLE with the address named, and a slow one says it was LATE.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/planServer.test.ts` → **32/32**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the SERVICE records of a repricing — whether the creator’s own claim is enough, and what happens to the subscriptions already made at the old price — is the nexus-api suite’s subject; this file grades what the Studio sends and what it makes of the answer. Whether the two writes SHOULD carry the claim in two different shapes is a decision about one wire across two writers, and it is pinned here as MEASURED rather than argued. And the door’s own words in the status fallback (“the service’s plans door answered…”) are pinned as measured: they name this door and not the offer’s reader, and a future cycle that unifies the two readers will have to move both.

## the service-side plans door’s SUBSCRIPTIONS READ, graded directly (M7/v449 — ADR 0016 amendment 442)

**The gap.** `readSubscriptions` had no direct cases of its own, reached only through the panel and the profile page. Three of its decisions are measured divergences from its neighbours rather than oversights to be smoothed, and all three are pinned as observed: the read carries NO signal where every write in this file carries the 15-second budget (v337) — the budget gap named a FOURTH time after v441, v442 and v445; a non-ok answer keeps the HTTP STATUS rather than the service’s own words, the exact opposite of the writes’ v313 rule; and a wire with no count falls back to the WINDOW’S LENGTH, where `readPlans` (v372) falls back to null. Measured on HEAD, all three were reachable only by reading this function beside its neighbours.
**The proof.** Nine new cases in the same file (32 → 41) ask them directly. The read asks by subscriber, encoded, as a plain GET with an accept header. The missing signal is pinned as MEASURED: a test that asserted a budget nobody wrote would be the lie this house refuses. A non-ok answer names the status and NOT the body’s message — the v313 shape is the writes’ alone. A 200 whose body is not JSON is a refusal in this door’s own words, the JSON branch being reachable only when the status says ok. An answer with no subscriptions ARRAY is a refusal, because a failed read is NOT an empty list. A success carries the subscriptions as they came; a wire with no `totalCents` answers zero, and a wire with no count answers the WINDOW’S LENGTH, pinned against `readPlans`’ null. And a read that never arrived answers UNREACHABLE with the address named, while a slow one says it was LATE.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/planServer.test.ts` → **41/41**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the SERVICE records of a subscription, and which window a subscriber is entitled to see, are the nexus-api suite’s subject; this file grades what the Studio asks and what it makes of the answer. Whether the reads SHOULD carry the budget the writes carry is a decision about one wire across many callers — pinned here as MEASURED for the fourth time, and left to a cycle that can change the source honestly rather than assert a budget that does not exist. And the two count fallbacks are pinned as measured: whether the subscription door’s length-stand-in and the plans door’s null SHOULD agree is a decision about two answers one panel reads, and this file records what each does now.

## the service-side plans door’s two TAKE-BACKS, graded directly (M7/v450 — ADR 0016 amendment 443)

**The gap.** `cancelSubscription` and `retirePlan` were the last two write doors in this file reached only through the Studio’s own route. They share one shape worth pinning: a DELETE naming the thing and the claimer in the QUERY, no body, the service’s 15-second budget, and an answer whose VERB field (`ended` / `retired`) must be PRESENT for the write to count as done. A 200 carrying no verb is a refusal, and a verb that is false is a SUCCESS — already ended, already retired — which is the decision a reader of the panel would otherwise have to take on faith. Measured on HEAD, both halves of that were reachable only by reading the code.
**The proof.** Twelve new cases in the same file (41 → 53) ask them directly. Each is a DELETE to the thing’s own door with the claimer encoded and only an accept header — no body, the one shape the offer’s POST JSON does not share. Each carries the SERVICE budget of 15 seconds, pinned on `AbortSignal.timeout` (v337). Each refusal keeps the service’s own words even though the body carries no `ok` field (v313). A 200 with no verb is a refusal naming the status: the answer must say whether the write moved anything. And an already-ended subscription and an already-retired plan answer ok with the verb FALSE — a take-back of a thing already taken back is a fact, not a failure. A write that never arrived answers UNREACHABLE with the address named, and a slow one says it was LATE.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/planServer.test.ts` → **53/53**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the SERVICE does with a retirement — that the subscriptions already made keep standing — is the nexus-api suite’s subject; this file grades what the Studio sends and what it makes of the answer. Whether the two take-backs SHOULD share the write doors’ answer reader or keep their own verb-checked one is a decision about one wire across five writers, and it is pinned here as MEASURED rather than argued. And every export of this file now has direct cases: the plans read (v396), the subscriptions read (v449), the subscribe write (v313), the offer (v447), the repricing (v448) and these two take-backs — the door is covered as a family, and the next cycle’s candidates lie elsewhere.

## the orbit’s INPUT SCALES, graded directly (M7/v451 — ADR 0016 amendment 444)

**The gap.** The component (`Scene3D.tsx`) is the only consumer of `ORBIT_RADIANS_PER_PIXEL`, `ORBIT_ZOOM_PER_NOTCH`, `ORBIT_PAN_PER_PIXEL`, `ORBIT_PAN_PER_KEY` and `SCENE_DOCUMENT_DEFAULT_AT`, and it is not a file this suite can run — the suite runs in Node with no DOM and no pointer events. What CAN be asked directly is the scale each constant declares and the arithmetic the component performs with it, fed through the pure orbit functions the suite already believes in. Measured on HEAD, a control whose scale nobody had stated was a control that lied by omission: the five numbers were reachable only by reading the component.
**The proof.** Five new cases in the same file (142 → 147) state the scales as countable facts. A hundred dragged pixels is ONE radian, both axes sharing the one scale, so a diagonal drag turns the camera diagonally instead of skewing it. A wheel notch is a TENTH of the distance the camera stands at — the zoom is multiplicative, so a notch near and a notch far move by different amounts and the same proportion, and the range still binds at the nearest wall. One key press is worth TWENTY-FIVE dragged pixels — a tap and a drag that agree are one control, not two that happen to move the same target. The pan box is FORTY-EIGHT key presses wide: the scale is stated against its own limit, and the number a person can count is the number the constants agree on. And a scene frame lands at `SCENE_DOCUMENT_DEFAULT_AT` when the caller names no place, with a named place winning over the default — the default is a fallback, not a floor.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/scene3d.test.ts` → **147/147**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the component does with a pointer event — the handlers, the listeners, the canvas the pixels arrive on — is the component’s subject, and this suite cannot run it; what is graded here is the SCALE the handlers apply, expressed as the pure arithmetic they hand to `applyOrbit` and `applyPan`. Whether the scales are the RIGHT ones — whether a notch should be a tenth and a press twenty-five pixels — is a decision about feel, and this file records what they are now rather than arguing what they should be. And the two sign conventions the component uses (`-dx`, `-dy`) are pinned through their effect on the pure functions: the drag direction a person expects is the component’s to keep, and the arithmetic it feeds is what is graded.

## the attach defaults and the save header’s two words, graded directly (M7/v452 — ADR 0016 amendment 445)

**The gap.** `DEFAULT_NPC`, `DEFAULT_PLAYER` and `DEFAULT_COMBATANT` are the only answer to “what does a record look like when the caller names nothing”, and `SAVE_FILE_FORMAT` / `SAVE_FILE_VERSION` are the two words a save file is stamped with. None of the five had a direct case: the attach doors were graded for what they REFUSE (`combatRecord.test.ts`) and the save doors for what they round-trip (`saveSnapshot.test.ts`), while the numbers a record is BORN with were reachable only by reading the constants. Measured on HEAD, a suite that pinned every refusal and every round-trip had never once asked what a fresh record holds.
**The proof.** Eight new cases in a new file ask them through the real attach and save paths. An NPC record starts from the declared words — archetype `villager`, the personality bag with ONE trait deliberately not the middle of the road (aggression 0.2, the rest 0.5), `wanderRadius` 5, faction `neutral` — and takes its NAME from the entity, a reading of the world rather than a constant pretending to be one. A named trait overrides ONE field and leaves the rest: the spread order is the whole rule, and a caller who names aggression does not silently reset curiosity. The player starts at full health, HALF mana, level one and no experience, with `inventory` and `skills` EMPTY — a record that claimed items from a constant would be the kind of save this lane has spent eleven grants refusing. A combatant starts SMALL on purpose: health 30 against attackPower 5 is six blows, and armor zero so the floor in `strike` is the first thing a caller meets. The declared-but-unread `attackSpeed` is NAMED by `combatFieldAudit` rather than carried in silence. And the save file names the runtime that wrote it and the version it was written at, with a foreign format and a future version both refused BY NAME.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root packages/three-runtime --no-file-parallelism test/attachDefaults.test.ts` → **8/8**; `npx tsc -p tsconfig.test.json --noEmit` (packages/three-runtime) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether these defaults are the RIGHT ones — whether a villager should be calm and a hero’s mana half their health — is a decision about feel, and this file records what they are now rather than arguing what they should be. What the SERVICE or a host does with a record is not this package’s subject; what is graded is what the runtime hands a caller who named nothing. And the save header’s refusal is pinned through `loadSaveFile`’s own words: the reader that refuses a future version is `saveSnapshot.test.ts`’s subject in full, and this file pins the two WORDS it refuses with.

## the DOM driver seam, graded directly (M7/v453 — ADR 0016 amendment 446)

**The gap.** `createDomDriver` was the one export of `uiRun.ts` no case named: the suite drives the runtime through a hand-written `FakeDriver`, so what the REAL driver does with a page was reachable only by reading it. The suite runs in Node with no DOM, which is why nothing had asked: the driver reaches for `document`, `HTMLButtonElement`, `HTMLInputElement` and `Event`, and a test that faked them badly would have graded the fake. Measured on HEAD, every one of the driver’s own decisions — which elements it can see, what a label is, what a click answers, how a type reaches a framework listener — was unasked.
**The proof.** Five new cases in the same file (12 → 17) install the smallest HONEST fakes and ask directly. A tree walker honours tag-QUALIFIED compound selectors, elements know their own tag and classes, and an input’s value accessor lives on the prototype the way a browser’s does — a canned answer per selector string would pass even if the driver asked for the wrong one, so the matcher is real. `elements()` lists the `.btn` buttons and `.field` inputs and NOTHING else: a div wearing the class is not a button, and nesting and document order hold. A button’s label is its trimmed text; an input’s is its placeholder cut at 28 characters. `click()` clicks the element and answers with its own words, refusing an input and an out-of-range index BY NAME. And `type()` writes through the NATIVE setter and fires a bubbling input event — an own-property shadow on the instance, the situation a framework leaves behind, does not block the write, which is the whole reason the trick exists. The driver reads the document at CALL time, not at creation.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/uiRun.test.ts` → **17/17**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What a REAL browser does with a detached anchor, a synthetic event or a prototype setter is the browser’s subject; what is graded here is what this code asks of the DOM, against fakes honest enough that a wrong ask would fail. Whether the driver SHOULD see `div.btn` — an element that looks like a button to a screen reader — is a decision about the markup contract, and the tag-qualified selector is pinned as MEASURED rather than argued. And the 28-character cut is pinned as measured: real placeholders carry hints beyond the key text, and where the cut lands is a decision about the label a human reads, not a fact this file can settle.

## the shared CARD GRID, graded directly (M7/v454 — ADR 0016 amendment 447)

**The gap.** `renderExploreCards` was exported at v270 so the profile page draws the same cards the gallery does, but it was reached only through `renderExploreHtml` — what one card IS was never asked directly. Two pages that draw the same card must draw it with the same code or they drift, and a drift in the card is invisible from the gallery: the grid’s own decisions — the hook a reader finds a card by, what a missing thumbnail says, whether the byline is a name or an address — were reachable only by reading the template. Measured on HEAD, every one of them was unasked.
**The proof.** Eight new cases in the same file (25 → 33) ask them directly. One `li.card` per item, each carrying `data-explore-card` — the hook a reader finds a card by. A card with a thumbnail draws the `img` and keeps the absence note OFF the card; a card with no thumbnail SHOWS the note, falling back to the declared words when none was given, because a blank card lies. The byline is a LINK to the author’s profile reading `by <handle> · <assurance>` — a name becomes an address — and an owner the service did not record means NO byline at all rather than `by undefined`. A remixed card names its source with an encoded link; a non-remix carries no such line. The two actions are open and remix, both addressed by encoded project id. Caller-controlled text is ESCAPED on its way into the card, the one rule every server-rendered page in this app shares. And an empty gallery still renders the grid — an empty list is not a missing page.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/explore.test.ts` → **33/33**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether the PROFILE page actually renders through this grid, and whether the service’s rows arrive in the shape the cards expect, are the route and nexus-api suites’ subjects; this file grades the grid’s own output for the items it is handed. Whether the byline SHOULD be a link at all — and to which route — is a decision about the ownership story’s surface, and the link shape is pinned as MEASURED rather than argued. And the escaping is pinned through the shared escaper’s exact output: what a browser does with an escaped entity is the browser’s subject, and what this code emits is what is graded.

## the build arc’s WALK plus its display answer, graded directly (M7/v455 — ADR 0016 amendment 448)

**The gap.** v177 split the walk’s `outcome` from the `answer` the route renders, and until now only the answer half was graded — through `runStudioBuildArc`, which discards the outcome. What the deploy face RECEIVES, and how the answer is projected out of the outcome, was reachable only by reading the mapping. Measured on HEAD, the two halves had never been asked together: a projection that dropped a receipt or invented a stoppedAt would have passed every case in the file.
**The proof.** Six new cases in the same file (8 → 14) ask the pair directly. An unplanned goal walks NOTHING: the outcome half is `null` and the answer says so, with no planner provenance, no steps and no receipts — the deploy face is handed nothing to deploy. A walked arc hands back the outcome beside the answer, and the answer is its projection: one step per result, one receipt per decision, in the same order. The `stepId` is the callId MINUS the arc prefix — the prefix belongs to the answer, not the step. A step is green only when the TOOL and the VERDICT agree: a container that exits 3 is a step whose tool ANSWERED (the outcome’s own result says ok) carrying a verdict that says red — two different questions, and the answer step is the AND of them. A completed walk’s answer carries no `stoppedAt` and no `reason` as ABSENT keys rather than undefined ones — the conditional spread, pinned. And a blank goal falls back to the face’s default while a named one rides along on both halves.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/buildServer.test.ts` → **14/14**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the CONTAINER HOST does with argv, and what the verifier decides about an exit code, are the exec-host suite’s subjects; this file grades what the Studio’s own door asks and what it makes of the answer. Whether the deploy face SHOULD receive the raw outcome at all — or only the display answer — is a decision about the seam between the two arcs, and it is pinned here as MEASURED rather than argued. And the planner’s choice of templates for a goal is the agent-runtime suite’s subject; this file grades only that the Studio forwards the goal and shows what was picked.

## the production DEPLOY FS seam, graded directly (M7/v456 — ADR 0016 amendment 449)

**The gap.** Every other case in the deploy file injects an in-memory fake fs. The DEFAULT is `nodeDeployFs`, and its two decisions had no direct case: it makes the directory before it appends, and its reads are node’s own. A fake cannot answer for `mkdir` — the in-memory fake in this very file would have passed a version of the seam that threw ENOENT on a fresh checkout. Measured on HEAD, the one fs the dock ACTUALLY runs on was the only fs no case had opened.
**The proof.** Four new cases in the same file (17 → 21) run against a REAL temp directory. `appendFileSync` CREATES the directory — a records path is allowed to be the FIRST thing in it, so a deploy into a fresh checkout records without a mkdir step anywhere else. `appendFileSync` APPENDS rather than rewrites: the records file is append-only, and two calls concatenate. `readFileSync` hands back the bytes as written, JSONL surviving whole. And a missing file throws ENOENT — node’s own answer, not a fake’s, which is what tells a cold read from an empty one.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/deployServer.test.ts` → **21/21**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What node’s fs does with a directory it is asked to create recursively, and what error a missing file raises, are node’s subjects; this file grades that the seam asks for exactly those two things and nothing more. Where the records file SHOULD live, and whether a deploy into a read-only checkout should record at all, are decisions about the dock’s deployment surface; the seam’s own two moves are pinned as measured, not argued. And the temp directory is created and removed by the cases themselves: nothing here writes inside the repository, which is the same rule the suite’s other file-touching cases follow.

## the six dock panels’ STYLE blocks, graded as a family (M7/v457 — ADR 0016 amendment 450)

**The gap.** Each of the six dock panels ships its own `<style>` constant, and until now no case had asked the FAMILY invariant: each block is one well-formed `<style>` element, every declared class is namespaced under the panel’s own stem, the six declared sets are pairwise disjoint (no shared stylesheet means the namespaces are the only wall between two panels on one profile page), and — the other half of the contract — each renderer emits ONLY classes its own block declares. Measured on HEAD, the last invariant caught three real drifts the moment it was asked: `renderPlanBar` emitted `.nexus-plan-terms` and `.nexus-plan-opened` with no rules behind them (the terms text rendered unstyled), and `followBar`/`tipBar` emitted per-row timestamps `.nexus-follower-when`/`.nexus-tip-when` their stylesheets never declared.
**The proof.** Four new cases in a new file ask the family directly. Each block is exactly one `<style>…</style>` pair. Every declared class starts with the panel’s stem. The six declared sets share no class. And every class a renderer puts on an element is declared by that renderer’s own block — which is what forced the repair: the four missing rules now ship beside the HTML that emits them, and the followBar fixture records the panel’s true stem `nexus-follower`, which covers both the plural chrome classes and the singular per-row timestamp.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/barStyles.test.ts` → **4/4**; `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/planBar.test.ts src/lib/followBar.test.ts src/lib/tipBar.test.ts` → **87/87**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What a browser does with an undeclared class (nothing) and with a colliding one is the browser’s subject; this file grades what this code emits against what it declares. Whether the panels SHOULD share one stylesheet instead of six is a decision about the dock’s packaging, and the six-block shape is pinned as measured, not argued. And the visual weight of each repaired rule (muted grey, small left margin, matching its neighbours) is a design decision; what is pinned is that the class is declared at all.

## the report bar’s REASON vocabulary and its style block, graded directly (M7/v458 — ADR 0016 amendment 451)

**The gap.** `REPORT_REASONS` is the closed set the report composer’s dropdown is built from — the client’s own four, and no others — and the list itself had never been asked: what a reader can report is a decision, and it lived in a constant only the component read. `REPORT_BAR_STYLES` was the seventh panel’s stylesheet and the family guard of v457 had never met it. Measured on HEAD, both exports were reachable only by importing them.
**The proof.** Eight new cases in the same file (4 → 12) ask both directly. The vocabulary is the four words in order. No reason appears twice — a dropdown would offer the same complaint twice. Every reason in the vocabulary carries a reader’s word, and `other` is the one that is not its own. The four reader words are all different — a reader can tell the complaints apart. And the seventh panel’s stylesheet is graded by the family’s own rules: one well-formed `<style>` pair, every declared class namespaced under `nexus-report`, no class colliding with any of the six blocks already graded, and `renderReportBar` emitting only classes its own block declares — a withdrawn report with a filed instant beside a fresh one exercises the per-row classes, which is where the drift would live.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/barStyles.test.ts` → **12/12**; `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/reportBar.test.ts` → **22/22**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether the composer actually offers these four — and in this order — is the component’s subject; this file grades the vocabulary the component reads. Whether `other` SHOULD be spelled “something else” rather than echoed is a decision about the reader’s words, and the mapping is pinned as measured, not argued. And what the service does with a reason outside this set is the service’s subject; the bar’s half — an unknown reason passes through rather than being relabelled — was already graded at v296.

## the TERMS NOTES, the pair that tells two moments apart, graded directly (M7/v459 — ADR 0016 amendment 452)

**The gap.** A creator may reprice a plan after a supporter agreed to it (v317), so “what was promised” and “what is on sale” are two different facts — and `PLAN_TERMS_NOTE`/`SUBSCRIPTION_TERMS_NOTE` are the only words that say which is which. Exported since v371 and graded only through the rows that carry them, the constants themselves had never been asked: an edit that made the two notes identical would have passed every case in the file. Measured on HEAD, the pair’s whole reason for existing — that the two words DIFFER — was unasked.
**The proof.** Four new cases in the same file (76 → 80) ask the pair directly. The two notes are different words — a reader must be able to tell what was promised from what is on sale. Neither note is blank — a note that says nothing is worse than no note. A subscription says “as agreed” WHATEVER its standing: active, expired and cancelled each arrive through the wire’s own two facts (`active`, `expiresAt`), and all three carry the agreed terms, because the note is about the moment of the promise, not the current state. And the profile bar and the economy row say the SAME words for the same fact — one constant feeds both surfaces, pinned by asking both row builders with the same live and retired plans.
Measured: `npx vitest run --root apps/studio --no-file-parallelism src/lib/economy.test.ts` → **80/80**; `npx tsc --noEmit` (apps/studio) → exit 0.
**Bounds (§60).** What the service does with a plan whose price moved after a subscription was taken is the service’s subject; this file grades the two words the client shows for the two moments. Whether “as agreed” and “on offer now” are the RIGHT words is a decision about the reader’s vocabulary (v374 rewrote them once already), and the wording is pinned as measured, not argued. And whether a cancelled subscription SHOULD still say “as agreed” is the same decision, pinned the same way: the promise was made, and the note says so.

## the durability CONSTANTS, graded directly (M7/v460 — ADR 0016 amendment 453)

**The gap.** `MEMORY_SCHEMA_VERSION` is the one number both halves of the durability file agree on: the writer stamps it and the reader refuses anything else. The round-trip cases only ever proved the two halves agree with EACH OTHER — a bump that moved one half and not the other would still round-trip, and the next schema would arrive silently. `DISK_TOOL_SPECS` is the declared surface an agent is shown when it asks what disk tools exist, and it was reachable only through the behaviour the loop over it produces. Measured on HEAD, both constants were imported and never asked.
**The proof.** Six new cases in the same file (8 → 14) ask them directly. The writer stamps the CURRENT version and the reader accepts exactly that one — a future version is refused with its number in the reason, and the present one loads. The version is a positive integer — a version that is not a count is not a version. The specs are the three allowlisted disk tools, in order. Only `disk.write` carries the write permission — the read paths cannot mutate the disk. Every spec declares its args honestly: the required ones named, the optional `prefix` not claimed as required, and every description non-empty. And `registerDiskTools` registers exactly the specs in the list — the registry’s own listing is the proof, and a name outside the list is absent.
Measured: `npx vitest run test/durability.test.ts` (agents/runtime) → **14/14**.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether version 1 is the RIGHT schema — and what a v2 file would carry — is a decision about the persistence format’s future; this file grades that writer and reader share ONE constant, so the bump moves both or neither. Whether the disk tools SHOULD be these three, and whether `disk.list`’s prefix SHOULD be optional, are decisions about the allowlist’s surface, pinned as measured, not argued. And what a host does with a spec’s permissions at invoke time is the registry’s subject; this file grades the declaration and that registration follows it exactly.

## the vector store’s schema VERSION, graded directly (M7/v461 — ADR 0016 amendment 454)

**The gap.** `VECTOR_SCHEMA_VERSION` is the number the builder stamps and the reader refuses to move past, and the rejection matrix only ever proved the reader refuses a HARDCODED 9 — nothing asked that the writer stamps THIS constant, or that a bump of the constant moves both halves together. Measured on HEAD, the constant was exported, imported by its own module, and asked by no case.
**The proof.** Four new cases in the same file (4 → 8) ask it directly. The builder stamps the CURRENT version on every db it builds, the empty one included. The reader accepts exactly that version, and a future one is refused with its number in the reason. The version is a positive integer — a version that is not a count is not a version. And one constant feeds both halves: the stamped db round-trips at the stamped version, so writer and reader cannot disagree about what “current” means.
Measured: `npx vitest run test/vectordb.test.ts` (agents/runtime) → **8/8**.
**Bounds (§60).** What a future schema would carry, and whether v1’s shape is the right one, are decisions about the store’s evolution; this file grades that the two halves share ONE constant. What the `dim` check adds to the version check — a same-version, wrong-dimension file — is the reader’s own second gate and was already graded in the rejection matrix; this file grades the version half. And whether the persisted store SHOULD be read across processes at all is the checkpoint gate’s subject (§28/ADR 0013); this file grades the seam’s honesty about its own version.

## the index snapshot’s schema VERSION, graded directly (M7/v462 — ADR 0016 amendment 455)

**The gap.** `INDEX_SCHEMA_VERSION` is stamped by the writer and enforced by the reader, and the rejection case only ever proved the reader refuses a HARDCODED 7 — nothing asked that the writer stamps THIS constant, or that one bump moves both halves together. Measured on HEAD, the constant was exported, used by its own module’s two halves, and asked by no case.
**The proof.** Four new cases in the same file (13 → 17) ask it directly. The writer stamps the CURRENT version on every snapshot it writes, the empty one included. The reader accepts exactly that version, and a future one is refused with its number in the reason. The version is a positive integer — a version that is not a count is not a version. And one constant feeds both halves: the stamped snapshot round-trips at the stamped version, so a bump cannot move the writer without the reader.
Measured: `npx vitest run test/indexStore.test.ts` (agents/runtime) → **17/17**.
**Bounds (§60).** What a v2 snapshot would carry — and whether the fingerprint shape is the right one — are decisions about the index’s evolution; this file grades that writer and reader share ONE constant. Whether the index SHOULD be persisted at all, and where, are the provider and checkpoint-gate subjects; this file grades the seam’s honesty about its own version. And the chunk-schema rejection beside it was already graded by the rejection case; this file grades the version half.

## the memory TOOL SPECS, graded directly (M7/v463 — ADR 0016 amendment 456)

**The gap.** `MEMORY_TOOL_SPECS` is the declared surface an agent is shown when it asks what memory tools exist. Every existing case reaches the tools through `registerMemoryTools` + `invoke`, so the behaviour is graded — but the DECLARATION was not: which tools exist, which of them may write, and what each one asks for were reachable only by reading the loop. Measured on HEAD, the constant was imported and never asked.
**The proof.** Four new cases in the same file (10 → 14) ask the declaration directly. The specs are the four memory tools, in order, under one `memory.` namespace. `save` and `forget` carry the write permission while `recall` and `list` are read-only — a memory surface where a read could mutate would be a different tool. Every spec declares its args honestly: save’s four required, recall’s optional `k` not claimed as required, list’s empty, forget’s required id. And `registerMemoryTools` registers exactly the specs in the list — the registry’s own listing is the proof, and a name outside the list is absent.
Measured: `npx vitest run test/memory.test.ts` (agents/runtime) → **14/14**.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether these four are the RIGHT tools — and whether `memory.list` SHOULD take no args — are decisions about the memory surface, pinned as measured, not argued. What the registry does with a spec’s permissions at invoke time (the checkpoint gate, the refusal wording) is the registry suite’s subject; this file grades the declaration and that registration follows it exactly. And what `store.save` does with a malformed ref is the store’s own subject, already graded above.

## the DEFAULT SKIP LIST, graded directly (M7/v464 — ADR 0016 amendment 457)

**The gap.** `DEFAULT_SKIP_DIRS` is the answer to “what does a real repository grow that is not source”, and the filtering case above only proved ONE of its entries works — `node_modules`, because the fixture happened to carry it. The list itself had never been asked: its exact membership, the shape of its entries, and the fact that an explicit option REPLACES it rather than extending it. Measured on HEAD, the constant was exported, defaulted into every walk, and named by no case.
**The proof.** Four new cases in the same file (5 → 9) ask it directly. The list is exactly the six non-source names — a walk that stopped skipping `dist` would index build output, and one that skipped `src` would index nothing. Every entry is a bare directory NAME: the skip matches `ent.name`, so a path-like entry could never match anything. A no-options walk keeps all six out while the source stays in — the default is what the caller gets when they say nothing. And an explicit `skipDirs` REPLACES the default rather than extending it: an empty set skips nothing at all, which is the difference between a default and a floor.
Measured: `npx vitest run test/realfs.test.ts` (agents/runtime) → **9/9**.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether these six are the RIGHT names — and whether a repository’s build output SHOULD be indexed — are decisions about ingestion policy, pinned as measured, not argued. The `.git`-carrying-subdirectory rule beside it is a separate, measured decision (amendment 149) and is not re-graded here. And what a walk does with a directory it cannot read is the walker’s own subject, already graded above.

## the workspace TOOL SPECS, graded directly (M7/v465 — ADR 0016 amendment 458)

**The gap.** `WORKSPACE_TOOL_SPECS` is the declared surface an agent is shown when it asks what it can do to a workspace. The wiring case reaches the tools through the registry, so the behaviour is graded — but the DECLARATION was not: which tools exist, which may write, and what each one asks for were reachable only by reading the loop. Measured on HEAD, the constant was exported and asked by no case.
**The proof.** Four new cases in the same file (4 → 8) ask the declaration directly. The specs are the six workspace tools, in order, under one `fs.` namespace. Only `fs.write` and `fs.append` carry the write permission — the four read tools cannot mutate the workspace, which is the wall between reading a repo and changing it. Every spec declares its args honestly: `fs.list`’s prefix optional, `fs.index`’s optional `topK` not claimed as required, the rest required. And registration registers exactly the specs in the list — the registry’s own listing is the proof, and a name outside the list is absent.
Measured: `npx vitest run test/workspace.test.ts` (agents/runtime) → **8/8**.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether these six are the RIGHT tools — and whether `fs.append` SHOULD exist beside `fs.write` — are decisions about the workspace surface, pinned as measured, not argued. What the registry does with a spec’s permissions at invoke time is the registry suite’s subject; this file grades the declaration and that registration follows it exactly. And what `fs.index` answers — the engine label, the provider wiring — is the index and vectordb suites’ subjects; this file grades only that the tool declares a required `query` and an optional `topK`.

## the test TOOL SPECS, graded directly (M7/v466 — ADR 0016 amendment 459)

**The gap.** `TEST_TOOL_SPECS` is the declared surface behind the orchestrator’s verification step. Every existing case reaches the tool through `registerTestTools` + `invoke`, so the behaviour is graded — but the DECLARATION was not: that there is exactly ONE tool, that it is gated like an exec, and that a bare call with no args is a full run. Measured on HEAD, the constant was exported and asked by no case.
**The proof.** Five new cases in the same file (18 → 23) ask the declaration directly. The list is exactly one spec, named `test.run`. It carries the EXEC permission — a run is gated like a subprocess even though it spawns none, which is the conservative choice the suite below then verifies at invoke time. Its only arg is the optional `only` filter, so a bare call with no args is a full run. The description keeps its two promises verbatim: in-process predicates, structured engine-labelled output. And registration registers exactly that spec — the registry’s own listing is the proof.
Measured: `npx vitest run test/testRunner.test.ts` (agents/runtime) → **23/23**.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether the verification tool SHOULD be exec-gated rather than write-gated is a decision about how dangerous a test run is, pinned as measured, not argued. What the runner does with an `only` filter that matches nothing — the vacuous-pass refusal — is the engine’s own subject and already graded above. And whether the report’s shape is the right one for the journal is the journal suite’s subject; this file grades the declaration and that registration follows it exactly.

## the simulate door’s CEILING, graded directly (M7/v467 — ADR 0016 amendment 460)

**The gap.** `WORLD_SIMULATE_MAX_STEPS` is the one number that keeps one call from moving the world forever. The existing cases ask what happens to steps that are not positive whole numbers; the ceiling itself — the boundary at the cap, and one step past it naming the cap and the remedy — had never been asked. Measured on HEAD, the constant was exported, enforced at the door, and named by no case.
**The proof.** Four new cases in the same file (27 → 31) ask it directly. The cap is a positive whole number — a ceiling that is not a count is not a ceiling. A call at exactly the cap moves the world: the boundary is INCLUSIVE, so the cap is the largest legal step and not a trap one below it. One step past the cap is refused, naming the cap and the remedy (“call it again for more”), and the world does NOT move — a refusal that still simulated would be a lie with a helpful tone. And the refusal is the same rule at any distance past the cap, not a range check — one tick over and a billion ticks over are the same answer.
Measured: `npx vitest run test/worldTools.test.ts` (agents/runtime) → **31/31**.
**Bounds (§60).** Whether 1000 is the RIGHT ceiling — and whether a long simulation SHOULD be reachable by repeated calls rather than one big one — are decisions about the world lane’s budget, pinned as measured, not argued. What the world engine does with the steps it is handed is the engine’s subject; this file grades the door’s arithmetic. And the write-class gate beside the ceiling is a separate rule, already graded; these cases all invoke behind an approving checkpoint so the ceiling is what refuses.

## the REAL spawn adapter, graded directly (M7/v468 — ADR 0016 amendment 461)

**The gap.** Every other case in the exec-host package injects a fake spawn — that file’s own row says “zero real processes in vitest” — and the DEFAULT is `NODE_SPAWN`, the one adapter every host actually runs on. It had no case of its own: what it does with argv, what it surfaces (the streams, the pid, the exit), and what `kill()` costs a child were reachable only by reading it. A fake cannot answer for `shell: false`; only a real shell-less spawn can. Measured on HEAD, the adapter was exported, defaulted into every host, and asked by no case.
**The proof.** Six new cases in a new file run against REAL processes — the interpreter running the suite itself. Stdout and stderr arrive on their own streams, with exit 0 and no error.
